NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)

I. Executive Summary
On December 29, 2025, Takeda Pharmaceuticals America, Inc. (Takeda, Applicant) submitted a
new drug application (NDA) for rusfertide for the proposed indication of the treatment of adults
with polycythemia vera (PV).
In patients with PV, iron homeostasis is dysregulated and hepcidin levels are suppressed, leading
to increased iron availability and excessive erythrocytosis. Rusfertide is a hepcidin mimetic that
inhibits ferroportin, thereby reducing iron availability for erythropoiesis and controlling
excessive production of red blood cells.
Rusfertide was granted Orphan Drug Designation (ODD) on June 12, 2020, for the treatment of
PV, Fast Track Designation on November 30, 2020, for the treatment of PV, and Breakthrough
Therapy Designation on August 21, 2025, for the treatment of erythrocytosis in patients with PV.
The Applicant submitted results from one phase 3 study (PTG-300-11, VERIFY) to demonstrate
effectiveness in adults with PV who had inadequate hematocrit (HCT) control, requiring frequent
phlebotomies (PHLs). PTG-300-11 included a 32-week randomized, double-blind, placebocontrolled treatment period (Part 1a), during which subjects were randomized 1:1 to receive
rusfertide or placebo, each given with any PV therapy (PHL with or without cytoreductive
therapy [CRT]). The primary efficacy endpoint was the proportion of subjects achieving a
response, defined as absence of PHL eligibility, assessed between Weeks 20 and 32, where PHL
eligibility was defined as an HCT ≥45% and at least 3% (absolute) higher than the baseline HCT
or an HCT >48%.
A higher proportion of subjects in the rusfertide arm achieved a response (76.9%) compared to
the placebo arm (32.9%), with a common risk difference (RD) of 43.8% (95% confidence
interval [CI]: 33.5%, 54.2%; p<0.0001). Rusfertide also demonstrated superiority for the
secondary endpoints of mean number of PHLs from Weeks 0 to 32 and the proportion of subjects
maintaining all HCT values below 45% from Weeks 0 to 32.
These findings of efficacy are supported by data from Part 2 of Study PTG-300-04 (REVIVE), a
three-part, phase 2 study of adults with PV who required frequent PHLs to maintain an HCT
<45%. Part 2 of the study was a double-blind, withdrawal phase during which subjects who
responded during Part 1 (single arm, open-label) were randomized to receive rusfertide or
placebo. The response rate, defined as the proportion of subjects who maintained HCT control
without PHL eligibility, was 69.2% in the rusfertide arm (19 of 26 subjects) compared to 14.8%
in the placebo arm (4 of 27 subjects), resulting in an absolute difference of 54.4% (95% CI:
32.2%, 76.6%; p<0.0001).
Assessment of risk was based primarily on data from the 291 subjects (145 in the rusfertide arm
and 146 in the placebo arm) who received blinded treatment for up to 32 weeks in PTG-300-11.

3
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)

1. Overview
1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action
NDA 220605 was reviewed by the multidisciplinary review team. Each discipline recommends
approval of rusfertide for the treatment of erythrocytosis in adult patients with PV, and I, the
signatory authority for this application, concur with those recommendations. The overall benefitrisk is favorable, as described in Section 2. Rusfertide is a first-in-class hepcidin mimetic and
will be the first approved drug to target the hepcidin pathway for the treatment of erythrocytosis
in PV. For further information supporting the basis for this approval, please refer to the detailed
reviews included in this integrated review.

1.2. Conclusions on Substantial Evidence of
Effectiveness
Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) was established with one adequate and wellcontrolled clinical investigation and confirmatory evidence.
The one adequate and well-controlled study was PTG-300-11, a multicenter, randomized,
placebo-controlled study conducted in 293 adults with PV who had inadequate HCT control
requiring frequent PHLs. The results for the primary endpoint of durable HCT control, defined as
absence of PHL eligibility between Weeks 20 and 32, provided evidence of superiority of
rusfertide over placebo (76.9% versus 32.9%; common RD: 43.8%; 95% CI: 33.5%, 54.2%;
p<0.0001). These findings were supported by the first and second key secondary endpoints,
which evaluated mean number of PHLs and proportion of subjects maintaining all HCT values
<45%. For these key secondary endpoints, rusfertide also demonstrated superiority over placebo.
The Applicant also conducted Study PTG-300-04, a three-part, phase 2, placebo-controlled,
randomized withdrawal study in 70 adults with PV who required frequent PHLs, which provides
confirmatory evidence of effectiveness for rusfertide. In the blinded randomized withdrawal
portion of the study, 69.2% of subjects in the rusfertide arm achieved a response compared to
14.8% in the placebo arm (absolute difference 54.4%; 95% CI: 32.2%, 76.6%; p<0.0001),
consistent with the findings from PTG-300-11. Further confirmatory evidence is provided by the
mechanistic and pharmacodynamic (PD) data.
Together, the data from Study PTG-300-11 with confirmatory evidence from Study PTG-300-04
and mechanistic and PD data establish substantial evidence of effectiveness of rusfertide for the
treatment of erythrocytosis in adults with PV.

Conclusions and Reasons

PV is a rare chronic disease that can cause arterial and
PV is a chronic myeloproliferative neoplasm (MPN)
venous thrombosis, hemorrhage, and transformation to
characterized by the overproduction of circulating blood cells
post-PV myeolofibrosis or AML. It can be associated with
with an annual incidence of 0.5 to 4.0 cases per 100,000
significant burden of disease-specific symptoms.
persons. The median age at diagnosis is approximately
60 years.
PV is caused by activating mutations in the JAK2 gene in
hematopoietic stem cells (HSCs), most commonly the
JAK2V617F point mutation. The JAK2V617F mutation renders
HSCs and their progeny hypersensitive to erythropoietin,
thrombopoietin, and granulocyte colony-stimulating factor,
resulting in constitutive, ligand-independent activation of
downstream proliferative signaling and the simultaneous
overproduction of red blood cells, platelets, and neutrophils.
Panmyelosis is a direct consequence of the clonal expansion of
JAK2V617F-mutant HSCs and their differentiated progeny
across all three lineages and explains why patients with PV
present not only with erythrocytosis but also with thrombocytosis
and leukocytosis.
PV is characterized by erythrocytosis, thrombocytosis, and
leukocytosis.
The erythroid expansion characteristic of PV disrupts normal
iron homeostasis through suppression of hepcidin. The
markedly expanded erythroid compartment releases
erythroferrone, which suppresses hepatic hepcidin production,
increasing iron availability and further fueling red blood cell
overproduction. This feed-forward loop between erythroid
expansion, hepcidin suppression, and iron availability is a key
pathophysiologic feature of erythrocytosis in PV.

Evidence and Uncertainties
Conclusions and Reasons
• Patients with PV commonly experience a significant burden of
disease-specific symptoms and signs, including fatigue, pruritus
(particularly aquagenic pruritus), night sweats, bone pain,
fevers, unintended weight loss, and splenomegaly.
• The most serious clinical manifestations of PV include arterial
and venous thrombosis, hemorrhage, and transformation to
post-PV myelofibrosis or acute myeloid leukemia (AML).
• Thrombotic events, including stroke, myocardial infarction, deep
vein thrombosis (DVT), pulmonary embolism (PE), and
splanchnic vein thrombosis, are the leading cause of morbidity
and mortality in PV.
• Thrombotic events may occur before, at the time of, or after the
diagnosis of PV, and overall occur in 20% of individuals with PV.
• Microvascular thromboses are attributed to thrombocytosis and
hyperreactive platelets, and include erythromelalgia, transient
ischemic attack, digital ischemia, migraine, and ocular
disturbances.

Current
treatment
options

•
•
•

•
•

The goals of therapy in PV are to prevent thrombotic events,
reduce disease-specific symptoms, and prevent or delay
progression to post-PV myelofibrosis or AML.
For all patients with PV, guidelines recommend maintaining the
hematocrit (HCT) at <45% and the use of low-dose aspirin
and/or cytoreductive therapy (CRT) to prevent complications.
HCT control is achieved through periodic therapeutic
phlebotomy (PHL), which reduces the red blood cell mass and
induces iron deficiency. HCT control may also be achived
through use of CRT. The goal of maintaing the HCT at <45% is
based on studies showing reduced deaths from cardiovascular
causes or major thrombotic events when HCT was <45%.
In patients with high-risk PV (age ≥60 years or history of a prior
blood clot) CRT is used in conjunction with therapuetic PHL or
as a substitute for therapeutic PHL.
Approved CRTs are:
– Ropeginterferon alfa-2b (Besremi) indicated for the
treatment of adults with PV. Interferons carry risks of liver
enzyme elevations, flu-like symptoms, and depression.

6
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

Maintaining HCT <45% is one of the mainstays in the
treatment of PV. Not all patients are able to achieve an
HCT <45% with PHL alone, and PHL is associated with
several treament burdens. While CRT can be effective for
the treatment of erythrocytosis, there are also risks
associated with these agents. Thus, there remains an
unmet need for new therapeutic options for the treatment
of erythrocytosis in patients with PV.

Dimension

Evidence and Uncertainties
Conclusions and Reasons
– Ruxolitinib (Jakafi) approved for PV in adults with an
inadequate response to or are intolerant of hydroxurea (HU).
• CRTs used off-label include: Pegylated interferon-alfa-2a, HU,
and rarely busulfan.
• Rates of permament discontinuation of HU in studies are 17.5%
to 20% (Boccia et al. 2015; Grunwald et al. 2020).
• Therapeutic PHL is associated with the burden of recurring
clinic visits and patient discomfort. Repeated PHL can lead to
iron deficiency, which may contribute to symptoms such as
fatigue and cognitive impairment even in the absence of
anemia.

Benefit

•

•

•

Rusfertide, a first-in-class hepcidin mimetic, will be the first
Evidence of benefit is derived primarily from PTG-300-11, a

Conclusions and Reasons
Rusfertide reduced the PHL requirement across all
baseline therapy groups.
The PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a measures clinically relevant
and important concepts to patients with PV. The PROMIS
Fatigue SF-8a and the corresponding endpoint are
adequate to potentially support labeling claims and are
supported by the overall clinical study design and analysis.
The fatigue finding is included in the clinical studies
section (section 14) of the United States Prescribing
Information as a descriptive numerical result.

Conclusions and Reasons
• Confirmatory evidence comes from the phase 2 study
PTG-300-04 and mechanistic data. In PTG-300-04, a doubleblind, randomized withdrawal study, 18 of 26 subjects (69%) in

during the randomized withdrawal phase (Weeks 29-41).
Mechanistic evidence was based on pharmacodynamic markers

hepcidin mimetic. Consistent increases in ferritin (~6-10-fold)
and decreases in transferrin (~4 µmol/L to 7 µmol/L) from
baseline to Week 32 were observed across three studies
(PTG-300-11, PTG-300-04, and PTG-300-08, a phase 1 study).

Evidence and Uncertainties
Conclusions and Reasons
• A pooled safety population was also evaluated reflecting
exposure of rusfertide as weekly injection in 375 adult subjects
with PV across four clinical studies. Among 375 subjects who
received rusfertide, 89.1% were exposed for 6 months or longer
and 68.3% were exposed for greater than 1 year. The most
common adverse reactions in the pooled population (>20%)
were injection-site reactions (57%) and anemia (26%).
– Serious adverse reactions occurred in 3 (0.8%) subjects
who received rusfertide including anemia, thrombocytosis
and dyspena in 1 subject each. Dosage reductions of
rusfertide due to an adverse reaction occurred in 40 (11%)
of subjects; the adverse reactions that required dosage
reduction included anemia in 37 (10%) subjects and
thrombocytosis and dyspena in 2 (0.5%) subjects each.
– Injection-site reactions occurred in 57% and were most
commonly erythrema (34%), pruritis (21%), pain (20%)
swelling (13%) bruising (7%), induration (6%), and irritation
(6%). Two subjects experienced Grade 3 injection-site
reactions; all others were Grade 1 or Grade 2, and most did
not require medication for treatment. Four patients
experienced injection-site reactions that led to treatment
discontinuation.

Rusfertide (PTG-300)

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
PV is a chronic myeloproliferative neoplasm (MPN) characterized by clonal erythrocytosis,
leukocytosis and thrombocytosis. The goals of therapy are to maintain HCT below 45% to
reduce the risk of thromboembolic complications and cardiovascular mortality, and also to
prevent disease progression and alleviate disease-related symptoms. For some patients, PHL and
low-dose aspirin are sufficient to manage disease. For others, particularly high-risk patients,
cytoreductive therapies such as hydroxurea (HU), ruxolitinib, and interferons are required, as
these agents address not only erythrocytosis but also leukocytosis, thrombocytosis,
splenomegaly, and symptom burden. A subset of patients does not achieve or sustain adequate
HCT control with PHL alone or with available CRTs, either due to inadequate response,
intolerance, or the cumulative burden of repeated PHLs and the associated iron deficiency.
Rusfertide is a hepcidin mimetic that takes a mechanistically distinct and narrower approach to
PV management, compared to CRT. By reducing iron availability for erythropoiesis, it controls
red blood cell production and hematocrit without inducing iron deficiency. However, it does not
address the leukocytosis or thrombocytosis that may also be present in patients with PV. Patients
requiring broad myeloproliferative disease control, including management of leukocytosis,
thrombocytosis, or prevention of disease transformation, will continue to require CRT, which
may be used concomitantly with rusfertide.
Overall, the benefit-risk analysis of rusfertide for the treatment of erythrocytosis in adult patients
with PV is favorable. Rusfertide is a first-in-class hepcidin mimetic and will be the first approved
drug to target the hepcidin pathway for the treatment of erythrocytosis in PV. Rusfertide reduces
the need for PHL, provides durable HCT control, and can reduce symptoms of fatigue. The risks
associated with rusfertide based on PTG-300-11 and supported by analysis of the integrated
safety database were manageable and can be adequately described in the labeling. Given that the
safety assessment is based on limited exposure in a drug that is potentially administered
chronically for years, there is uncertainty over the long-term risks, related primarily to increases
in platelet count. These risks will be assessed in a postmarketing requirement (PMR).

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)

II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
3. Introduction
Rusfertide (proposed proprietary name MIMRYLO), a new molecular entity, is a peptide
mimetic of the endogenous hormone hepcidin, the primary regulator of iron homeostasis.
Rusfertide reduces availability of iron to the bone marrow and thereby reduces red-blood-cell
production, resulting in lower HCT levels, reduced blood viscosity, decreased need for PHL, and
improvements in iron parameters.
The proposed indication for rusfertide is the treatment of adults with PV. The approved
indication is the treatment of erythrocytosis in adults with PV.
The recommended dosage for rusfertide is 19 mg subcutaneously (SC) once a week, with dose
adjustment to a maximum daily dose of 82 mg and maximum weekly dose of 108 mg based on
efficacy and safety.
Disease Background
PV is a chronic MPN characterized by the overproduction of circulating blood cells. PV is
caused by activating mutations in the Janus-associated kinase (JAK)-2 gene in hematopoietic
stem cells (HSCs), most commonly the JAK2V617F point mutation. The JAK2V617F mutation
renders HSCs and their progeny hypersensitive to erythropoietin, thrombopoietin, and
granulocyte colony-stimulating factor, resulting in constitutive, ligand-independent activation of
downstream proliferative signaling and the simultaneous overproduction of red blood cells,
platelets, and neutrophils (Stein et al. 2015; Harrison et al. 2025). PV usually develops in late
adulthood, and its frequency increases exponentially after the age of 60 years with a slight male
predominance.
Clinical Manifestations
PV is characterized by erythrocytosis, thrombocytosis, and leukocytosis. Patients with PV
commonly experience a significant burden of disease-specific symptoms and signs, including
fatigue, pruritus (particularly aquagenic pruritus), night sweats, bone pain, fevers, unintended
weight loss, and splenomegaly. The most serious clinical manifestations of PV include arterial
and venous thrombosis, hemorrhage, and transformation to post-PV myelofibrosis or acute
myeloid leukemia (AML). Thrombotic events, including stroke, myocardial infarction (MI), deep
vein thrombosis (DVT), pulmonary embolism (PE), and splanchnic vein thrombosis, are the
leading cause of morbidity and mortality in PV. Thrombotic events may occur before, at the time
of, or after the diagnosis of PV, and overall occur in 20% of individuals with PV (Moliterno et
al. 2021). Microvascular thromboses are attributed to thrombocytosis and hyperreactive platelets,
and include erythromelalgia, transient ischemic attack (TIA), digital ischemia, migraine, and
ocular disturbances (Cortelazzo et al. 1995; Michiels et al. 2006).

12
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
Management of PV
The goals of therapy in PV are to prevent thrombotic events, reduce disease-specific symptoms,
and prevent or delay progression to post-PV myelofibrosis or AML. The management of PV is
guided by risk stratification based on age (≥60 years) and/or history of prior thrombotic events,
with the primary therapeutic goal of maintaining HCT below 45% to reduce the risk of
thromboembolic complications and cardiovascular mortality.
Low-risk patients are typically managed with therapeutic PHL and low-dose aspirin. High-risk
patients generally require the addition of CRT. HU is the most widely used first-line CRT, with
interferons (peginterferon alfa-2a and ropeginterferon alfa-2b) representing an alternative,
particularly in younger patients where long-term HU exposure is a concern. Ruxolitinib, a
JAK1/2 inhibitor, is approved for patients who are resistant or intolerant to HU.
Table 3 shows the available therapies. All available therapies have recognized limitations.
•

PHL reduces HCT by removing circulating red blood cells and iron but does not address the
underlying clonal expansion of JAK2V617F-mutant HSCs or the multilineage
overproduction characteristic of PV. PHL is associated with treatment burdens, including
clinic visits, iron deficiency, and patient discomfort. Repeated PHL can lead to iron
deficiency, which may contribute to symptoms such as fatigue and cognitive impairment
even in the absence of anemia.

•

CRTs such as HU reduce HCT by suppressing erythroid, megakaryocytic, and granulocytic
production across all three myeloid lineages. This multilineage suppression addresses not
only erythrocytosis but also leukocytosis and thrombocytosis, providing more comprehensive
hematologic control than PHL alone. However, a complete hematologic response is achieved
in only approximately 40 to 50% of HU-treated patients.

•

JAK inhibitors such as ruxolitinib reduce HCT by inhibiting JAK1/JAK2 signaling, thereby
suppressing erythroid, megakaryocytic, and granulocytic production. Ruxolitinib achieves
HCT control and achieves modest reductions in JAK2V617F variant allele fraction (VAF) in
a subset of patients, with approximately 56% of patients achieving ≥50% VAF reduction at a
median follow-up of 48 months. Prospective randomized data (Harrison et al. 2023)
demonstrate that VAF reduction is independently associated with improved event-free and
overall survival.

•

Immunotherapies such as ropeginterferon alfa-2b reduce HCT by mechanisms that include
suppression of erythroid production and, importantly, direct targeting and depletion of
JAK2V617F-positive HSCs. Ropeginterferon induces complete hematologic remissions and
sustained and progressive reductions in JAK2V617F VAF, with mean VAF declining from
37% to 8% at 60 months of treatment, and 20% of patients achieving complete molecular
remission (VAF <1%).

3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
Table 4 provides an overview of the clinical studies conducted to support the benefit-risk
assessment of rusfertide for patients with PV.
The results from subjects with PV from Study PTG-300-11 formed the primary basis for the
assessment of efficacy.
The assessment of risk was based on the analysis of PTG-300-11 (N=285), PTG-300-04 (N=70),
PTG-300-21(N=46), and PTG300-08 (N=20). Additionally, the assessment of risk was based on
data from a pooled safety set comprising all adverse events (AEs) occurring in subjects with PV
while receiving any dosage of rusfertide in a clinical study (N=375).

5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics,
and Clinical Pharmacology
5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
Erythrocytosis, a massive expansion of the red-cell mass, is the primary driver of cardiovascular
(CV) disease and thrombotic complications in PV. PV is a clonal myeloproliferative neoplasm
arising from somatic mutations where the JAK2 V617F variant is the predominant pathogenic
variant (~95% prevalence) directly activating JAK-STAT (signal transducer and activator of
transcription) signaling and rendering erythropoiesis largely independent of erythropoietin
22
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
regulation. The resultant pathologic erythrocytosis elevates HCT and increases whole-blood
viscosity, substantially compounding thromboembolic and CV mortality risk. HCT control,
achieved with therapeutic PHL, HU, or both, has been clinically shown to reduce the risk of CV
death and major thrombotic events in patients with PV (Marchioli et al. 2013). However,
repeated PHLs deplete systemic iron stores and can exacerbate iron deficiency, which may
contribute to fatigue, lethargy, impaired concentration, and other symptoms that diminish quality
of life in patients with PV (Ginzburg et al. 2018; Edahiro and Komatsu 2025).
Hepcidin, the master regulator of systemic iron homeostasis, restricts iron availability for
erythropoiesis by binding to the ferroportin iron transporter on enterocytes and macrophages,
inducing its internalization and degradation, thereby blocking iron absorption from the
gastrointestinal (GI) tract and iron release from cellular storage sites (Figure 1). Consequently,
the administration of hepcidin mimetics (or agonists) may be effective for minimizing or
eliminating the need for therapeutic PHLs in patients with PV.
Rusfertide is a first-in-class synthetic cyclic peptide engineered to mimic endogenous hepcidin,
which directly targets and binds to the ferroportin iron exporter expressed on enterocytes and
macrophages, actively driving its internalization and lysosomal degradation and thereby
restricting systemic iron availability (Figure 1). Rusfertide is being developed for once-weekly
SC injection for the treatment of adult patients with PV at a starting dose of 19 mg with a
maximum weekly dose of 108 mg.
The Applicant provided pharmacological and pharmacokinetic (PK) data to support rusfertide’s
proposed mechanism of action and dosing regimen. Mechanistically, rusfertide is expected to
restrict iron bioavailability for erythropoiesis through a JAK-STAT-independent pathway, acting
at the level of ferroportin-mediated iron egress rather than targeting the upstream oncogenic
driver. This iron-restricted suppression of erythropoiesis is anticipated to translate into sustained
HCT control and a clinically meaningful reduction in PHL frequency.

23
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
Figure 1. Distribution of Iron in the Adult Human Body and Regulation of Iron Traffic by Hepcidin

Source: Sebastiani et al. (2016).
Circulating iron is bound to transferrin (holo-Tf) and delivered to tissues (black arrows). Holo-Tf is primarily replenished by iron
recycled from tissue macrophages (thick red arrow), but also by dietary iron absorbed by duodenal enterocytes (thin red arrow).
Under conditions of iron deficiency, iron stored in hepatocytes can also be mobilized (thin red arrow). Iron efflux to the bloodstream
is inhibited by the liver-derived peptide hormone hepcidin, which binds to the iron exporter FPN and promotes its degradation.
Abbreviations: FPN, ferroportin; Holo-TF, transferrin, Fe3+; iron, RBCs, red blood cells

Rusfertide induced ferroportin internalization and degradation in cells expressing green
fluorescent protein-tagged ferroportin, with a half-maximal effective concentration (EC50) of
11.5 ng/mL (4.71nM), a ≥5-fold potency advantage over endogenous hepcidin (EC50: 28nM). To
more closely approximate physiological conditions, ferroportin internalization activity was
further evaluated in T47D human epithelial tumor cells, which endogenously express ferroportin
at the cell surface. Rusfertide and its metabolites M1, M4, and M9 induced ferroportin
internalization in the T47D system but exhibited distinct potency profiles under human serum
albumin conditions. M1 (initial proteolytic product by hydrolysis) was the most potent
metabolite, with 2.8-fold greater potency than rusfertide (half-maximal inhibitory concentration
[IC50]: 13.7nM), whereas M4 (exopeptidase removal of S13, K14, G15, and K17 of M1) and M9
(hydroxylated rusfertide at the palmitoyl fatty-acid chain) were less active, showing
24
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
approximately 35% and 83% lower potency relative to rusfertide (IC50: 58.3nM and 225nM,
respectively). M4 and M9 are major human metabolites—and represent approximately 15% and
31% of total drug-related exposure at steady state, respectively—and were adequately qualified
for safety based on dedicated toxicology studies (M4) and pivotal chronic toxicity studies in
monkeys (M9).
SC administration of rusfertide at human equivalent doses (HEDs) of 4.9 to 36.6 mg every other
day for 4 weeks reduced systemic iron mobilization in an HFE2⁻/⁻ mouse model of juvenile
hereditary hemochromatosis—characterized by hepcidin insufficiency, systemic iron overload,
and parenchymal iron deposition—across cohorts maintained on low- or high-iron diets.
Rusfertide significantly reduced serum iron, transferrin saturation (TSAT), and serum ferritin
across all dose levels, and increased total iron-binding capacity at the 36.6 mg HED. At the
highest dose, rusfertide redistributed nonheme iron from parenchymal organs, including the liver,
kidneys, and pancreas, to the spleen relative to vehicle controls, thereby mitigating systemic iron
overload.
Similarly, SC administration of rusfertide to wild-type animals yielded robust, dose-dependent
reductions in serum iron, directly correlating with systemic exposure across multiple species.
Single SC HEDs ranging from 0.12 to 4.7 mg in C57BL/6 mice produced rapid
pharmacodynamic activity, reducing serum iron by 25.6% to 93.0% within 4 hours postdose,
with maximal suppressive effects persisting for up to 30 hours relative to vehicle controls.
Rusfertide demonstrated greater relative potency and a longer duration of action than equimolar
doses of endogenous hepcidin, sustaining 35.0% to 93.0% reductions in serum iron for 24 hours,
whereas hepcidin produced only transient 26.0% to 50.0% reductions that resolved by 24 hours.
Dose-dependent serum iron reductions were similarly observed in jugular vein-cannulated
Sprague–Dawley rats administered single SC HEDs of 9.7 or 29 mg, with significant decreases
detected by 4 or 2 hours postdose, respectively, reaching a nadir at 24 hours with reductions of
74.4% and 93.9% relative to predose values, and returning to normal levels by 32 hours.
Consistent with these findings, exposure-dependent reductions in mean serum iron
concentrations of 67%, 80%, and 76% relative to predose values were achieved in healthy male
cynomolgus monkeys administered single subcutaneous HEDs of 4.7, 11.8, or 18.8 mg,
respectively. Nadir effects occurred at 8 hours postdose at the 4.7 mg HED and at 24 hours
postdose at the 11.8 and 18.8 mg HEDs. Correspondingly, in terms of the transient
pharmacodynamic profile after single-dose administration, serum iron returned to near-baseline
levels by 48 hours postdose at 4.7 mg HED and by 72 hours postdose at 11.8 and 18.8 mg HEDs.
Notably, in oral iron-challenge studies, rusfertide inhibited GI iron absorption, as shown by
reduced increases in serum iron and transferrin saturation in mice and monkeys coadministered
ferrous sulfate at 2 mg/kg.
Pharmacokinetic/toxicokinetic evaluations provided evidence that rusfertide’s plasma half-life
ranged from approximately 11 to 12 hours in monkeys to 33 to 67 hours in humans following SC
administration. Rusfertide exhibited consistent PK with minimal accumulation following onceweekly SC administration in rats and monkeys for up to 39 weeks. In repeat-dose toxicology
studies, steady-state plasma exposures were maintained throughout the dosing interval, and
sustained PD effects on serum iron and hematologic parameters supported once-weekly
administration in humans.

25
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
Overall, the Applicant’s nonclinical in vitro pharmacology studies support rusfertide’s proposed
mechanism of action as a hepcidin mimetic that restricts iron availability for erythropoiesis
through ferroportin inhibition. The in vivo studies across nonclinical species demonstrate dosedependent and sustained decreases in serum iron, inhibition of dietary iron absorption, and
therapeutic benefit in a disease model of iron overload and ineffective erythropoiesis at clinically
relevant exposures, corresponding to 4- to 10-fold the human maximum recommended human
dose (MRHD) maximum plasma concentration (Cmax) of 1,302 ng/mL.

Rusfertide is a synthetic cyclic peptide that mimics the inhibitory effect of hepcidin on ferroportin. Inhibition of

The rusfertide plasma concentration-time profiles were generally similar following injections in the abdomen and

Following SC administration of 19 mg rusfertide in healthy subjects, the mean (±SD) rusfertide plasma elimination
half-life is 28.6 (±11.3) hours.
Rusfertide is catabolized into smaller peptides via two independent pathways. One pathway involves proteolysis by
trypsin like proteases to form metabolite M1, which undergoes further proteolysis to form metabolite M4. Rusfertide
also undergoes hydroxylation to form M9. M1 is a minor metabolite (<1% of total drug-related exposure). M4 and M9
are pharmacologically active but have potencies that are approximately 65% and 14% of the potency of rusfertide,
respectively. Following multiple-dose SC administrations of rusfertide 54 mg in healthy subjects, M4 and M9 account
for approximately 15% and 31% of total drug-related exposure, respectively, at steady state.

Drug Information
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight (44.5 to 151 kg) was not identified as a statistically significant covariate on rusfertide PK in the
population PK analysis.
Age (20 to 86 years) was not identified as a statistically significant covariate on rusfertide PK in the population PK
analysis.
Following SC administration of 19 mg rusfertide, no clinically meaningful difference was observed in rusfertide
systemic exposures (AUC) between subjects with severe renal impairment (eGFR <30 mL/min) and subjects with
normal renal function (eGFR ≥90 mL/min).
Following SC administration of 19 mg rusfertide, no clinically meaningful difference was observed in rusfertide
systemic exposures (AUC) between subjects with moderate hepatic impairment (Child–Pugh B) and those with
normal hepatic function. Rusfertide has not been studied in subjects with severe hepatic impairment (Child–Pugh C).
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
Based on in vitro study results, rusfertide does not inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19,
CYP2D6, or CYP3A at clinically relevant concentrations. Rusfertide does not induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A.
Based on in vitro study results, rusfertide does not inhibit BCRP, BSEP, MATE1, MATE2K, OATP1B1, OATP1B3,
OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, or P-gp.
Immunogenicity
ADA against rusfertide were assayed using a semiquantitative bridging enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay and
NAb were detected using a cell-based flow cytometry uptake assay. Both assays were evaluated by the Office of
Product Quality Assessment III. The ADA-binding assay used for PTG-300-11 was found suitable for its intended
purpose. The NAb assay was found not suitable for intended purpose due to insufficient sensitivity; however, given
the lack of observed clinical impact of ADA, further optimization of the NAb assay was not recommended. The
incidence of NAb may be underestimated.
In the pivotal phase 3 study (Study PTG-300-11), which had a median duration of exposure of 61 weeks over a total
of 108 weeks, the overall incidence of anti-rusfertide antibodies was 34.4% (97 of 282). Of these 97 subjects, 35
(36%) developed NAb and 12 (12%) developed antibodies that showed cross-reactivity to human hepcidin.
There was no apparent correlation of anti-rusfertide antibody development with the PK, effectiveness, and safety of
rusfertide in Study PTG-300-11.

6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential
Effectiveness
Applicant’s Proposed Dosing Regimen
The proposed dosing regimen of rusfertide is a starting dose of 19 mg by SC injection once
weekly. The dose is adjusted based on efficacy or safety with a maximum daily dose of 82 mg
and a maximum total weekly dose of 108 mg.
Rusfertide is available as a lyophilized powder for SC injection in five dose strengths: 9.5 mg,
19 mg, 28 mg, 41 mg, and 54 mg. Doses exceeding 54 mg are administered as two separate SC
injections per the dosing instructions summarized in Table 7. For weekly dosages greater than
82 mg, the first injection is administered on Day 1 and the second injection on Day 4 or 5.

Adjusted Dose Strengths for Rusfertide Lyophilized Powder Formulation
The intended commercial lyophilized powder formulation (LPF) was used in the phase 3 pivotal
clinical study (PTG-300-11) and also in clinical pharmacology studies (PTG-300-15,
PTG-300-16, PTG-300-17, PTG-300-18, PTG-300-20, and PTG-300-22). Upon review of the
rusfertide manufacturing process in accordance with the Food and Drug Administration (FDA)
Manual of Policies and Procedures 5019.1 Rev. 1 (FDA 2022), it was identified that expansion
volume was not accounted for during development of the LPF, resulting in dose strengths that
deviated from the originally planned values. Following alignment with the Agency at the Type B
pre-NDA Chemistry, Manufacturing, and Controls meeting on November 24, 2025, dose
strengths for labeling were adjusted to reflect the targeted strengths used in the clinical batches
as shown in Table 10.

Grade ≥2 anemia based on Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events (CTCAE) grading is defined as
hemoglobin (Hb) <10 g/dL.

Selection of the Dosing Regimen for the Phase 3 Study
The selection of the proposed dosing regimen for the phase 3 study (PTG-300-11) was based on
the results of the phase 2 dose-finding study (PTG-300-04). Study PTG-300-04 was conducted in
adult subjects with PV requiring PHL, using a liquid formulation (LF) in a prefilled syringe
(PFS) of 10 mg, 20 mg, and 40 mg strength, administered SC. The study employed a dose
titration design, in which rusfertide was initiated at 20 mg SC once weekly and adjusted up or
down in 20 mg increments with the goal of maintaining HCT <45% without PHL. The maximum
weekly dose was 120 mg, not exceeding 80 mg per day, with flexibility to use intermediate doses
(e.g., 30 mg, 50 mg) via multiple syringes, alternating weekly doses (e.g., 40 mg alternating with
20 mg to achieve average dose of 30 mg/week), or twice-weekly dosing schedules (e.g., dosing
on Day 1 and Day 4 or 5). The majority of subjects was dosed with 40 to 60 mg per week, with
some subjects requiring higher doses of up to 120 mg per week. In the placebo-controlled
randomized period of the study, the response rate (subjects who maintained HCT control for
12 weeks and did not undergo PHL) was statistically significantly higher in rusfertide-treated
subjects (69.2%) compared to placebo-treated subjects (14.8%) (p<0.0001). HCT levels
remained below 45% for 80.8% of subjects randomized to rusfertide and 37.0% of those
randomized to placebo. In addition, the Applicant reported that rusfertide treatment was
generally well-tolerated in this study.
The phase 3 study (PTG-300-11) was conducted in adult subjects with PV using the LPF. The
recommended starting dose of rusfertide in the phase 3 study was 19 mg SC once weekly, with a
maximum daily dose of 82 mg and a maximum total weekly dose of 108 mg. Of note, the
maximum daily dose in the phase 3 study (90 mg LPF) is higher than that used in phase 2 (80 mg
LF; and based on Study PTG-300-15, the 19 mg LPF resulted in approximately 1.6-fold higher
Cmax and area under the concentration-time curve (AUC) compared to the 20 mg LF formulation.
However, the Applicant justified this by noting that the dose-titration approach based on
individual HCT response would ensure appropriate dosing.
Evaluation of the Proposed Dosing Regimen
The proposed rusfertide dosing regimen was evaluated using the intended commercial LPF
formulation in the phase 3 study PTG-300-11. The proposed dosing regimen and dose
adjustment are primarily supported by the safety and efficacy findings from Study PTG-300-11.
During Weeks 20 to 32 of this study, significantly more subjects in the rusfertide arm (76.9%)
achieved a clinical response (defined as absence of PHL eligibility) compared with the placebo
arm (32.9%) (p<0.0001).
32
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
A statistically significant relationship between rusfertide exposure and the probability of PHL
was observed, with higher systemic exposures associated with a decreased likelihood of PHL
(Figure 2). This finding supports the dose-titration approach for rusfertide.

In the exposure-safety analysis, no apparent relationship was identified between rusfertide
exposure and injection-site reactions (ISRs), anemia (based on adverse event reporting), or any
Grade ≥3 treatment-emergent adverse event (TEAE). The exposure-response relationship and the
time course of PK-PD relationship for increases in hematologic parameters, such as platelets and
leukocytes, have not been characterized.
Refer to Section 6.2 for more details regarding the clinical efficacy of rusfertide. Refer to
Section 7 for details regarding the safety of rusfertide.

6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To
Demonstrate Efficacy
6.2.1. Study PTG-300-11
6.2.1.1. Design, PTG-300-11
The design of Study PTG-300-11 is shown in Figure 3.
PTG-300-11 is a phase 3, global, multicenter, randomized, placebo-controlled, double blind,
safety and efficacy study of rusfertide in approximately 250 subjects with PV who required
33
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
frequent PHLs to control their HCT despite ongoing therapy. Subjects were randomized 1:1 to
receive rusfertide or placebo. Randomization was stratified by ongoing PV therapy (PHL only,
PHL + HU, PHL + ruxolitinib, PHL + interferon (IFN), and PHL + other). Subjects received
blinded therapy for 32 weeks, where the primary endpoint was the proportion of subjects who
achieved a response between Week 20 and Week 32.
The study comprised four sequential parts. Part 1a was a 32-week randomized double-blind
phase. Part 1b was a 20-week open-label phase during which all subjects received rusfertide,
including those who had been on placebo during Part 1a. Part 2 is an ongoing, 104-week,
long-term extension in which all subjects continue to receive open-label rusfertide. Part 3, added
via Protocol Amendment 6.1 after the data cutoff date, is an additional rusfertide treatment
extension for subjects who completed Part 2 and continued to derive clinical benefit but lacked
access to the study drug commercially or through a post-study access program.
Figure 3. Design Scheme for Study PTG-300-11

Source: NDA 220605 Protocol PTG-300-11, Figure 1.
Abbreviations: NDA, new drug application; PV, polycythemia vera

6.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria, PTG-300-11
Key eligibility criteria are listed below.
Inclusion Criteria
•

Male and female subjects aged 18 years (or the minimum country-specific age of consent if
≥18 years.

•

A diagnosis of PV based on the revised 2016 World Health Organization criteria.

34
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
•

Phlebotomy requiring defined as ALL of the following:
− At least three PHLs due to inadequate HCT control in the 28 weeks before randomization
or at least five PHLs due to inadequate HCT control in the 1 year before randomization,
and
− Last PHL due to inadequate HCT control within 3 months before randomization, and
− No PHL within 6 days prior to randomization (did not include day of PHL and day of
randomization in the 6-day count).
▪

•

Note: PHLs performed within an 8-day period were counted as a single PHL.

Complete blood count (CBC) values immediately prior to randomization:
− HCT <45%,
− White blood cell (WBC) count of 4000/µL to 20,000/µL (inclusive), and platelet count of
100,000/µL to 1,000,000/µL (inclusive)

•

Subjects receiving CRT at randomization must have been on a stable PV therapy regimen as
follows:
− HU: at least 2 months.
− JAK inhibitor: at least 2 months.
− IFN: at least 6 months.
▪

•

Note: A “stable dose regimen” of CRT did not mean an unchanged dose regimen.
Temporary adjustments in dose regimen or temporary suspension of dosing were
allowed. However, the total weekly doses of HU and JAK inhibitor or total monthly
dose of IFN could not be higher at randomization than the doses at the beginning of
the prerandomization observation period. The prerandomization observation period
was 2 months for HU and JAK inhibitors and 6 months for IFN.

Subjects treated with PHL alone at randomization must have stopped:
− HU at least 2 months before Screening.
− JAK inhibitor at least 2 months before Screening.
− IFN at least 6 months before Screening.

Exclusion Criteria
•

Subjects who required PHL at HCT levels lower than 45%.

•

Clinically significant thrombosis (e.g., DVT or splenic vein thrombosis) within 2 months
prior to randomization.

•

Active or chronic bleeding within 2 months prior to randomization.

•

Met the criteria for post-PV myelofibrosis as defined by the International Working GroupMyeloproliferative Neoplasms Research and Treatment.

•

Subjects who did not complete at least 4 days of Myelofibrosis Symptom Assessment Form
(MFSAF) assessments within 1 week prior to randomization.

6.2.1.3. Clinical Outcome Assessments
Study PTG-300-11 uses two clinical outcome assessment (COA) instruments in key secondary
endpoints: the PROMIS Fatigue Short Form (SF)-8a and Myelofibrosis Symptom Assessment
Form Version 4.0 (MFSAF v. 4.0).
PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a
The PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a is an eight-item PRO instrument designed to assess fatigue-related
symptoms and impacts. Each item is rated using a five-point verbal rating scale ranging from Not
at all/Never (1) to Very much/Always (5). The recall period is the previous week (past 7 days).
Scoring
The PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a is scored using item-level calibrations derived from the responsepattern scoring method. Subjects’ item responses are submitted to the Scoring Service which, in
conjunction with the measure’s calibrated item parameters, produces an expected a priori ability
estimate on the T-score metric. The Health Measures Scoring Service converts a raw score into a
unidimensional single T-score together with an associated error term. The T-score transforms the
raw score into a standardized score with a mean of 50 and a standard deviation of 10. The
calibration sample is the default “PROMIS Wave 1.”
MFSAF v. 4.0
The MFSAF v. 4.0 is a seven-item PRO instrument designed to assess myelofibrosis symptom
severity. Each item is rated using an 11-point numeric rating scale ranging from 0 (Absent) to 10
(Worst imaginable). The recall period is the previous 24 hours.
Scoring
The MFSAF v. 4.0 generates a total score, referred to as the total symptom score (TSS). The TSS
is derived by summing the seven items into a raw score, which ranges from 0 to 70, with a higher
score corresponding to more severe symptoms. The TSS can generate a Daily TSS7 score or
Weekly TSS7 score. For the Daily TSS7 score, all seven items must be completed to be
computed. For the Weekly TSS7 score, a weekly average score was calculated by averaging
daily scores over a 7-day interval (i.e., 7 days preceding the clinic visit; specifically, Day −6 to
Day 1). At least four daily TSS7 scores are required to calculate a weekly TSS7 score. Scores
were considered missing if four or more days of data were missing during the 1-week interval.

36
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
6.2.1.4. Statistical Analysis Plan (Version 1.0), PTG-300-11
Major Efficacy Endpoints
Primary Endpoint
The proportion of subjects achieving a response starting at Week 20 through Week 32 (inclusive)
who receive rusfertide compared to placebo. Response is defined as absence of PHL eligibility,
where PHL eligibility is defined as either:
•

A confirmed HCT ≥45% and that is at least 3% (absolute) higher than the baseline HCT.

or
•

An HCT >48%.

Key Secondary Endpoints
1. Mean number of PHLs from baseline through Week 32 (inclusive).
2. Proportion of subjects with all HCT values <45% from baseline through Week 32
(inclusive). A single HCT value ≥45% is allowed. HCT values from each clinical site’s local
laboratory will be used for this endpoint.
3. Mean change from baseline at end of Part 1a (Week 32) in the PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a total
T-score.
4. Mean change from baseline at end of Part 1a (Week 32) in the MFSAF v. 4.0 TSS7.
Multiplicity Adjustment
The study uses a fixed-sequence hierarchical testing procedure to control the family-wise error
rate at the 0.05 significance level across the primary and four key secondary endpoints.
Endpoints are tested in a prespecified order, with each tested only if the prior endpoint is
statistically significant at α=0.05. If any endpoint is not significant, testing stops and all
remaining endpoints are considered not statistically significant, regardless of their observed
p-values.
Sample Size Planning
The study was planned to enroll approximately 250 subjects, randomized 1:1 (125 per arm) to
rusfertide or placebo, with all endpoints tested at a two-sided α=0.05 level and overall Type 1
error controlled via a fixed-sequence hierarchical testing procedure.
The primary endpoint (proportion of subjects achieving a response for absence of PHL eligibility
from Weeks 20 to 32) assumes response rates of 25% for placebo and 50% for rusfertide, which
was informed by study CINC424B2301 (RESPONSE). This requires 95 subjects per arm to
achieve ~95% power using a z-test based on the large sample normal approximation to binomial
distribution.

37
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
Sample Size Considerations for Key Secondary Endpoints
•

Mean number of PHLs (baseline to Week 32): Assuming a mean of 3 (placebo) versus 1
(rusfertide) PHLs with a standard deviation of 4, 106 subjects per arm are needed for ≥95%
power using a two-sample t-test.

•

Proportion with all HCT values <45%: Assuming response rates of 60% (rusfertide) versus
35% (placebo), 106 subjects per arm are needed for ≥95% power using a z-test.

•

Mean change in PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a T-score at Week 32: Assuming a detectable
difference of 1 unit and a standard deviation of 2.8, 125 subjects per arm provide ≥80%
power using a two-sample t-test.

•

Mean change in MFSAF TSS7 at Week 32: Assuming a detectable difference of 2 units and a
standard deviation of 5.5, 125 subjects per arm provide ≥80% power by two-sample t-test.

Analysis Populations
•

Intent-to-treat set (ITT): Comprises all randomized subjects analyzed according to the
treatment assigned at randomization. For the U.S. submission, the ITT is the primary analysis
population for efficacy endpoints.

•

Full analysis set (FAS): Comprises all randomized subjects who received at least a dose of
study drug and who had a baseline and at least one postbaseline assessment in Part 1a.

•

Per-protocol set (PPS): Comprises the subset of subjects in the ITT who did not have major
protocol deviations at study entry or during the study that could impact efficacy endpoints.
The inclusion of subjects into the PPS was to be determined before soft database lock for
unblinding. This set was used for supportive analyses of the primary and key secondary
endpoints.

Analysis of Endpoints
Primary Efficacy Endpoint
•

Statistical method: The primary analysis uses the Cochran–Mantel–Haenszel (CMH) test
(General Association statistic) at a two-sided significance level of α=0.05, stratified by
ongoing PV therapy at randomization. The Mantel–Haenszel stratum-adjusted common RD
and its 95% CI are used as the primary measure of treatment difference.

•

Handling of intercurrent events and missing data: Subjects are classified as nonresponders
under several prespecified intercurrent event scenarios, including: 1) missing HCT
assessment at Week 32; 2) premature discontinuation of study treatment during Part 1a; 3)
more than one missing HCT value between Week 20 and Week 32; 4) PHL between
Week 20 and Week 32; 5) HCT ≥45% at Week 32 without confirmation; and 6) receipt of
prohibited medications or prohibited changes in CRT. Subjects who received the wrong
treatment or were mis-stratified are included in the efficacy analyses per the ITT principle.

38
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
Key Secondary Endpoint 1
•

Statistical method: The mean number of PHLs is analyzed using an analysis of covariance
model. The model includes the number of PHLs in the 32 weeks prior to study treatment as a
covariate, with treatment and ongoing PV therapy at randomization included as fixed effects.
The treatment comparison is based on the difference in least squares (LS) means between
rusfertide and placebo, with an associated 95% CI.

•

Handling of intercurrent events and missing data: Several intercurrent events are prespecified
with corresponding data-handling strategies. For subjects who discontinue study treatment
prematurely during Part 1a for any reason, missing PHL data from the time of
discontinuation through Week 32 are imputed using the subject’s historical PHL rate prior to
study treatment, normalized to 32 weeks. For subjects who receive prohibited medications or
prohibited changes in CRT, data after that point are treated as missing and imputed using the
same historical rate approach. Subjects who were randomized but never dosed are also
imputed using historical data. Subjects who received the wrong treatment or were
mis-stratified are analyzed as randomized per the ITT principle. For subjects who did not
comply with treatment, data are used as observed.

Key Secondary Endpoint 2
•

Statistical method: The analysis is conducted similarly to the primary endpoint, using the
CMH test at a two-sided significance level of α=0.05, stratified by ongoing PV therapy at
randomization. The Mantel–Haenszel stratum-adjusted common RD and its 95% CI are
reported as the measure of treatment difference.

•

Handling of intercurrent events and missing data: Several intercurrent events are prespecified
with corresponding nonresponder classifications. Subjects are classified as nonresponders if
they missed an HCT assessment at Week 32 and had at least one value ≥45% prior to
Week 32, if they discontinue study treatment prematurely during Part 1a for any reason, or if
they have more than two missing HCT values at any scheduled visit between baseline and
Week 32. Subjects are also classified as nonresponders if they are phlebotomized between
baseline and Week 32, or if they receive prohibited medications or prohibited changes in
CRT. Subjects who did not comply with treatment have their data used as observed. Subjects
randomized but never dosed are classified as nonresponders. Subjects who received the
wrong treatment or were mis-stratified are analyzed as randomized per the ITT principle.

Key Secondary Endpoint 3
•

Statistical method: The mean change from baseline in PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a T-score is
analyzed using a mixed model for repeated measures (MMRM). The model includes the
following:

•

Fixed effects: Treatment, scheduled visit, and stratification variable (ongoing PV therapy at
randomization).

•

Interaction terms: Treatment-by-visit and baseline-by-visit interactions.

•

Covariate: Baseline PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a T-score.

39
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
•

Covariance structure: Unstructured within-subject covariance matrix with restricted
maximum likelihood (ReML) estimation.

•

Degrees of freedom: Estimated using the Kenward–Roger method.

•

The primary contrast of interest: The LS mean difference between rusfertide and placebo at
Week 32. If the model fails to converge using an unstructured covariance matrix, simpler
covariance structures are employed sequentially: heterogeneous Toeplitz (TOEPH),
heterogeneous autoregressive of order 1 (ARH(1)), and autoregressive of order 1 (AR(1)).

•

Handling of intercurrent events and missing data: The MMRM approach utilizes all available
visit data, including data from subjects with some missing visits, to estimate the mean
treatment effect. For subjects who discontinue study treatment prematurely during Part 1a for
any reason, data are used as observed and incorporated into MMRM. For subjects who
receive prohibited medications or prohibited changes in CRT, data after that point are treated
as missing; as a sensitivity analysis, data are used as observed. For subjects who did not
comply with treatment, data are used as observed. For subjects randomized but never dosed,
missing data at each scheduled assessment are imputed by drawing a simple random sample
with replacement from all remaining placebo subjects with data at that assessment and
replacing the subject’s score with the mean of the 100 drawn samples. For subjects without
postbaseline assessment, the same resampling approach is applied drawing from all subjects
with the same treatment assignment. Subjects who received the wrong treatment or were
mis-stratified are analyzed as randomized per the ITT principle.

Key Secondary Endpoint 4
•

Statistical method: The mean change from baseline in MFSAF TSS7 is analyzed using an
MMRM, which is similar to that in Key Secondary Endpoint 3, with baseline MFSAF TSS7
substituted as the covariate. The primary contrast of interest is the LS mean difference
between rusfertide and placebo at Week 32.

•

Handling of intercurrent events and missing data: The intercurrent event and missing data
handling strategy follows the same approach as Key Secondary Endpoint 3.
6.2.1.5. Results of Analyses, PTG-300-11
6.2.1.5.1. Subject Disposition

Table 11 summarizes the disposition of all subjects. In PTG-300-11, a total of 399 subjects were
screened and 293 were randomized: 147 who received rusfertide and 146 who received placebo.
The percentage of subjects who completed the randomized double-blind phase was higher in the
placebo arm (141 [96.6%]) compared to the rusfertide arm (134 [91.2%]) The most common
reason for discontinuation of treatment was AEs, the rates of which were similar in the two arms
(eight [5.4%] subjects in the rusfertide arm and five [3.4%] subjects in the placebo arm).

6.2.1.5.3. Efficacy Results of the Primary Endpoint
The proportion of subjects meeting the primary endpoint of absence of PHL eligibility during
Weeks 20 to 32 was higher in the rusfertide arm (113 of 147; 76.9%) compared to the placebo
arm (48 of 146 subjects; 32.9%). The difference between the two treatment arms was statistically
significant (p<0.001), with a common RD of 43.8% (95% CI: 33.5%, 54.2%) favoring rusfertide
over placebo (Table 14).

Subgroup Analyses
The superiority of rusfertide over placebo for the primary endpoint was consistent across all
prespecified subgroups (Figure 4). Some numerical differences are observed in the point
estimates of the common RDs—for example, females versus males (60.40 versus 37.61) and
PHL + IFN versus PH + HU (58.62 versus 35.82). Despite these differences, the CIs for all
subgroups exclude zero, suggesting a consistent treatment benefit of rusfertide across the
subgroups examined.

Sensitivity Analyses
A sensitivity analysis was conducted by applying treatment policy strategy to handle the
intercurrent event of treatment discontinuation. In this sensitivity analysis, 122 of 147 subjects
(83.0%) in the treatment arm and 52 of 146 subjects (35.6%) in the control arm were responders.
The stratified CMH test demonstrated a statistically significant treatment difference (p<0.001).
The estimated common RD was 47.1% (95% CI: 37.1%, 57.1%), further supporting the
robustness of the primary findings.
Several additional sensitivity analyses for the primary endpoint were conducted, including
analyses using the PPS and the FAS, all of which produced results consistent with the primary
efficacy analysis.
6.2.1.5.4. Efficacy Results of Key Secondary Endpoints
Key Secondary Endpoint 1
Prior to randomization, the PHL burden was comparable between the two treatment arms. In the
32 weeks before dosing, the mean number of PHLs was 4.6 (±1.8) for subjects randomized to
rusfertide and 4.5 (±1.5) for those randomized to placebo.
In the analysis of the first key secondary endpoint, a statistically significant difference in the
mean number of PHLs was observed between the two treatment arms during the 32-week
double-blind period. The mean number of PHLs during Part 1a was 0.5 (±1.2) for subjects

6.2.1.6. Approach to Evaluation of Clinical Benefit: Clinical
Outcome Assessments
The Applicant used several COAs in Study PTG-300-11, of which the FDA review team
evaluated the PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a and MFSAF v. 4.0 endpoints for their adequacy to support
a demonstration of clinical benefit.
To support use of the instruments, the Applicant included an evidence dossier with the NDA
submission. The PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a and MFSAF v. 4.0 were reviewed for content validity,
other measurement properties (i.e., inter-rater, intrarater reliability, etc.), and score
interpretability (i.e., what constitutes clinically meaningful change scores on the COA-based
endpoint).

48
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
6.2.1.6.1. Content Validity
The Applicant completed the following instrument development activities to evaluate the content
validity of the PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a and MFSAF v. 4.0:
•

Targeted literature review.

•

Patient input (concept elicitation [CE] and cognitive debriefing [CD] interviews with patients
with PV).

The review team evaluated the data generated from the literature review and qualitative
interviews (hybrid concept elicitation and cognitive interviews; n=20).
The literature review identified the following symptoms and impacts as relevant to the
experience of patients with PV: itch, fatigue, tiredness, low energy, difficulty maintaining
stamina, and nonspecific pain.
A total of 20 subjects were interviewed from the United States and Canada. Based on the CE
portion of the interviews, the three most salient signs/symptoms spontaneously reported were:
•

Fatigue (12 subjects with an average bothersome score of 7.6/10).

•

Itchiness (11 subjects with an average bothersome score of 5.9/10).

•

Tiredness (11 subjects with an average bothersome score of 7.0/10).

The three most salient impacts spontaneously reported were:
•

Sleep issues (13 subjects with average impact rating of 6.8/10).

•

Difficulties with self-care or activities of daily living (11 subjects with an average impact
rating of 6.6/10).

A high-level summary of the results of the cognitive interview portion is provided below for
each instrument. For a comprehensive evaluation of the content validity of the PROMIS Fatigue
SF-8a and MFSAF v. 4.0, refer to the full COA review.
6.2.1.6.2. PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a
Findings from the hybrid CE/CD interviews suggested that the PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a items
measured concepts that were important and relevant to patients with PV and were reasonably
well understood by the subjects.
6.2.1.6.3. MFSAF v. 4.0
The hybrid CE/CD interviews yielded conflicting findings. Although the Applicant reported that
all subjects found the instructions clear and easy to understand, nine subjects (n=9/20; 45%)
noted that they did not have “myelofibrosis,” as referenced in the instructions. Additionally,
several MFSAF v. 4.0 items were not endorsed as relevant during the qualitative interviews.
Specifically, more than one-third of subjects (>40%) reported that night sweats (Item 2; n=10/18;
44.4%) and abdominal discomfort (Item 4; n=10/18; 44.4%) were not relevant to their
experience. More than half of subjects reported that pain under the ribs (Item 5; n=12/19;
63.2%), early satiety (Item 6; n=10/19; 52.6%), and bone pain (Item 7; n=11/19; 57.9%) were
49
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
also not relevant. These findings suggest that these symptoms may not be central to the PV
experience.
Data from Study PTG-300-11 further question the relevance of the MFSAF v. 4.0; item-level
distributional analyses revealed that 20% to more than 70% of subjects endorsed the least-severe
response category (Absent) across MFSAF v. 4.0 items at baseline.
Taken together, these qualitative and quantitative findings raise concerns regarding the content
relevance and appropriateness of the MFSAF v. 4.0 for patients with PV broadly, and
particularly for those who are asymptomatic or present with mild symptomatology.
6.2.1.6.4. Other Measurement Properties
The Applicant submitted quantitative psychometric evidence for the PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a
total T-score using data from Study PTG-300-11. The review team evaluated the submitted
evidence supporting the measurement properties of the PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a in adult patients
with PV.
Confirmatory factor analyses supported the unidimensional structure of the PROMIS Fatigue
SF-8a, with adequate factor loadings across all items at baseline (range: 0.902 to 0.969),
consistent with the use of a single total score. While the comparative fit index (CFI: 0.997) and
standardized root mean square residual (SRMR: 0.017) met the prespecified thresholds of ≥0.95
and ≤0.08, respectively, the root mean square error of approximation (RMSEA: 0.139; 95% CI:
0.117 to 0.162) did not meet the conventional threshold of ≤0.06 (Hu and Bentler 1999). In
response to FDA’s information request (received April 7, 2026), the Applicant noted that root
mean square error of approximation tends to overestimate poor fit in simple models with small
degrees of freedom. The review team found this explanation reasonable, as investigation of
residual correlations showed no evidence of local dependence between items that would indicate
model misspecification.
The PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a total T-scores demonstrated good test-retest reliability (intraclass
correlation coefficient [ICC]: 0.89; 95% CI: 0.85 to 0.92) and high internal consistency
reliability at baseline (Cronbach’s alpha: 0.97; McDonald’s omega: 0.99). Convergent evidence
was supported by strong correlations with the measures with the same or similar construct at
baseline, including the European Organization for Research and Treatment of Cancer Quality of
Life Questionnaire Core 30 (EORTC QLQ-C30) Fatigue scale (r=0.723), the Myeloproliferative
Neoplasm Symptom Assessment form (MPN-SAF) Fatigue item (r=0.851), and the MFSAF
Weekly Fatigue item (r=0.817). Known-groups evidence was demonstrated by a monotonic
relationship between the PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a total T-score and increasing severity levels
defined by the Patient Global Impression of Severity (PGI-S) as well as MFSAF weekly fatigue
item at baseline, indicating that the instrument can distinguish between patients with differing
levels of symptom severity.
Taken together, the quantitative evidence supports the PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a total T-score as a
reliable and valid measure of fatigue in adult patients with PV. However, the review team notes

50
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
that floor effects2 were observed for all items except Item 1 at baseline, and a large proportion of
subjects selected the least severe response options. These floor effects may limit the ability of the
PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a total T-score to capture treatment-related improvements in the study
population, as subjects with minimal fatigue at baseline have limited room for improvement.
6.2.1.6.5. Score Interpretability
Using anchor-based meaningful change analyses, the review team assessed whether the observed
treatment effect on the PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a total T-score endpoint reflects a clinically
meaningful benefit from the patient perspective.
The Applicant’s initial analyses considered PGI-S, Patient Global Impression of Change
(b) (4)
(PGI-C), and EORTC QLQ-C30 Item
as the anchors and ultimately used the PGI-S based on the correlation between the
PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a total T-score and anchors. However, the review team identified several
key limitations, including conceptual and recall period misalignment between the PGI-S and the
(b) (4)
PROMIS Fatigue measure (i.e., th
), post hoc specification of
the target anchor change category that is meaningful to patients, and no account for baseline
symptom severity and misclassification rates.
Based on these limitations, FDA issued an information request on March 26, 2026, for additional
anchor-based analyses using EORTC QLQ-C30 items more closely aligned with the concept of
fatigue (Items 10: “Did you need to rest?”, 12: “Have you felt weak?”, and 18: “Were you
tired?”). The review team notes a limitation of these additional analyses that the EORTC QLQC30 items are collected at baseline, introducing a timing misalignment between the anchor and
endpoint analysis periods. However, this limitation is considered less consequential than the
conceptual misalignment associated with the PGI-S.
After incorporating considerations for baseline symptom severity and misclassification rates, the
review team derived a final meaningful score difference (MSD) range of approximately −12.7 to
−4.5 points, wider than the Applicant’s estimate of −7.45 to −6.60 points based on the updated
analyses using the additional three anchor items. Applying this MSD range to the observed
efficacy data:
•

The between-group difference in the proportion of subjects achieving a meaningful
improvement was approximately 8% to 14.63%, providing some support that the observed
treatment effect reflects a clinically meaningful benefit from the subject’s perspective.

•

The LS mean change from baseline to Week 32 was approximately −1.79 points for the
rusfertide arm and 0.19 points for the placebo arm. Both estimates are markedly below the
lower bound of the MSD range (−4.5 points), suggesting that on average, subjects in neither
treatment arm are expected to have experienced a clinically meaningful improvement in
fatigue by Week 2.

The Applicant described this observation as a “ceiling effect” in the COA Evidence Dossier; however, the review
team considers this characterization to be more accurately described as a floor effect, as the observed clustering of
scores at the least severe end of the scale indicates that subjects had limited room to demonstrate improvement.
