6.2.1.6.6. Conclusions Regarding Adequacy of COAs
•

The PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a is measuring clinically relevant and important concepts to
patients with PV based on the CE portion of the qualitative interviews.

•

The PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a appears to be reasonably well understood based on the cognitive
portion of the qualitative interviews.

•

Quantitative evidence provided limited support that the observed statistically significant
treatment effect in the PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a reflects a clinically meaningful improvement
in fatigue for patients with PV. However, the Applicant’s proposed range of meaningful
change values likely falls within what could be considered a clinically meaningful
improvement in fatigue.

•

The MFSAF v. 4.0 does not appear to measure clinically relevant and important concepts to
patients with PV based on the submitted qualitative and quantitative evidence.

•

The interpretability of the MFSAF v. 4.0-based endpoint score results are difficult given the
content validity issues. Thus, from a COA perspective:
(b) (4)

− The PROMIS Fatigue SF-8a and the corresponding endpoint are adequate to potentially
support labeling claims if supported by the overall clinical study design and analysis.
53
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
COA results should be interpreted with caution given the potential for functional unblinding, as
subjects may have been tracking their number of PHLs, which may have influenced their
responses to the COA measures.

6.2.2. Confirmatory Evidence
Confirmatory evidence supporting the findings of Study PTG-300-11 comprises the results from
Study PTG-300-04 and mechanistic and PD data from across the rusfertide development
program.
6.2.2.1. Study PTG-300-04
PTG-300-04 was a phase 2 randomized withdrawal study in subjects with PV. The Division
identified several concerns in the conduct of the study that precluded its use as a primary
registration study; however, the study was used to provide confirmatory evidence. The concerns
identified with the study include:
•

Severe asymmetric early discontinuation in Part 2. A major concern is the substantial
imbalance in early discontinuation during the randomized withdrawal phase. In Part 2, 74.1%
of placebo subjects discontinued early compared with 26.9% of subjects receiving rusfertide.
This raises concern that the randomized comparison was not maintained for a meaningful
duration in many placebo subjects and that the observed treatment difference may be
influenced by asymmetric protocol-driven discontinuation.

•

Potential withdrawal effect and protocol-driven early discontinuation. This concern is further
supported by the short median time to treatment failure in the placebo group: 4.3 weeks for
loss of response and 7.7 weeks for both PHL eligibility and first HCT ≥45%. Because Part 2
lasted from Week 29 to Week 41, and subjects who became PHL-eligible were required by
protocol to end Part 2 early and enter Part 3, many placebo subjects appear to have exited the
randomized phase soon after meeting treatment-failure criteria. This raises concern that the
observed treatment difference may primarily reflect loss of response after withdrawal of
active treatment, combined with the study’s early-exit design, rather than a robust
demonstration of efficacy in an adequate and well-controlled study.

•

Clinical hold and coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) disruptions. The FDA clinical hold
dated September 16, 2021, together with COVID-19 pandemic disruptions, introduced
additional operational and analytical confounding. These disruptions may not be adequately
addressed by sensitivity analyses, particularly given the small, randomized analysis
population and the potential effect of prolonged prerandomization rusfertide exposure on
withdrawal-phase outcomes.

Design
PTG-300-04 was a phase 2, multicenter, three-part clinical study evaluating the safety and
efficacy of rusfertide in adults with PHL-requiring PV. The study was conducted at 16 sites (14
in the United States and 2 in India) and enrolled 70 subjects in total.

54
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
The study schema is shown in Figure 7. The study comprises three sequential parts:
•

Part 1—Open-label dose-finding phase (Weeks 1 to 28):
− All enrolled subjects receive rusfertide SC, starting at 20 mg weekly.
− Doses are titrated (range: 10 to 120 mg/week) to maintain HCT <45%, with an
aspirational target of ≤42%.
− Duration: 28 weeks (could be extended for subjects affected by the clinical hold).
− Primary goal: Identify an effective, tolerable dose for each subject.

•

Part 2—Blinded randomized withdrawal phase (Weeks 29 to 41):
− Subjects who were not PHL-eligible at Week 29 were randomized 1:1 to:
▪
▪

Continue rusfertide (at their established dose), or
Switch to identical-appearing placebo.

− Duration: 12 weeks.
− Subjects who became PHL-eligible were to end participation in Part 2 early and begin
Part 3.
•

Part 3—OLE (up to ~3.5 years):
− All subjects (including those who had been on placebo in Part 2) received open-label
rusfertide.
− Doses continued to be titrated to maintain HCT <45%.
− Duration: Up to approximately 3.5 years.

The efficacy evaluation was based on Part 2 of the study. The primary efficacy endpoint was the
proportion of responders during Part 2 (Weeks 29 to 41). There were no prespecified key
secondary efficacy endpoints in Study PTG-300-04.
Figure 7. Design Scheme for Study PTG-300-04

Source: NDA 220605, Protocol PTG-300-04, Figure 1.
Abbreviation: NDA, new drug application

55
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
The study enrolled male and female subjects ≥18 years of age who met the revised 2016 World
Health Organization criteria for a diagnosis of PV. Subject had to have an HCT <45% before
initial dosing and must be PHL-requiring (regularly spaced PHLs over at least 28 weeks prior to
first dose with at least two PHLs during the 28 weeks prior to first dose and last PHL within
approximately 12 weeks before Screening). Patients could receive CRT (HU, IFN, or
ruxolitinib), and if they were, must have received CRT for at least 24 weeks and been on a stable
dose or had a decreasing dose for at least 8 weeks prior to their first dose of rusfertide.
Subjects could not have an absolute neutrophil count (ANC) <1,000/µL, platelet count
<100,000/µL, estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) <40 mL/min/1.73 m2, alanine
aminotransferase (ALT) or aspartate aminotransferase (AST) ≥2.5× upper limit of normal (ULN)
or direct bilirubin >1.5×ULN, or other clinically meaningful laboratory abnormality. Patients
with a history of clinically significant thrombosis within 1 month of Screening, active or chronic
bleeding within 4 weeks of Screening, or who meet the criteria for post-PV myelofibrosis (as
defined by the International Working Group–Myeloproliferative Neoplasms Research and
Treatment (IWG-MRT) were also excluded.
Endpoints
The primary efficacy endpoint was the proportion of responders during the blinded randomized
withdrawal period (RWP) (Week 29 to Week 41), where:
•

A subject will be considered a responder during the blinded RWP if HCT control is
maintained without PHL eligibility.

•

“PHL eligibility” is defined if any one of the following three criteria is met:
− HCT 45% that was 3% higher than Week 29 prerandomization HCT value, or
− HCT >48%, or
− An increase of 5% in HCT compared to the Week 29 prerandomization HCT value.

Although a few secondary efficacy endpoints were prespecified, none was designated as a key
secondary endpoint within a formal testing hierarchy. No multiplicity adjustment was planned.
Secondary endpoints are not discussed further.
Sample Size Planning
The sample size for Study PTG-300-04 was determined based on the Part 2 blinded RWP.
Assuming a response rate of 80% for PTG-300 and 32% for placebo, 25 subjects per arm
(approximately 50 in total) were required to detect a statistically significant difference at a
two-sided significance level of 0.05 with at least 90% power. To account for an anticipated
dropout rate of approximately 15% during the open-label dose-finding period, the original
enrollment target was set at approximately 60 subjects.
Following the FDA-imposed clinical hold in September 2021—during which 31 subjects had
already been randomized into Part 2 and were subsequently rolled over to Part 3, rendering them
ineligible for the primary efficacy analysis—the enrollment target was revised upward to up to
80 subjects to compensate for a potentially higher dropout rate. In practice, however, the clinical
hold had minimal impact on dropout, with only two Part 1 subjects not restarting the study. As a
result, only 7 additional subjects beyond the original cohort were needed, yielding a total actual
enrollment of 70 subjects.
56
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
Analysis Plan
The primary endpoint was analyzed using a two-sided Fisher’s exact test, comparing the
responder rates between the rusfertide and placebo arms at the 0.05 significance level. The
analysis is conducted on the FAS.
Handling of Intercurrent Events and Missing Data
•

Missing postrandomization data are handled using a nonresponder imputation approach,
whereby any subject with a missing HCT assessment at Week 41 is automatically classified
as a nonresponder.

•

Subjects who discontinue study treatment prematurely after at least one injection in Part 2 are
classified as nonresponders.

•

Subjects who receive a PHL during Part 2, whether or not they meet formal PHL eligibility
criteria, are classified as nonresponders.

•

Clinical hold-related discontinuations: Subjects who discontinued Part 2 due to the clinical
hold are explicitly excluded from the FAS and therefore from the primary efficacy analyses.

Results
For the blinded RWP, the primary efficacy analysis was conducted on the FAS, which was
defined as all subjects who:
•

Either completed or discontinued Part 2 prior to the clinical hold, or

•

Were randomized after restart of the study and were treated with any study drug in Part 2.

The FAS does not include subjects who were randomized and were undergoing treatment in
Part 2 at the time of the clinical hold.
Of the 70 subjects enrolled in Part 1, 61 completed the phase, of whom 59 entered Part 2 and
were randomized to receive rusfertide or placebo. Six the fifty-nine had their blinded treatment
interrupted by a clinical hold, leaving 53 in the FAS. Of the 53 subjects in the FAS, 27 were
randomized to receive placebo and 26 to receive rusfertide.
The response rate was statistically significantly higher in the rusfertide arm (69.2%, 18 of 26
subjects) compared to the placebo arm (14.8%, 4 of 27 subjects), with a p-value of <0.0001. The
absolute difference between treatment arms was 54.4% (95% CI: 32.2%, 76.6%).
Subgroup analyses of the primary endpoint were conducted across multiple prespecified subject
characteristics and consistently demonstrated higher response rates in rusfertide-treated subjects
compared to placebo-treated subjects. For example:
1. By risk status, among high-risk subjects (defined as age ≥60 years and/or history of prior
thrombotic event), the response rate was 72.7% for rusfertide versus 11.8% for placebo. The
difference in rate was 61.0% (95% CI: 30.5%, 91.4%). For low-risk subjects, the response
rate was 66.7% for rusfertide versus 20.0% for placebo, demonstrating benefit regardless of
disease risk category. The difference in rate was 46.7% (95% CI: 12.3%, 81.1%).

57
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
2. By concurrent CRT status, among subjects receiving PHL alone (no CRT), the response rate
was 66.7% for rusfertide versus 18.2% for placebo. The difference in the rate was 48.5%
(95% CI: 17.0%, 80.0%). Among subjects receiving concurrent CRT, the response rate was
75.0% for rusfertide versus 12.5% for placebo, suggesting that rusfertide provided
meaningful benefit both as monotherapy and in combination with existing cytoreductive
agents. The difference in the rate was 62.5% (95% CI: 28.4%, 96.6%).
Several sensitivity analyses for the primary endpoint were conducted. These included an analysis
of all randomized subjects (incorporating the six subjects who discontinued Part 2 due to the
clinical hold [treated as treatment failures]), and a multiple imputation to assess robustness with
respect to missing HCT data. All the analyses produced results consistent with those of the
primary efficacy analysis.
6.2.2.2. Additional Confirmatory Evidence
In patients with PV, iron homeostasis is dysregulated and hepcidin levels are suppressed, leading
to increased iron availability and excessive erythrocytosis. Rusfertide is a hepcidin mimetic that
inhibits ferroportin, thereby reducing iron availability for erythropoiesis and controlling
excessive red-blood-cell production. Thus, the PD effects on iron parameters are considered
evidence of activity related to rusfertide’s mechanism of action. Changes in ferritin and
transferrin in Study PTG-300-11 (Table 20) and for Studies PTG-300-04 and PTG-300-08 (Table
21) are shown below.
Ferritin and transferrin were selected as the most appropriate confirmatory PD markers for
rusfertide based on both statistical and mechanistic considerations. Mechanistically, ferritin and
transferrin are the PD markers most directly reflective of rusfertide’s action as a hepcidin
mimetic, and importantly, they operate as inverse markers of the same underlying biological
process. By blocking ferroportin-mediated iron export, rusfertide promotes iron retention in
storage cells, which is captured by rising ferritin levels. Concurrently, as iron stores are
replenished, the body’s compensatory upregulation of transferrin—a hallmark of iron deficiency,
whereby the body produces more transport carriers to scavenge available circulating iron—is no
longer needed, and transferrin levels fall accordingly. This inverse relationship between the two
markers is particularly compelling as confirmatory PD evidence: They move in opposite
directions for the same underlying reason, providing two independent and mechanistically
coherent lines of evidence pointing to the same conclusion—that rusfertide is restoring iron
homeostasis as expected from its mechanism of action.
In addition, across studies, serum iron and TSAT demonstrated high interindividual variability,
with standard deviations at or exceeding the mean in some instances, limiting their
interpretability as reliable markers of treatment effect (based on the Applicant’s reported results).
In contrast, ferritin showed a large and consistent magnitude of increase across all three studies,
and transferrin demonstrated a consistent decrease with notably low variability, reflecting
reliable directional changes across subjects.
For Study PTG-300-11 (as seen in Table 20) at baseline, ferritin and transferrin levels were
similar in both treatment arms, consistent with the iron depletion characteristic of PV patients.
Following 32 weeks of treatment, the rusfertide arm demonstrated a striking approximately
six-fold increase in ferritin from baseline to Week 32; the rise continued through Week 52,
alongside a meaningful and consistent decrease in transferrin, reflecting restoration of iron
58
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
homeostasis. In contrast, the placebo arm showed virtually no change in either marker through
Week 32, with values remaining nearly identical to baseline. The validity of these findings is
further reinforced by the crossover design: Once placebo patients initiated rusfertide at Week 32,
both ferritin and transferrin rapidly moved in the same direction as in the rusfertide arm, with
Week 52 values converging closely between the two arms. This crossover effect provides
independent confirmation that the changes observed in the rusfertide arm are attributable to a
drug effect rather than disease progression or other confounding factors.
Consistent increases in ferritin and decreases in transferrin were observed in both PTG-300-04
and PTG-300-08, with ferritin rising approximately 10-fold from baseline by end of study in
PTG-300-04 and nearly 6-fold by Week 52 in PTG-300-08 (Table 21).
The consistency of these PD findings across three independent studies provides confirmatory
evidence supportive of the clinical benefit demonstrated in the pivotal Study PTG-300-11.

7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Nonclinical Data
The pharmacologic mechanism of rusfertide as a hepcidin mimetic/ferroportin inhibitor revealed
a well-characterized risk of exaggerated on-target iron restriction, which has been identified as a
significant safety concern in two nonclinical species. Additional safety concerns with chronic
59
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
rusfertide administration identified in nonclinical studies include potential thrombotic risk related
to elevated platelet counts, injection-site and local hypersensitivity reactions, and embryofetal
toxicity.
The Agency’s evaluation of genetic toxicology and carcinogenicity studies with rusfertide
identified no malignancy concerns. Detailed study reviews and conclusions are provided in the
supplemental Pharmacology and Toxicology review (filed separately).
Exaggerated On-Target Iron-Restriction
In healthy rats and cynomolgus monkeys, rusfertide induced a pharmacologically mediated,
on-target functional iron deficiency characterized by decreased circulating iron and TSAT,
reduced bone-marrow iron availability, and microcytic anemia with compensatory reticulocytosis
and thrombocytosis. Healthy animals do not recapitulate the heterogeneous, disease- and
treatment-modified iron-depleted state of patients with PV and chronically treated animals
generally maintain compensatory hematopoietic responses. However, one monkey died on
Week 26 at a human equivalent dose (HED) of ~97 mg/week after progressive clinical decline,
severe anemia, ~78% reduced HCT, and bone-marrow hypocellularity alongside secondary
granulocytic and lymphoid reductions. These findings suggest failure of compensatory
erythropoiesis and broader cytopenic effects under sustained severe anemia, with limited
relevance for iron overload in the setting of PV.
Unlike phlebotomy, rusfertide restricts functional iron bioavailability rather than inducing
absolute iron deficiency; therefore, ferritin may be preserved or increased despite reduced serum
iron and TSAT. Because patients with PV may initiate treatment with variable iron reserves
(Camaschella 2015; Ginzburg et al. 2018), the therapeutic window between iron restriction for
erythropoiesis control and pathological marrow suppression may differ across patients. These
findings support the monitoring of CBC parameters, reticulocytes, ferritin, and TSAT during
chronic treatment, with attention to worsening anemia or emerging cytopenias even when HCT is
stably controlled.
Thrombotic Risk
Repeated-dose rusfertide administration produced dose- and duration-dependent thrombocytosis
in rats and cynomolgus monkeys. In monkeys, platelet counts increased at HEDs of
≥39 mg/week, peaking at >300% above concurrent controls at an HED of ~117 mg/week. At
maximal exposures, thrombocytosis was not fully reversible after a 35-day recovery period,
suggesting that prolonged or high-dose exposure may result in sustained platelet elevations.
In healthy nonclinical models, thrombocytosis was considered a nonadverse, secondary
compensatory mechanism driven by pharmacologically mediated anemia, evidenced by
concurrent erythroid suppressions and reticulocytosis. Crucially, monkeys demonstrated no
abnormalities in coagulation parameters (prothrombin time, activated partial thromboplastin
time, or fibrinogen) or histopathological evidence of thrombosis, although rats exhibited slight
PT prolongation at HED ≤29 mg/week.
Patients with PV have an elevated baseline thrombotic risk driven by multilineage
myeloproliferation, platelet activation, and endothelial dysfunction. Therefore, rusfertideinduced thrombocytosis, even if compensatory to pharmacology-mediated iron restriction, could
exacerbate any underlying thrombogenicity.
60
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
These findings supported careful dose titration, monitoring of platelet counts and thrombotic
events, and consideration of concomitant cytoreductive, antiplatelet, or anticoagulant therapy.
Injection-Site and Local Hypersensitivity Reactions
Subcutaneous administration of rusfertide produced dose-dependent ISRs in rats and cynomolgus
monkeys. Histopathological findings revealed SC fibrosis, edema/hemorrhage, and myofiber
degeneration/regeneration in rats, and perivascular lymphocytic infiltrates and active chronic
inflammation in monkeys. Although fibrosis was observed in vehicle controls, its incidence and
severity were amplified in rusfertide-treated arms.
While localized ISRs are common with SC-administered therapeutic peptides, the findings of
active inflammation and myofiber remodeling were observed in rusfertide-treated animals only,
supporting a direct, drug-related etiology. These nonclinical findings support proactive clinical
mitigation strategies, specifically injection-site rotation and monitoring for progressive or
refractory ISRs.
Embryofetal Toxicity
Nonclinical reproductive and developmental toxicity studies with rusfertide demonstrated
embryofetal lethality, structural malformations, and fetal growth restriction in rats and rabbits at
exposures at or below those anticipated at the MRHD. These adverse developmental findings
occurred in the absence of overt maternal toxicity at similar exposure levels, underscoring the
sensitivity of the developing embryo and fetus to perturbations in iron homeostasis. During
human pregnancy, maternal hepcidin concentrations are physiologically suppressed in the second
and third trimesters, facilitating enhanced intestinal iron absorption and mobilization of maternal
iron stores to increase iron bioavailability for placental transfer supporting fetal development and
the establishments of neonatal iron stores (Koenig et al. 2014; Fisher and Nemeth 2017;
McDonald et al. 2022). Pharmacological inhibition of hepcidin activity by rusfertide is therefore
expected to counteract this adaptive suppression, limit ferroportin-mediated iron export, and
reduce the maternal iron supply available for placental transfer to the fetus. These findings
support the implementation of robust contraceptive requirements for woman of childbearing
potential treated with rusfertide.

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
There are no approved hepcidin mimetics to allow for evaluation of drug class or other drugspecific risks or safety concerns.

7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified
Through Postmarket Experience
Rusfertide is not currently approved or marketed in the United States or any other country.

7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
The rusfertide PV clinical development program enrolled 375 subjects across three studies
(PTG-300-11, PTG-300-04/21, and PTG-300-08), see Table 22 for individual study details.
The primary safety assessment for the intended population of subjects with PV was based on the
analysis of the 32-week randomized placebo-controlled treatment period, Part 1a, of PTG-30011. This is supported by analysis of the open-label durability follow-up (Part 1b) and long-term
extension (Part 2).
An integrated assessment of safety was based on analysis of all studies, PTG-300-11, PTG-30004/21, and PTG-300-08, focusing on serious adverse events (SAEs), adverse events of special
interest (AESIs), and rare but clinically significant events that might be identified only in
analysis of a larger safety set but where even a single event would be notable.

Clinical study data were independently analyzed by FDA. Clinical data scientists from the
Division of Biomedical Informatics generated the safety tables and figures for Study
PTG-300-11. These include safety tables and figures for exposure data, TEAEs, and SAEs.
Analyses of TEAEs from Studies PTG-300-11, PTG 300-04, and PTG-08 (N=375) were
performed by the Clinical Reviewer using JMP, JMP Clinical, and the Office of Computational
Science Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA)-based Adverse Event
Diagnostics tool.
The Applicant’s translations of verbatim terms to the MedDRA (v. 28.0) preferred terms (PTs)
for TEAEs were reviewed and found to be acceptable. The review team did not identify any
major data quality or integrity issues that precluded performing a thorough safety review.
TEAEs were defined as any AE worsening or occurring after receiving a dose of the study drug.
TEAEs were collected 28 days after the last administration of rusfertide. All AEs in the reviewed
studies were graded by investigators using the terms mild (asymptomatic or mild symptoms,
intervention not indicated), moderate (minimal, local or noninvasive intervention indicated;
limiting age-appropriate instrumental activities of daily living), and severe (severe or medically
significant but not immediately life-threatening; hospitalization or prolongation of
hospitalization indicated; disabling; limiting self-care activities of daily living; life-threatening;
or death).
TEAEs were analyzed at all levels of the MedDRA hierarchy and using Office of New Drug
Custom Medical Queries (OCMQs) narrow. OCMQs (narrow) are a collection of approximately
110 standardized groups, each of which comprises AE terms that reflect a specific medical
concept. Each OCMQ term is composed of one or more PTs. OCMQs are not mutually
exclusive; that is, an individual PT may be counted in more than one OCMQ. When reporting
OCMQ analyses, all OCMQs, including overlapping terms, are included in the presentation of
the data in this review. In addition, not all PTs are included in an OCMQ. To assure that all
potential safety signals are identified, analysis of all reported TEAEs not included in at least one
OCMQ were identified and analyzed.
The review of safety was based on:
•

Clinical study reports

•

Protocols for studies

•

Datasets for the populations of the clinical studies described above (data cutoff May 27,
2025)

•

Summary of clinical safety

•

Ninety-day safety update (data cutoff: December 10, 2025)

•

Subject narratives

•

Case report forms

•

Case report forms and narratives were provided and reviewed for SAEs, and AESIs

•

Applicant’s response to an FDA IR; received May 14, 2026

•

Applicant’s response to an FDA IR; received June 11, 2026

7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
Summaries of drug exposure for Study PTG-300-11 and for the Integrated Summary of Safety
(defined as 375 subjects with PV who received rusfertide in Studies PTG-300-11/04/21/08) for
this safety assessment are shown in Table 23.
The safety analysis set for PTG-300-11 Part 1a comprised 291 enrolled subjects who received at
least one dose of study drug (rusfertide or placebo). Two subjects were randomized but received
no study drugs. Both of these were randomized to receive rusfertide. Thus, the safety analysis set
and the ITT set used for efficacy differed slightly; also, the demographics and baseline disease
characteristics differed slightly from those reported for the ITT in Table 12.
For the randomized controlled portion of PTG-300-11 (Part 1a), the mean exposure (SD) was
30.63 (5.89) weeks with a median of 32 weeks (range: 2 to 36 weeks). The mean (SD) exposure
to rusfertide across all of Study PTG-300-11 was 61.18 (27.49) weeks with a median of
63 weeks (range: 2 to 132.9 weeks). This includes subjects who crossed over from receiving
placebo in Part 1a as well as those continuing in the open-label parts.
For the Integrated Safety Set, the mean (SD) exposure was 73.31 (54/66) with a median of
63 weeks (range: 2 to 254 weeks). Overall, 68.3% of the 375 subjects received ≥1 year of
exposure, with 33.6% reaching ≥1.5 years, 18.9% receiving ≥2 years, and 12% receiving
≥3 years. The total rusfertide exposure in PV was roughly 550 subject-years. The size of the
safety database is considered adequate for a safety assessment of rusfertide for the proposed
indication, subject population, dosage regimen, and duration, given the rarity of PV.

Applicability to the U.S. Population
PTG-300-11 enrolled a predominantly Caucasian population across North American and
European sites and is most directly relevant to the anticipated U.S. patient population.
PTG-300-04/21 provides longer-term safety data in a similar population with higher baseline
risk, and PTG-300-08 provides safety data in an exclusively East Asian population with more
aggressive baseline myeloproliferative disease characteristics. The consistency of the primary
safety signals across all three studies and across both ethnic populations supports their
generalizability to the broad U.S. PV population.

7.6. Safety Results
7.6.1. Summary
Overall, rusfertide demonstrated a manageable safety profile in subjects with PV, with no fatal
AEs reported across the clinical development program. The primary safety assessment was based
on the 32-week randomized, placebo-controlled period of Study PTG-300-11, which enrolled
291 subjects (145 rusfertide, 146 placebo). The size of the safety database (375 subjects across
all studies) is considered adequate for safety assessment given the rarity of PV.

67
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
Key safety findings from Study PTG-300-11, Part 1a (32-week randomized controlled period)
are:
•

There were no deaths in the rusfertide clinical development program.

•

SAEs occurred in 3.4% of rusfertide-treated subjects compared to 4.8% of placebo-treated
subjects during the 32-week controlled period.

•

Adverse events leading to permanent discontinuation occurred in 5.5% of rusfertide-treated
subjects compared to 2.7% of placebo-treated subjects, primarily driven by ISRs and
thrombocytosis-related events.

•

Dose reductions due to AEs occurred more commonly in the rusfertide arm (4.1%) compared
to placebo (0.7%), primarily due to anemia.

•

Most AEs were mild to moderate in severity. Severe or worse AEs occurred in 9.7% of
rusfertide-treated subjects compared to 6.8% of placebo-treated subjects.

•

The most common adverse reactions were ISRs (56%), anemia (16%), thrombocytosis (8%),
and dyspnea (8%).

Three interrelated safety signals were identified and represent key safety concerns of this review,
thrombocytosis (Safety Issue #1), leukocytosis (Safety Issue #2), and JAK2V617F (Safety
Issue #3).
The results of the analysis of Safety Review Issue #1 were addressed with a Warnings and
Precautions in the labeling for new or worsening thrombocytosis. A PMR to complete Study
PTG-300-11 and enhanced pharmacovigilance activities through 5 years postapproval will
further evaluate the long-term risks of thrombocytosis, thromboembolic events, and CRT
management patterns, addressing Safety Issues #1 to 3.

7.6.2. PTG-300-11
7.6.2.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse
Events
Part 1a
A high-level overview of TEAEs for Part 1a of PTG-300-11 is summarized in Table 25.
A total of 131 subjects (90.3%) in the rusfertide arm and 126 subjects (86.3%) in the placebo
arm experienced at least one TEAE during the 32-week treatment period. The frequency of AEs
was comparable between the two treatment arms, but ISRs, anemia, dyspnea, and thrombocytosis
were more frequently reported in the rusfertide arm. No deaths were reported in either treatment
arm during the study period. SAEs occurred in five subjects (3.4%) in the rusfertide arm and
seven (4.8%) in the placebo arm.
AEs leading to permanent discontinuation of study drug occurred more commonly in the
rusfertide arm (eight subjects; 5.5%) compared to the placebo arm (four subjects; 2.7%).

68
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
AEs leading to dose reduction occurred more commonly in the rusfertide arm (six subjects;
4.1%) compared to the placebo arm (one subject; 0.7%) in the placebo arm. Dose delays and
interruptions were uncommon in both arms, occurring in ≤2.8% of subjects.
With respect to severity, severe or worse AEs occurred more commonly in the rusfertide arm (14
subjects, 9.7%) than in the placebo arm (10 subjects; 6.8%). Moderate AEs were reported more
commonly in the rusfertide arm (80 subjects; 55.2%) than in the placebo arm (72 subjects;
49.3%). Mild AEs were reported less commonly in the rusfertide arm (37 subjects; 25.5%) than
in the placebo arm (44 subjects; 30.1%). Most AEs were of mild to moderate severity.

7.6.2.2. Deaths
No deaths were reported in Study PTG-300-11.
7.6.2.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Part 1a
In Part 1a, 13 SAEs occurred in 12 subjects. In the rusfertide arm, five SAEs occurred in five
subjects (3.4%): acute MI (one subject), blood glucose increased (one subject), diabetic
ketoacidosis (DKA) (one subject), hematuria (one subject), and hematoma (one subject). In the
placebo arm, eight SAEs occurred in seven subjects (4.8%). Two SAEs, diarrhea and drug
hypersensitivity, each occurred in a single subject. The remaining six SAEs each occurred in a
separate subject: headache, bullous erysipelas, infective exacerbation of asthma,
hemiparesthesia, colorectal cancer, and peripheral vascular disorder, each occurring in one
subject.
Analysis of SAEs by organ system and OCMQ (Narrow) produced similar findings.
Narratives for the five subjects in the rusfertide arm who experienced an SAE are provided
below. The Applicant assessed each of these events as unrelated to rusfertide. The Division
agreed with their assessment for the events of cholangiocarcinoma, worsening hematuria,
diabetic ketoacidosis, and blood glucose elevated, but considered the SAE of acute MI as at least
possibly related to rusfertide.

(b) (6) ),

(b) (6) and received rusfertide 20 mg SC
The subject was enrolled in Part 1a on
weekly. On SD 23, the subject was admitted to hospital with oppressive chest pain, right arm
pain, left arm pain, tiredness, and stomach discomfort. Peak troponin I was 138.2 ng/L
(reference range: <15.6 ng/L). Coronary angiography on SD 26 showed mild atheromatosis
of all three coronary arteries with a possible intramyocardial bridge in the mid-distal left
anterior descending (artery), with normal left ventricular function. MINOCA was suspected,
possibly due to thrombocytosis or coronary spasm in the context of JAK2-positive PV.
Echocardiography on SD 27 showed normal systolic function and left ventricular ejection
fraction of approximately 65%. A conservative treatment plan was introduced including
isosorbide dinitrate, acetylsalicylate lysine, fondaparinux, clopidogrel, and atorvastatin. The
event was considered resolved on SD 28 and the subject was discharged in good general
condition with a diagnosis of “MINOCA as a result of thrombocytosis in the context of
JAK2-positive PV.”

No action was taken with rusfertide (dose not changed). Both the Investigator and the
Applicant assessed the acute MI as not related to rusfertide, attributing the event to the
subject’s longstanding, uncontrolled PV (with marked thrombocytosis and JAK2 positivity)
as the most important risk factor, and noting the pre-existing ECG abnormality and possible
intramyocardial bridge identified on coronary angiography.
The Division did not agree. The MINOCA occurred in a low-risk PV subject (age <60 years
and no prior thrombotic event), within 23 days of initiating rusfertide, during which time her
platelet count was rising. Platelets reported by the study central laboratory were 689 at
screening (SD −10), 699 at baseline (SD 1), 774 at SD 16, and 1,029×10⁹/L at SD 30. The
temporal relationship between the platelet rise and the CV event supports rusfertide
associated thrombocytosis as a contributing factor. Therefore, the Division concludes that the
event is at least possibly related to rusfertide.

subjects; these are summarized in Table 26. Based on the mechanism of action of rusfertide and
the subjects’ past medical history, the Division agreed with the Applicant’s assessment of
causality as not related or possibly related to 13 of the SAEs. The Division did not agree with the
(b) (6) )
Applicant’s assessment of causality of as not related in the SAEs of ischemic stroke (
(b) (6) ). In both cases, the events occurred in the setting of increased platelets
and acute MI (
associated with the onset of rusfertide exposure, and the contribution of rusfertide to the cause of
the event cannot be ruled out. The Division considers these events as at least possibly related to
rusfertide.
Review of SAEs during Parts 1b and 2 did not identify any additional SAE signals not identified
in analysis of Part 1a.

7.6.2.4. Adverse Events Leading to Treatment
Discontinuation
Part 1a
AEs leading to permanent discontinuation of study drug occurred in eight subjects (5.5%) in the
rusfertide arm compared to four subjects (2.7%) in the placebo arm. These are presented in Table
27 by system organ class and PT; narratives for the subjects in the rusfertide arm who
discontinued treatment due to TEAEs are presented below.
Based on mechanism of action, the Division considered three events as related to rusfertide:
injection-site erythema in one subject, consistent with local effects of SC administration; von
Willebrand disease in one subject, related to extreme thrombocytosis (platelet count
1,360×10⁹/L), a known PD effect of rusfertide; and anemia in one subject, consistent with the
intended pharmacologic effect of rusfertide on erythropoiesis. Additional events assessed as at
least possibly related to rusfertide included facial swelling and dyspepsia in one subject, which
resolved after discontinuation and abdominal pain; and decreased appetite, and fatigue in one
subject, which resolved within 2 to 4 weeks after discontinuation, supporting at least a possible
relationship.
Events assessed as unlikely related to rusfertide included pruritus (not injection site) in one
subject, which remained unresolved at discontinuation and may be related to underlying PV, and
multiple symptoms (night sweats, oropharyngeal pain, weight gain, pain, pruritus) in one subject,
where rapid resolution (within 2 days during continued rusfertide exposure) and lack of
mechanistic plausibility argued against a causal relationship.

Narratives
PTG-300-11-

(b) (6)

: A 69-year-old white male randomized to receive rusfertide (Part 1a).

Discontinuing AE: Anemia (Grade 1): Subject had a history of benign prostatic hyperplasia
and baseline intermittent hematuria. During Part 1a, he developed worsening hematuria requiring
hospitalization, associated with initiation of anagrelide after platelet count increased from
baseline of 625 to 831 by SD 29. By SD 90 hematuria recurred and required hospital admission
for management, which included bladder irrigation. On SD 100, he underwent transurethral
resection of the prostate. Following the procedure, he developed Grade 1 anemia (onset SD 106;
Hb 10.4 g/dL) that persisted despite rusfertide dose reduction to 10 mg. On SD 228 the subject
entered Part 1b, and on SD 253 rusfertide was held due to anemia. The subject permanently
discontinued treatment on SD 316. Both the Investigator and the Protagonist assessed the event
of anemia as related to rusfertide. The Division agreed with the assessment of causality.
PTG-300-11-

(b) (6)

: A 75-year-old male randomized to receive rusfertide (Part 1a).

Discontinuing AEs: Facial swelling (Grade 2) and dyspepsia (Grade 1): Subject developed
intermittent facial and bilateral hand swelling and recurrent dyspepsia during the study. Facial
swelling worsened to Grade 2 on SD 88 and was managed with topical triamcinolone. Both
events resolved by SD 121, at which point the subject withdrew consent, permanently
discontinued treatment, and was terminated. Both events were assessed as related to rusfertide by
both the Investigator and the Protagonist. The Division agreed with the assessment of causality.
PTG-300-11-

(b) (6)

: A 55-year-old white male randomized to receive rusfertide (Part 1a).

Discontinuing AE: Injection-site erythema (Grade 1): Subject experienced a series of ISRs
from the start of treatment, including Grade 2 pain on Day 1, Grade 2 skin rash after the second
dose, and intermittent erythema at the lower left quadrant, which resolved. A new injection-site
erythema at the lower right quadrant developed on SD 36, at which point rusfertide was
withdrawn. The erythema resolved by SD 46. The event was assessed as related to rusfertide by
both the Investigator and the Protagonist. The Division agreed with the assessment of causality.
PTG-300-11-

(b) (6)

: A 69-year-old white male randomized to receive rusfertide (Part 1a).

Discontinuing AEs: Night sweats, oropharyngeal pain, weight gain, pain, pruritus: Subject
developed multiple AEs within the first 2 months of treatment, including night sweats,
oropharyngeal pain, weight gain, right-sided pain under the ribs, and pruritus, with onsets
between SD 28 to 51. The majority resolved within days. Rusfertide was permanently
discontinued on SD 58. The subject cited side effects as the reason for not wanting to continue.
The Investigator assessed pruritus, weight gain, and pain as possibly related to rusfertide; The
Protagonist assessed all events as not related to rusfertide. The Division assessed the events of
night sweats, oropharyngeal pain, weight gain, pain, and pruritis as unlikely related to rusfertide
as the symptoms of pain under the ribs, night sweats, and pruritis all resolved over 2 days. The
symptoms of oropharyngeal pain after rusfertide were unlikely related to rusfertide given the
mechanism of action and limited description of these symptoms.
PTG-300-11-

(b) (6)

: A 72-year-old white female randomized to receive rusfertide (Part 1a).

Discontinuing AEs: Abdominal pain (Grade 1), decreased appetite (Grade 1), and fatigue
(Grade 1): Subject developed abdominal pain and decreased appetite on SD 35, followed by
fatigue on SD 51. No medications were prescribed for any of these events. The subject

Rusfertide (PTG-300)
discontinued study drug on SD 59, with all three events resolving within approximately 2 to
4 weeks after the last dose. Both the Investigator and the Protagonist assessed all events as
possibly related to rusfertide. The Division agrees with the assessment of causality as at least

PTG-300-11-

(b) (6)

: A 76-year-old male randomized to receive rusfertide (Part 1a).

Discontinuing AE: Hematoma (nonserious, Grade 1): Subject developed low back pain during
Part 1a and was treated with intramuscular diclofenac injections. He subsequently developed a
hematoma at the diclofenac injection site requiring ER admission on SD 80, with rusfertide
interrupted at that time. A second minor hematoma episode followed. When the subject returned
to site on SD 113 intending to resume rusfertide, it was withheld due to persistent anemia, and
the subject was ultimately withdrawn on SD 155. Both hematoma episodes were assessed as not
related to rusfertide by both the Investigator and the Protagonist. The Division agrees with the
assessments that the hematomas were unlikely to be related to rusfertide but notes that the
subject’s withdrawal from the study appears at least possibly related to the persistent anemia for
which the drug was being held. The Division assesses this anemia as related to rusfertide.
PTG-300-11-

(b) (6)

: A 33-year-old white male randomized to receive rusfertide (Part 1a).

Discontinuing AE: von Willebrand’s disease (VWD) (Grade 2): Subject experienced
vasovagal syncope on SD 1 and epistaxis on SD 22. VWD was then reported on SD 29, with
notably elevated platelets (1,360×10⁹/L). Rusfertide was withdrawn on SD 33 and the event
remained unresolved at time of discontinuation. The Investigator assessed the event of VWD as
related to rusfertide; the Protagonist as not related to rusfertide. The Division assesses the event
of VWD as related to rusfertide given the well-recognized phenomenon of acquired VWD,
which occurs in the context of very high platelet counts, the increase in platelets observed with
rusfertide, and documented increase in thrombocytosis in this subject.
PTG-300-11-

(b) (6)

: A 37-year-old white male randomized to receive rusfertide (Part 1a).

Discontinuing AE: Pruritus (Grade 2): Subject reported pruritus on SD 110, which was treated
with cetirizine, mepiramine, and clobetasol. Rusfertide was permanently discontinued on
SD 162. Pre-existing bone pain had also worsened significantly during study participation,
contributing to the subject’s voluntary withdrawal. Pruritus remained unresolved at time of
discontinuation. Both the Investigator and the Protagonist assessed pruritus as possibly related to
rusfertide. The Division agrees that the event of pruritus is at least possibly related to rusfertide.
Discontinuations Due to AEs Across the Study
Across all phases of PTG-300-11, AEs led to permanent discontinuation in 15 subjects (5.3%)
who received rusfertide. Discontinuation rates were highest during Part 1a (eight subjects, 5.5%)
and lower in the open-label phases (Part 1b: three subjects, 1.1%; Part 2: four subjects, 1.9%). In
the open-label phases, the most common PTs leading to discontinuation were myelofibrosis and
thrombocytosis, each occurring in two subjects. Individual events included asthenia, flank pain,
ISR, malaise, and rash, each occurring in one subject. The interpretation of discontinuation rates
in the open-label phases is limited by the absence of a placebo comparator and the enrichment of
subjects who tolerated rusfertide during Part 1a.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
7.6.2.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events Leading
to Dose Reduction and Treatment Interruption
Dose Reduction
During Part 1a, seven subjects required dose reduction for TEAEs, six in the rusfertide arm and
one in the placebo arm. Five of the six subjects in the rusfertide arm required dose reduction for
anemia, and one had their dose reduced for the PT of asthenia. The single subject in the placebo
arm had dose reduction for anemia.
During Parts 1b and 2, 34 subjects had their dose reduced at least once while receiving rusfertide.
The primary reason for dose reduction was anemia, occurring at least once in 23 unique subjects.
Additional reasons for dose reduction included fatigue occurring at least once in seven subjects,
asthenia, dizziness, and dyspnea each occurring in at least two subjects. The majority of events
was Grade 1 or 2. All events of anemia were Grade 1 or 2. Grade 3 fatigue leading to dose
reduction occurred in two subjects, Grade 3 asthenia occurred in one subject, and Grade 3
syncope occurred in one subject.
Dose Interruption
During Part 1a, seven subjects required dose interruption for TEAEs, four in the rusfertide arm
and three in the placebo arm. Reasons for dose interruption in the rusfertide arm were diabetic
ketoacidosis (one subject), COVID-19 (one subject), hematoma (one subject), and anemia (one
subject). Reasons for dose interruption in the placebo arm were bullous erysipelas (one subject),
COVID-19 (one subject), and colorectal cancer (one subject).
During Parts 1b and 2, 14 subjects required dose interruption. The most frequent reason for
interruption was anemia in six subjects. Other reasons occurring in only one subject were fatigue,
hemoglobin decreased, injection-site erythema, and injection-site swelling. Three subjects had
Grade 3 events leading to dose interruptions. These Grade 3 events occurred in a single subject:
fatigue, hematuria, urinary tract infection, cholecystitis, anemia, and invasive breast cancer.
7.6.2.6. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Part 1a
The frequency of TEAEs during Part 1a is summarized in Table 28.
The most frequently reported AEs in the rusfertide arm were ISRs, anemia, thrombocytosis,
dyspnea, and GI symptoms. AEs occurring at a notably higher frequency in the rusfertide arm
compared to placebo (RD >5%) included injection-site erythema, injection-site pruritus,
injection-site swelling, anemia, thrombocytosis, and dyspnea; these are discussed in detail in
below.
Injection-site erythema, pruritus, and swelling occurred at higher frequencies in the rusfertide
arm compared to the placebo arm, with RDs that did not cross zero (Table 28). In contrast,
injection-site pain occurred at similar rates in both arms (15.9% rusfertide versus 15.1%
placebo), suggesting that the event of pain associated with an injection is not differentially
associated with rusfertide compared to placebo. Most ISRs were mild to moderate in severity.

79
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
Anemia was reported more frequently in the rusfertide arm (15.2%) than the placebo arm (4.1%;
RD: 11.1%; 95% CI: 4.5%, 18.3%). The frequency of anemia reported as an adverse event
(15.2%) was higher than the frequency of subjects experiencing laboratory-defined Grade 2 or
higher anemia (hemoglobin [Hb] <10 g/dL; 4.1% rusfertide versus 0.7% placebo (Table 32),
indicating that adverse event reporting was driven by investigator assessment of clinical
significance rather than laboratory thresholds alone. Anemia is an expected pharmacologic effect
of rusfertide on erythroid suppression.
Thrombocytosis was reported in 10 subjects (6.9%) in the rusfertide arm and 0 subjects in the
placebo arm (RD: 6.9%; 95% CI: 3.8%, 12.2%). The mechanism and clinical significance of
rusfertide-induced thrombocytosis are examined comprehensively under Safety Review Issue #1
(Thrombocytosis).
Dyspnea was reported in 10 subjects (6.9%) in the rusfertide arm and 2 subjects (1.4%) in the
placebo arm (RD: 5.5%; 95% CI: 1.1%, 11.0%). The lower bound of the CI marginally excludes
zero; however, the clinical significance of this difference remains uncertain given confounding
by underlying PV-related symptoms and rusfertide-associated anemia.
Gastrointestinal AEs, including diarrhea, constipation, and abdominal distension, were reported
in the rusfertide arm at frequencies of 8.3%, 8.3%, and 6.9%, respectively. However, the RDs for
the three events did not exclude zero (Table 28), and these events are not considered
differentially associated with rusfertide compared to placebo based on the available data.
AEs occurring at a notably higher frequency in the rusfertide arm and considered potentially
drug-related are further characterized in the Integrated Safety Assessment, where causality is
(b) (4) are presented.

Across Study PTG-300-11

7.6.2.7. Laboratory Findings
Part 1a
Laboratory assessments were performed at baseline and at regular intervals throughout the
32-week treatment period. Key laboratory findings are summarized below and are presented in
Table 29, Table 30, Table 31, and Table 32 and in Figure 9.
Electrolytes and Renal Function
Potassium elevation (>5.5 mEq/L) occurred in both treatment arms but occurred in more subjects
in the rusfertide arm than the placebo arm. A total of 18 subjects (12.4%) in the rusfertide arm
compared to 5 subjects (3.4%) in the placebo arm (RD: 9.0%, 95% CI: 3.0%, 15.8%) had serum
potassium >5.5 mEq/L. Severe potassium elevation (>6.5 mEq/L) did not occur in either arm.
Serum potassium elevations are common in individuals with MPN due to release from platelets
during in vitro platelet lysis and clot formation. This elevation is considered pseudohyperkalemia
due to its in vitro nature and the absence of clinical evidence of electrolyte imbalance (Delgado
et al. 2022). The higher prevalence of serum potassium elevations in the rusfertide arm compared
with the placebo arm is consistent with the increase in platelet count observed with rusfertide
compared with placebo. No serum potassium elevations in the study were associated with AEs.
Sodium levels were generally well-maintained in both arms, with low sodium (<132 mEq/L)
occurring in only three subjects (2.1%) in the rusfertide arm and one subject (0.7%) in the
placebo arm. Chloride and bicarbonate levels were similar between the two arms. Creatinine
elevation ≥1.5× baseline occurred in one subject (0.7%) in the rusfertide arm and three subjects
(2.1%) in the placebo arm. No subject in either arm experienced a creatinine elevation ≥2× or
≥3× baseline.
Glucose elevation (random glucose ≥200 mg/dL) occurred in five subjects (3.4%) in the
rusfertide arm and five subjects (3.4%) in the placebo arm. Severe hyperglycemia (>250 mg/dL)
occurred in three subjects (2.1%) in each arm. Glucose depression (<70 mg/dL) occurred in 50
81
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
subjects (34.5%) in the rusfertide arm and 51 subjects (34.9%) in the placebo arm, indicating
similar frequencies of hypoglycemia between the two arms. One subject in the rusfertide arm
experienced an SAE of diabetic ketoacidosis. This subject had a medical history of type 1
diabetes mellitus, and the SAE of diabetic ketoacidosis was in the setting of chronic weight loss
and less optimal glucose control and was not attributed to rusfertide exposure.

Liver Biochemistry
ALT elevation >3× ULN occurred in two subjects (1.4%) in the rusfertide arm and zero subjects
in the placebo arm. ALT elevation >5× ULN occurred in one subject (0.7%) in the rusfertide arm
and zero subjects in the placebo arm. Overall, the liver biochemistry findings were not
considered clinically significant. No subject in either arm experienced ALT, AST, or total
bilirubin elevations at the highest threshold (>10× ULN and >3× ULN, respectively), and no
liver biochemistry abnormalities were associated with hepatitis, sustained elevations, or AEs
leading to treatment discontinuation (Table 31).

Hematology
An outlier analysis of hematology analyte values is shown in Table 32.
Hemoglobin
Mean Hb values at baseline were similar between the rusfertide and placebo arms. While a
reduction in Hb is an expected consequence of rusfertide's mechanism of action, excessive
decreases beyond the therapeutic intent may result in anemia, representing a clinically important
safety risk. Based on laboratory assessment, six subjects (4.1%) in the rusfertide arm compared
to one subject (0.7%) in the placebo arm experienced Hb values between 8 and <10 g/dL
(Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events [CTCAE] Grade 2 anemia) at some point
during the 32-week treatment period. No subjects in either arm experienced Hb <8 g/dL
(CTCAE Grade 3 anemia). A detailed analysis of anemia, including AE reporting, dose
modifications, and clinical context, is presented in Section 7.6.3.2.
White Blood Cells
WBC counts were similar at baseline between arms.
An increase in WBC to >10.8×10³ cells/μL occurred in 65 subjects (44.8%) in the rusfertide arm
and 65 subjects (44.5%) in the placebo arm.
A decrease in WBC to <3.5×10³ cells/μL occurred in 14 subjects (9.7%) in the rusfertide arm
and 16 subjects (11.0%) in the placebo arm. WBC increase, neutrophil increase, eosinophil
increase, and lymphocyte decrease occurred at similar frequencies in the rusfertide and placebo
arms and are features of underlying PV.
Platelets
Mean platelet counts were similar between arms at baseline (rusfertide: 414.9×109/L [95% CI:
382.9, 446.9]; placebo: 409.4×109/L [95% CI: 376.9, 442.0]). Figure 8 shows the rapid rise in
platelets by Week 4 in the rusfertide arm which was sustained throughout the study. In contrast,
the platelet count in the placebo arm remained stable until crossover to rusfertide after Week 32;
by Week 36, platelet count rose to a level similar to that in the rusfertide arm.

7.6.2.8. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury,
Study PTG-300-11, Part 1a Randomized
Controlled Period
Figure 9 shows a screening assessment for potential cases of serious drug-induced liver injury.
No potential Hy’s Law cases (concurrent ALT or AST ≥3× ULN and total bilirubin ≥2× ULN
within 30 days) were identified in either arm.
Figure 9. Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening Plot, Safety Population, Study
PTG-300-11 Part 1a RCP

Source: adlb.xpt; software, R.
Duration is 32 weeks.
Each data point represents a subject plotted by their maximum ALT or AST versus their maximum TB value postbaseline.
A potential Hy’s Law case was defined as having any postbaseline total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2× ULN after a postbaseline
ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3× ULN. Those subjects who meet total bilirubin equal to or exceeding 2× ULN criterion within
30 days of the ALT or AST equal to or exceeding 3× ULN criterion are circled in red.
The within 30 days analysis window rule does not apply to cholestasis and temple’s corollary quadrants.
All subjects with at least one postbaseline ALT or AST, bilirubin, and ULN are plotted.
In addition to central laboratory data, local laboratory data may be included in the analysis, if applicable.
For number of subjects in each quadrant, see the table “Subjects in Quadrant of Interest for Potential Hepatocellular DILI Screening
Plot …” and the listing “Listing of Subjects in Hepatocellular Drug-Induced Liver Injury Screening….”
Abbreviations: ALP, alkaline phosphatase; ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase

7.6.2.9. Vital Signs, Study PTG-300-11, Part 1a RCP
There were no clinically meaningful changes in systolic or diastolic blood pressure, pulse rate,
body temperature, or respiratory rate in Study PTG-300-11.

An overview of TEAEs by demographic subgroup is provided in Table 33. AE frequencies did
not appear to differ across age, sex, race, ethnicity, or geographic region.

7.6.2.11. ECG Assessment
Serial 12-lead ECGs were collected at protocol-specified timepoints throughout the study. The
majority of ECG abnormalities observed across both arms were present at baseline or were
assessed as not clinically significant by site investigators, consistent with the background CV
burden expected in an older PV population.
No clinically meaningful differences in QTcF or other ECG interval parameters were observed
between the rusfertide and placebo arms during Part 1a. No subject in the rusfertide arm
developed a new clinically significant arrhythmia or conduction abnormality attributable to study
drug.
Two subjects experienced acute MI events during the study. These events are discussed in the
SAEs section and are further evaluated in the context of rusfertide-associated thrombocytosis in
89
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
Safety Review Issue #1. They are not considered to represent a direct electrophysiologic effect of
rusfertide.

7.6.3. Integrated Assessment of Safety
The integrated assessment of safety evaluated safety across the clinical development program,
which includes 374 unique subjects enrolled across three studies. The TEAE profile of rusfertide
was consistent across all three studies, with ISRs, anemia, and thrombocytosis emerging as the
most clinically relevant treatment-associated findings.
The integrated safety population was evaluated for potential events of special concern; i.e.,
events where even a single occurrence would warrant regulatory action. No such events were
identified, including no cases of anaphylaxis, Stevens-Johnson syndrome, toxic epidermal
necrolysis, drug reaction with eosinophilia and systemic symptoms (DRESS), torsades de
pointes, aplastic anemia, agranulocytosis, thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura, drug-induced
liver injury meeting the Hy’s Law criteria, drug-induced encephalopathy or progressive
multifocal leukoencephalopathy, drug-induced interstitial lung disease, or rhabdomyolysis.
7.6.3.1. Adverse Events of Special Interest
The following AESIs were identified across the rusfertide development program: thrombosis,
thrombocytosis, progression to myelofibrosis, transformation to acute myeloid leukemia, and
malignancy. These AESIs were selected based on the known disease biology of PV, the PD
mechanism of rusfertide, and signals identified during clinical development. For each AESI,
findings from all three studies are considered together, with PTG-300-11 providing the primary
comparative evidence and PTG-300-04 and PTG-300-08 providing supportive context,
particularly with respect to longer-term exposure and specific subpopulations.
Thrombosis
Thrombotic events across the rusfertide clinical development program are shown in Table 34.

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
Thirteen thrombotic events occurred in 12 subjects across the rusfertide clinical development
program: five events in PTG-300-11, six in PTG-300-04/21, and two in PTG-300-08. Arterial
events predominated, comprising 10 of 13 events (5 MIs including 3 MINOCAs, 2 ischemic
strokes, 2 TIAs, and 1 mesenteric artery occlusion), with 3 venous events (2 DVTs and 1 PE).
All events were assessed as not related or unlikely related to rusfertide by the Investigator and
the Applicant.
Thrombosis in PV is multifactorial, driven by the interplay of erythrocytosis, thrombocytosis,
leukocytosis, and the prothrombotic biology of the underlying malignant clone. No single factor
can be held solely responsible for any individual event in this program, and pre-existing vascular
disease, CV risk factors, and inadequate myeloproliferative disease control were present in many
of the affected subjects. Nevertheless, the predominance of arterial over venous events, the
platelet counts at the time of events—several exceeding 1,000×10⁹/L—and the temporal
relationship between rusfertide initiation, rapid platelet rise, and thrombotic events in subjects
with no prior vascular history are difficult to dismiss. Rusfertide’s mechanism of action produces
a rapid, sustained, and dose-dependent increase in platelet count as a direct pharmacodynamic
consequence. The detailed analysis of these interacting mechanisms is presented in Safety
Review Issues #1, #2, and #3.

Rusfertide (PTG-300)
Seven thrombocytosis-related events occurred across the rusfertide clinical development
program: three in PTG-300-11 (one in Part 1a, two in Parts 1b/2), one in PTG-300-04/21, and
three in PTG-300-08. Events ranged from Grade 2 thrombocytosis with acquired VWD and
bleeding, to Grade 4 SAEs of extreme thrombocytosis, hemoptysis, and worsening migraine
requiring hospitalization. Four subjects discontinued rusfertide due to thrombocytosis or its
consequences (Cases 1, 2, 3, and 4); in the remaining three cases rusfertide was continued, in two
instances after cytoreductive escalation with anagrelide.
Causality assessments were discordant between the Investigator and the Applicant in three cases
(Cases 1, 4, and 5). Across all three studies, platelet counts at the time of events ranged from
696×109/L to 1,284×109/L, and all events occurred in the context of rusfertide exposure with
platelet counts substantially above baseline. The clinical consequences of extreme
thrombocytosis in this program included bleeding (acquired VWD, hemoptysis, epistaxis),
microvascular events (worsening migraine), and discontinuation of antiplatelet therapy. The
implications of these findings for risk management and labeling are discussed in Safety Review
Issue #1.

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
All six cases occurred in subjects with baseline splenomegaly, most with markedly elevated
JAK2V617F VAF (≥65%) and long disease duration, all features of higher risk of myelofibrosis
progression. Overall, it is unlikely that disease progression to myelofibrosis was related to
rusfertide exposure given the high-risk features for progression that were at present at baseline.
Transformation to AML
One case of AML transformation was identified across the rusfertide clinical development
program. A 64-year-old high-risk male on HU (JAK2V617F VAF 79.3%, prior thyroid cancer
treated with radioactive iodine) developed AML on SD 253 during Part 2 of PTG-300-04/21,
while receiving placebo, following 28 weeks of prior rusfertide exposure in Part 1. Bone marrow
examination confirmed AML with JAK2V617F VAF increase to 97.3%. The Investigator and
Applicant assessed the event as unlikely related to rusfertide, citing prior thyroid cancer,
radioactive iodine exposure, and high baseline JAK2V617F VAF as independent risk factors for
transformation. Study drug was withdrawn on SD 259.
The Reviewer concurs with this assessment. A single case in a program of 375 subjects with PV,
a disease carrying inherent AML transformation risk, does not constitute a signal attributable to
rusfertide.
Cancer Events
Cancer events were identified in both PTG-300-11 and PTG-300-04/21. In PTG-300-11,
malignancies occurred in 25 subjects (8.6%) across all study phases, with a higher frequency in
the placebo arm during Part 1a (5.5% placebo versus 2.1% rusfertide). Skin cancers were the
most common malignancy (15 subjects; 12 basal cell or squamous cell carcinomas, 3
melanomas); solid tumors including colorectal, hepatobiliary, prostate, renal cell, and breast
cancers were also identified. Many were detected at or near screening or had signs and symptoms
at baseline, suggesting pre-existing disease. Most were treated with local resection without
requiring study discontinuation.
In PTG-300-04/21, 11 subjects (15.7%) were diagnosed with cancer, of whom 3 had a prior
malignancy history. Nonmelanoma skin cancers predominated (9 of 11 cases, 81.8%). Three
subjects developed melanoma; one was assessed as possibly related by the Investigator and
unlikely related by the Applicant. Other malignancies comprised Stage I lung adenocarcinoma,
Stage 0 anal cancer, and Stage I bladder transitional cell carcinoma.
The higher cancer incidence in PTG-300-04/21 compared to PTG-300-11 (15.7% versus 8.6%)
likely reflects a higher baseline cancer risk in that population (prior malignancy history 25.7%
versus 19%), longer cumulative rusfertide exposure (median: 131.4 versus 61.0 weeks), and
differences in ascertainment across studies. The predominance of nonmelanoma skin cancers and
early-stage malignancies identified at or near study entry is consistent with expected PV-related
malignancy risk and pre-existing disease rather than a novel rusfertide-associated oncogenic
signal. No pattern of malignancy type, timing, or frequency emerged across the program that
would suggest a causal relationship with rusfertide.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
7.6.3.2. Adverse Events of Clinical Interest
The following frequently occurring events were identified as likely related to rusfertide and of
clinical interest.
Injection-Site Reactions
ISRs were the most frequently reported AEs across all three studies, occurring in 55.9% of
rusfertide-treated subjects in PTG-300-11 Part 1a, 87.1% in PTG-300-04/21, and 80% in
PTG-300-08. The higher frequencies in PTG-300-04/21 and PTG-300-08 likely reflect the openlabel design, longer exposure, and differing ascertainment compared to the blinded Part 1a of
PTG-300-11. Erythema, pruritus, swelling, and pain were the most common components across
all studies; most events were mild to moderate. Across the overall PV treatment arm, Grade 3
ISRs occurred in only two subjects, none was serious, and only four subjects (1.1%)
discontinued treatment due to ISRs.
Anemia
Anemia was reported as a TEAE in 15.2% of rusfertide-treated subjects in PTG-300-11 Part 1a,
22.9% in PTG-300-04/21, and 15% in PTG-300-08. Across the overall PV treatment arm, 25.6%
of subjects experienced at least one anemia event.
In PTG-300-11 Part 1a, all anemia adverse events were Grade 1 or Grade 2. Across the
integrated safety population, two subjects experienced Grade 3 anemia; no Grade 4 anemia
events occurred. Importantly, anemia was the most common cause of dose reduction across the
program, occurring in 37 subjects (9.9%), and led to drug interruption in 10 subjects; however,
only 1 subject across the entire program discontinued treatment due to anemia.
Thrombocytosis
Thrombocytosis was reported as a TEAE in 8.3% of rusfertide-treated subjects in PTG-300-11
Part 1a, 10.0% in PTG-300-04/21, and 25% in PTG-300-08. Across the overall PV treatment
arm, thrombocytosis or platelet count increased was reported in 41 subjects (10.9%). The higher
frequency in PTG-300-08 is likely attributable in part to the substantially higher starting dose
used in that study (40 mg twice weekly, equivalent to 80 mg/week), which is approximately
twice the median weekly dose in PTG-300-11 and PTG-300-04/21, and which produced a more
rapid and extreme early platelet rise (mean platelet count 484×109/L at baseline rising to
756×109/L at Week 4). Across all three studies, approximately 9.1% of subjects in the overall PV
treatment arm had persistent platelet counts above 1,000×109/L for at least 8 continuous weeks;
no subject reached the protocol stopping rule threshold of persistent platelets above 1,500×109/L
in PTG-300-04/21. Thrombocytosis led to treatment discontinuation in three subjects across the
program. The clinical consequences of rusfertide-associated thrombocytosis are described in the
AESI sections above and examined systematically in Safety Review Issue #1.
7.6.3.3. Adverse Reactions
Potential adverse reactions are defined as TEAEs occurring in greater than 5% of rusfertidetreated subjects and at a higher rate than placebo (≥ +5%) in Study PTG-300-11 Part 1a. This
cutoff results in AEs where the RD excludes zero. Applying this criterion to the frequency table
100
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
of TEAEs (Table 28) identified the following adverse reactions: injection-site reactions, anemia,
thrombocytosis, and dyspnea (Table 37).
The integrated assessment of safety supported a conclusion that the TEAE profile observed
during Part 1a is consistent with that observed across longer exposure periods and in the broader
PV population, supporting the use of the 32-week controlled period as the basis for adverse
reaction identification.
No other adverse reactions were identified based on overall safety analysis or on the mechanism
of action of rusfertide.
