7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
7.7.1. Thrombocytosis
Issue
Rusfertide causes a rapid, sustained, and dose-dependent increase in platelet count as a direct
pharmacodynamic consequence of its mechanism of action. This thrombocytosis carries a dual
clinical risk: arterial thrombosis in a population already predisposed to platelet-mediated
vascular events, and bleeding through acquired VWD at extreme platelet counts.
Background
Rusfertide is a hepcidin mimetic that restricts iron availability and suppresses erythropoiesis.
Platelet elevation is a mechanistically expected consequence of iron restriction, reflecting a shift
in megakaryocyte-erythroid progenitor differentiation toward megakaryocytopoiesis under
conditions of iron deficiency. Elevated platelet counts in MPN are associated with increased
platelet activation, platelet-leukocyte aggregate formation, and endothelial injury, all of which
contribute to arterial thrombotic risk independently of hematocrit (Landolfi et al. 2004; Falanga
et al. 2013). At extreme platelet counts, generally above 1,000×10⁹/L, consumption of large von

101
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
Willebrand factor multimers impairs platelet adhesion and causes paradoxical mucocutaneous
bleeding despite thrombocytosis (Michiels et al. 2006).
Assessment
Rusfertide-Associated Platelet Elevation
Laboratory data from PTG-300-11 demonstrate a rapid and sustained increase in platelet count in
the rusfertide arm, with no corresponding change in the placebo arm. Mean platelet counts were
similar at baseline (rusfertide: 414.9×109/L; placebo: 409.4×109/L). Divergence between the
arms was detectable by Week 2, with mean platelet counts of 493.4×109/L in the rusfertide arm
versus 395.2×109/L in the placebo arm. By Week 4, the mean platelet count in the rusfertide arm
reached 546.7×109/L—a mean increase of approximately 132×109/L (+32%) from baseline—
compared to a mean of 406.5×109/L in the placebo arm (+0.4%). The elevation was sustained
through Week 32, when the mean platelet count in the rusfertide arm was 536.3×109/L compared
to 422.6×109/L in the placebo arm. No subject in either arm exceeded 1,500×109/L. The platelet
elevation pattern in PTG-300-08, where the starting dose was substantially higher than in PTG300-11, was more pronounced, consistent with a dose-dependent PD effect.
Thrombotic Events
Thirteen thrombotic events occurred across the rusfertide clinical program, as detailed in Table
34 of the Integrated Safety Assessment. Ten of the thirteen events were arterial (77%), including
five MIs, two ischemic strokes, two TIAs, and one mesenteric artery occlusion; three were
venous (two DVTs and one PE). The arterial predominance is biologically plausible given the
known role of platelet activation in arterial thrombosis.
The events occurred across a range of SDs and platelet count levels. Several occurred early in
treatment, during the period of most rapid platelet rise, including a NSTEMI on SD 23 (platelet
count rising to 1,029×109/L), a TIA on SD 57 (platelets: 1,107×109/L), and a MINOCA on
SD 70 (platelets: 1,218×109/L). Others occurred later in treatment, including an anterior STEMI
with angiographic left anterior descending thrombus on SD 468 and a mesenteric arterial
occlusion on SD 1,322. Platelet counts at the time of arterial events were 570×109/L to
1,218×109/L. No thrombotic events occurred during placebo exposure in the controlled period of
PTG-300-11.
Thrombocytosis-Related Bleeding and Clinical Events
Seven thrombocytosis-related AEs of clinical significance were identified across all studies, as
detailed in Table 35 of the Integrated Safety Assessment. These events included thrombocytosis
requiring cytoreductive escalation or leading to discontinuation, bleeding complications, and
microvascular symptoms. All seven events occurred at platelet counts between 1,000 and
(b) (4)
1,500×109/L,
(b) (4)

102
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
Conclusion
Rusfertide causes a rapid, sustained, and dose-dependent increase in platelet count, which is a
direct and expected PD consequence of its mechanism of action.
Arterial thrombosis in PV is multifactorial, and rusfertide-associated thrombocytosis is unlikely
to be the sole cause of any individual event. The Applicant assessed all thrombotic and
thrombocytosis-related events as not related or unlikely related to rusfertide, attributing them to
underlying PV or pre-existing vascular disease. However, the arterial predominance of events,
their temporal relationship to rusfertide initiation, and the known pharmacodynamic association
between rusfertide and platelet count elevation collectively support the position that rusfertide
cannot be excluded as a contributing or precipitating factor, notwithstanding the inherently
elevated thrombotic risk associated with PV.
The observed bleeding complications are consistent with the known biology of extreme
thrombocytosis and acquired VWD.
(b) (4)

FDA concludes that thrombocytosis requires a Warnings and Precautions designation in the
rusfertide labeling, with language that accurately characterizes the magnitude and time course of
platelet elevation, provides actionable monitoring recommendations calibrated to the early onset
of the effect, and establishes intervention thresholds grounded in the observed clinical event data.

7.7.2. Leukocytosis
Issue
Rusfertide controls HCT through selective suppression of erythropoiesis but provides no
myeloproliferative control over WBC or the underlying malignant clone and may exacerbate
thrombocytosis (see Safety Review Issue #1). CRT provides broad myelosuppressive control that
targets more than HCT control. The PTG-300-11 protocol permitted CRT dose reductions while
prohibiting dose increases, confounding the assessment of myeloproliferative disease control
during rusfertide treatment.
Background
WBC elevation is an independent thrombotic risk factor in PV, operating separately from HCT.
In the CYTO-PV study3, subjects with WBC >11×109/L experienced significantly more
thrombotic events even when HCT was maintained below 45% (Barbui et al. 2015)and REVEAL
registry data4 demonstrated a two-fold increase in thrombotic risk with WBC >10×109/L (Gerds
3

The CYTO-PV was a randomized controlled study in subjects with PV and demonstrated that maintaining an HCT
<45% significantly reduced the rate of CV death and major thrombotic events compared to a less stringent target of
45% to 50%, establishing the 45% HCT threshold as the standard-of-care target in PV management.
4
The REVEAL registry was a large prospective observational study in the United States enrolling over 2,500
patients with PV. The registry documented the real-world burden of PV, including high rates of thrombotic events,
disease-related symptoms, and healthcare resource utilization, and highlighted that a substantial proportion of
patients remained inadequately controlled despite current therapies (including HU).

103
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
et al. 2024). HU suppresses all three hematopoietic lineages; upon dose reduction or
discontinuation, myelosuppressive control reverses rapidly and cell counts rebound toward
underlying disease levels. Ruxolitinib, a JAK1/2 inhibitor, provides effective WBC and symptom
control in HU-resistant or intolerant patients. Ropeginterferon alfa-2b offers durable WBC
control and, uniquely among approved PV therapies, can achieve sustained reductions in WBC,
HCT, platelets, and JAK2V617F VAF over time.
Assessment
In response to FDA’s first Information Request (received May 14, 2026), the Applicant provided
detailed analyses of platelet, WBC, and JAK2V617F VAF trajectories across the entire study
population and stratified by background therapy (PHL only, HU, IFN, or ruxolitinib). During
Part 1a (Weeks 0 to 32), rusfertide-treated subjects receiving concurrent HU showed a faster
WBC rise than placebo-treated patients in the same subgroup. No divergence between arms was
observed in the No-CRT or IFN subgroup, confirming that rusfertide does not directly drive
WBC elevation. At Week 32, HU placebo subjects crossing over to rusfertide showed an
acceleration in WBC trajectory matching the rusfertide arm, implicating an interaction between
rusfertide and HU management rather than natural disease progression.
In response to FDA’s second Information Request (received June 11, 2026), the Applicant
provided analyses examining the relationship between HU dose changes and WBC/JAK2V617F
VAF trajectories. During Part 1a, subjects in the rusfertide arm reduced or discontinued HU at
2.8-fold the rate of placebo-treated subjects (25% versus 9%). Among subjects receiving
concurrent HU who underwent CRT dose reductions, the median percentage decrease from
baseline in weekly HU dose was greater in the rusfertide arm (−40.8%) compared with the
placebo arm (−22.2%).
Reductions in HU dosing were preceded by declining HCT levels and were followed by
increases in platelet and WBC counts. Mean WBC at the time of HU dose reduction or
discontinuation was 8.4×109/L; 16 weeks after dose reduction or discontinuation, mean WBC
increased to 10.7×109/L.
Once CRT dose increases and initiation were permitted in Part 2 of PTG-300-11, 35 of 266
subjects (13.2%) had CRT dose increases or started new CRT. Among the 19 subjects who were
receiving PHL only at baseline, 11 initiated HU, 4 initiated IFN, and 2 initiated RUX. An
additional nine subjects increased their ongoing CRT dose, and seven subjects switched to a
different CRT agent. The most commonly documented reasons for CRT intensification were
cytoreduction due to leukocytosis and/or thrombocytosis or management of PV per standard of
care. Following CRT intensification, mean WBC decreased from 13.6×109/L at the time of CRT
increase or initiation to 10.5×109/L at 16 weeks after intervention, and mean platelet count
decreased from 812.8×109/L to 657.5×109/L over the same period (IR Response, June 11, 2026).
Conclusion
The Applicant concluded in its IR response (June 2026) that WBC elevation reflects CRT
withdrawal effects independent of rusfertide and that no rusfertide-specific labeling is warranted.
FDA does not dispute that rusfertide has no direct pharmacologic effect on WBC. However, the
clinical management pattern it may trigger—CRT dose reduction in response to rusfertideinduced anemia—could result in loss of broad myelosuppressive control and progressive WBC
104
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
elevation in CRT subgroups. Because WBC elevation is an independent thrombotic risk factor in
PV operating separately from HCT, the net effect on thrombotic risk of achieving HCT control
while losing WBC control is uncertain and potentially unfavorable.
This concern is addressed in labeling through a Warnings and Precautions advising that increases
in platelet count should be monitored and that CRT may need to be initiated, increased, or the
rusfertide dose reduced or discontinued in response. The interaction between rusfertide and
concurrent CRT management, including the pattern of protocol-driven CRT dose reductions and
their hematologic consequences, will be further characterized through a postmarketing
requirement and enhanced pharmacovigilance as part of the completed PTG-300-11 study, which
will provide a full accounting of thrombotic events and CRT dosing across the full study
duration.

7.7.3. JAK2V617F VAF
Issue
Although rusfertide has no direct effect on JAK2V617F VAF, the protocol-driven CRT dose
reductions described in Safety Issue #2 may indirectly promote clonal expansion by relieving
cytoreductive pressure on the malignant clone. JAK2V617F VAF was collected as an
exploratory endpoint in PTG-300-11 but was not available to investigators in real time to guide
treatment decisions.
Background
JAK2 V617F VAF reflects the proportion of hematopoietic cells carrying the driver mutation
and serves as a marker of clonal burden in PV. Higher JAK2V617F VAF is associated with
higher WBC counts, increased thrombotic risk, higher phlebotomy requirements and greater
likelihood of disease progression to myelofibrosis or acute myeloid leukemia (Moliterno et al.
2023). HU, ruxolitinib, and ropeginterferon alfa-2b can each reduce JAK2V617F VAF; effective
cytoreductive control and clonal suppression appear to be linked, as demonstrated in recent
clinical studies (Gisslinger et al. 2020; Harrison et al. 2023). Rusfertide does not target the
mutant clone or JAK2 activation directly and would not be expected to suppress clonal
expansion. The concern is that by indirectly promoting CRT dose reductions, rusfertide may
remove the cytoreductive pressure that was restraining the clone.
Assessment
FDA subgroup analysis of JAK2V617F VAF trajectories in PTG-300-11 revealed a pattern
mirroring that described for WBC in Safety Review Issue #2. The mean JAK2V617F VAF
increase in the HU-rusfertide arm exceeded that in the HU-placebo arm by 8.6 percentage points
over the 32-week randomized period. No divergence in JAK2V617F VAF was observed between
treatment arms in the No CRT or interferon subgroups—confirming that rusfertide has no direct
effect on JAK2 V617F VAF. At Week 32, HU placebo subjects crossing over to rusfertide
showed a sharp upward shift in JAK2V617F VAF coinciding precisely with rusfertide initiation.
This is consistent with the protocol-driven HU dose reduction pattern in Safety Review Issue #2,
in which rusfertide-treated subjects reduced or discontinued HU at 2.8-fold the rate of placebo-

105
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
treated subjects (25% versus 9%), with a median dose reduction of −40.8% versus −22.2% (IR
Response, June 2026).
Three sentinel cases of marked JAK2V617F VAF increases are described in the Integrated
Safety Assessment (Table 34; Cases 6, 9, and 10), all occurring in the HU subgroup in the
context of HU dose reduction or inadequate dosing, and all associated with serious arterial
thrombotic events. The largest JAK2V617F VAF increase in the entire rusfertide development
program occurred in Case 10 (mesenteric arterial occlusion, SD 1,322), in which JAK2V617F
VAF increased from 22.1% to 82.0% (+59.9 percentage points) over approximately 3.6 years
concurrent with HU dose reduction and rising WBC. In Case 9 (ischemic stroke, SD 670), JAK2
V617F VAF increased from 22.6% to 58.2% (+35.6 percentage points) despite nominally stable
HU dosing, suggesting inadequate cytoreductive control. In Case 6 (anterior STEMI, SD 468),
JAK2V617F VAF increased from undetectable to 23.7%, representing emergence of a detectable
clone.
Conclusion
The Applicant concluded that JAK2V617F VAF changes in the HU subgroup reflect expected
HU withdrawal effects independent of rusfertide. FDA acknowledges that rusfertide has no
direct effect on JAK2V617F VAF and that the subgroup analyses are exploratory. However, as
discussed in Safety Review Issue #2, the same protocol-driven mechanism that produced WBC
elevation also produced JAK2V617F VAF increase, and the two findings are biologically
coherent. Loss of cytoreductive pressure allows the malignant clone to expand relative to
residual wild-type clone, driving both leukocytosis and increasing VAF simultaneously.
JAK2V617F VAF increases in the HU subgroup represents a biological marker of the same loss
of myeloproliferative control described in Safety Review Issue #2, occurring potentially through
the same protocol-driven mechanism of CRT dose reduction. At the population level,
JAK2V617F VAF in the HU-rusfertide arm exceeded that in the HU-placebo arm by
8.6 percentage points over 32 weeks, with no corresponding divergence in the No CRT
subgroups. Three sentinel cases of substantial clonal expansion associated with life-threatening
arterial thrombosis illustrate the potential clinical consequence at the individual patient level.
Because JAK2V617F VAF testing is not universally available and interpretive thresholds are not
established in clinical practice, a specific VAF monitoring recommendation is not proposed for
labeling. The pattern of clonal expansion in the context of CRT dose reductions—and its
potential association with serious arterial thrombotic events—will be further characterized
through the PMR and enhanced pharmacovigilance described in Safety Review Issue #2. This
will provide a full accounting of JAK2V617F VAF trajectories, CRT dosing, and thrombotic
events across the completed PTG-300-11 study.

Rusfertide (PTG-300)

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
Renal Impairment
The effect of renal impairment on the PK of rusfertide was evaluated in a dedicated clinical study
(PTG-300-18) in subjects with severe renal impairment (eGFR <30 mL/min and not on dialysis)
compared to healthy subjects (eGFR >90 mL/min) following a single SC dose of rusfertide
19 mg LPF. Compared to healthy subjects, rusfertide AUCinf was 12% higher and Cmax was 36%
higher in subjects with severe renal impairment (geometric LS mean ratios of 1.12 [90% CI:
0.94, 1.34] and 1.36 [90% CI: 1.05, 1.76], respectively). The modest increase in rusfertide
exposure is consistent with the finding that renal clearance is not a major elimination pathway
for rusfertide, as urinary concentrations were below the limit of detection following SC
administration. These findings were supported by the population PK analysis, which did not
identify eGFR as a statistically significant covariate on rusfertide PK across the eGFR range of
6.42 to 167 mL/min.
For metabolite M9, AUCinf was 93% higher in subjects with severe renal impairment compared
to healthy subjects. The mean ratio of M9 AUCinf to total AUCinf (calculated as AUCinf values
for rusfertide + M4 + M9) was 29.9% for healthy subjects and 42.3% for severe renal
impairment subjects; however, this is not considered clinically meaningful given the
substantially lower potency of M9 relative to rusfertide (14% of rusfertide potency). No
meaningful differences in M4 exposure were observed (AUCinf was 11% lower and Cmax was
24% lower).
Therefore, dose adjustment of rusfertide in patients with renal impairment is not warranted.
Hepatic Impairment
The effect of hepatic impairment on the PK of rusfertide was evaluated in a dedicated clinical
study (PTG-300-18) in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment (Child–Pugh B, N=8) and
matched healthy subjects (N=8) receiving a single SC dose of rusfertide 19 mg LPF. Compared
to healthy subjects, the rusfertide AUCinf decreased 34% and the Cmax decreased 23% in subjects
with moderate hepatic impairment (geometric LSM ratios of 0.66 [90% CI: 0.52, 0.84] and 0.77
[90% CI: 0.57, 1.05], respectively).
As rusfertide is a synthetic peptide that undergoes proteolysis rather than cytochrome P450
(CYP)-mediated hepatic metabolism, the decrease in rusfertide exposure observed in moderate
hepatic impairment may be related to reduced absorption rather than altered elimination, as
evidenced by the comparable half-lives across arms. Population PK analysis confirmed that
hepatic function-related covariates (ALT, AST, albumin, and bilirubin) were not statistically
significant covariates on rusfertide PK. Furthermore, rusfertide dosing is individually titrated
based on a patient’s HCT response, the decrease in exposure observed in patients with hepatic
impairment can be managed via dose titration.
For metabolite M4, AUCinf was 13% lower and Cmax was 44% lower in subjects with moderate
hepatic impairment compared to healthy subjects. For metabolite M9, AUCinf was 11% higher
and Cmax was 24% higher in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment compared to healthy
107
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0–modified (09/05/25)
Reference ID: 5861026

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
subjects. Given that both metabolites are formed through non-CYP pathways and have lower
potencies than the parent drug (65% and 14% of rusfertide potency for M4 and M9,
respectively), modest changes in their individual exposures are of limited clinical relevance.
Therefore, dose adjustment of rusfertide in patients with mild to moderate hepatic impairment is
not warranted. Rusfertide has not been studied in patients with severe hepatic impairment
(Child–Pugh C).

8.2. Extrinsic Factors
No dedicated clinical drug–drug interaction (DDI) studies were conducted for rusfertide. As a
synthetic peptide, rusfertide is primarily metabolized by proteolysis rather than CYP-mediated
pathways, PK-based DDI is unlikely to occur. This is supported by in vitro evidence that
rusfertide and its major metabolites (M4 and M9) did not inhibit or induce major CYP enzymes
and were not substrates or inhibitors of human drug transporters at clinically relevant
concentrations. Accordingly, no clinically meaningful PK-based DDIs are expected for
rusfertide.

8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
Rusfertide was granted ODD for the treatment of PV on June 12, 2020 (ODD# DRU-20197158). Therefore, this application is exempt from the requirements of the Pediatric Research
Equity Act. In addition, the Applicant filed a request for a full waiver of the requirement for
pediatric studies since PV is recognized as an adult-related condition that rarely occurs in the
pediatric population, making clinical studies impossible or highly impractical. The division
agreed with the waiver for the initial pediatric study plan in a letter to the Applicant dated
September 27, 2021.

8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of
Reproductive Potential
The reproductive and developmental toxicity of rusfertide was evaluated in fertility an early
embryonic development (FEED) studies in male and female rats and embryofetal development
studies in pregnant rats and rabbits. Rusfertide poses a potentially significant risk of embryofetal
harm based on findings from animal embryofetal development studies in rats and rabbits. The
mechanism of embryofetal toxicity may be related to iron restriction during critical periods of
organogenesis, as endogenous hepcidin levels normally decrease during pregnancy to facilitate
maximal iron transport to the developing fetus.
Final edited language at the label and additional information supporting edits are provided in
Table 38.

9. Product Quality
Approval
The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality review team has assessed NDA 220605 with respect to
chemistry, manufacturing, and controls and has determined that the quality information in the
NDA meets all applicable standards to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it
purports to have. As such, the Office of Pharmaceutical Quality recommends approval of this
NDA from a quality perspective. A shelf life of 24 months is granted for the drug product when
stored at 20°C to 25°C. The drug product is marketed as a single-dose kit including both a
diluent PFS and lyophilized powder in vials, which must be reconstituted using the diluent prior
to injection. Given the close spacing of the doses to be administered when reconstituted
according to the approved labeling, reviewers of any future supplemental NDAs should assess
risks associated with dose overlap that may accompany a change in the drug product or its
labeling.

9.1. Device or Combination Product
Considerations
The drug is marketed in a kit containing both LP in vials and a diluent solution in a PFS. As
such, the drug product is designated a combination product. The diluent PFS was reviewed as
part of the drug product review, and no separate consult to Center for Devices and Radiological
Health was required.

10. Human Subjects Protections/Clinical Site
and Other Good Clinical Practice
Inspections/Financial Disclosure Review
Studies PTG-300-04 and PTG-300-11 were conducted under International Council on Harmonisation
Good Clinical Practices guidelines. A signed written informed consent form was required to enroll in
any of the studies. The studies were reviewed and monitored by an institutional review board,
research ethics board, or an independent ethics committee, depending on the country in which the
study site was located.
A clinical site inspection review was completed by Dr. Anthony Orenica in the Office of Scientific
Investigations and is summarized in Section 15. The results of the clinical site inspections and
Applicant inspection support the conclusion that the studies were conducted adequately, and that the
data generated support the proposed indication. Review of the financial disclosures did not raise any
concerns about the validity or reliability of the data. A Financial Disclosure summary review is
located in Section 18.

III. Additional Information
12. Summary of Regulatory History
Rusfertide (PTG-300) was granted Orphan Drug Designation for the treatment of PV on June 12,
2020.
On August 20, 2020, an initial investigational new drug (IND) submission was received for the
development of PTG-300 for the treatment of PV with Protocol PTG-300-04.
Rusfertide was granted Fast Track Designation for the treatment of PV on November 30, 2020.
A Type B, End-of-Phase 2 meeting was held on March 9, 2021, to discuss the results of Study
PTG-300-04 and the design of the pivotal study, PTG-300-11.
A preliminary Breakthrough Therapy Designation Request (BTDR) Advice teleconference was
held with the Agency on March 26, 2021. The Agency stated it was appropriate for the Applicant
to formally submit a BTDR.
The formal BTDR submission was received on April 9, 2021, and the Agency granted rusfertide
BTD on June 2, 2021, for the treatment of patients with PV for the reduction of erythrocytosis
who do not require further treatment for thrombocytosis and/or leukocytosis.
A Type B Initial Comprehensive BTD meeting was held on September 7, 2021, to discuss the
overall rusfertide development plan.
A Type B Other meeting was held on September 17, 2021, to discuss the Applicant’s revised
phase 3 study plan.
(b) (4) and IND 152414 were placed on full clinical hold due to
On September 17, 2021, IND
positive carcinogenic outcomes identified in the RasH2 transgenic study and human cancer
diagnoses during Study PTG-300-04. The full clinical hold was removed on October 8, 2021.

An Initial Pediatric Study Plan (iPSP) for rusfertide was submitted on May 6, 2021, to request
full waiver of pediatric studies. An Agreed iPSP was issued on September 27, 2021.
On March 7, 2022, an Intent to Rescind Breakthrough Therapy Designation was issued to
IND 152414 due to multiple reported malignancies and because the risk-benefit profile appeared
unfavorable.
On April 11, 2022, the Applicant submitted a Type B meeting request in response to the Intent to
Rescind Breakthrough Therapy Designation letter, and Preliminary Meeting Comments were
issued on June 2, 2022. The Applicant cancelled the meeting and issued a request to withdraw
BTD on June 3, 2022.
A preliminary BTDR Advice meeting was held on April 30, 2025. The Agency needed more
information from the Applicant to decide if it was appropriate for a formal submission. An IR to
obtain additional clinical information was issued to the Applicant on May 2, 2025. The Applicant
submitted the IR response, and the Agency decided it was appropriate for the Applicant to
submit a formal BTDR. The formal BTDR submission was received on June 23, 2025.

17. Postmarketing Requirements and
Commitments
The following PMR will be issued to assess the known serious risk of worsening thrombocytosis,
as well as the unexpected serious risks of worsening thrombosis and thromboembolic events,
hematologic transformation to myelofibrosis or acute leukemia, and malignancy:
Conduct a study to characterize the long-term safety of rusfertide in patients with
polycythemia vera based on a pooled analyses of data from studies PTG-300-11
(VERIFY) and PTG-300-04 (REVIVE)/PTG-300-21 (THRIVE). Provide at least 3 years
of follow-up for enrolled subjects treated with the rusfertide dose titration regimen.
Safety outcomes of interest include thrombocytosis, thromboembolic events, hematologic
transformation to myelofibrosis or acute myeloid leukemia, JAK2 allele burden, and
cytoreductive therapy use.
Include a summary of the major safety findings for all patients and submit datasets with
the final clinical study report submission.
Final Report Submission:

June 2029

The following 506B (reportable) postmarketing commitments (PMCs) will be issued:
PMC 1: Complete Study PTG-300-11 (VERIFY): A Phase 3 Study of the Hepcidin
Mimetic Rusfertide (PTG-300) in Patients with Polycythemia Vera.
Study/Trial Completion:

August 2028

Final Report Submission:

December 2028

PMC 2: Complete Study PTG-300-21, an ongoing multicenter, open-label, phase 3 study
to assess long-term safety, tolerability, and efficacy of treatment with rusfertide.
Trial Completion:

January 2027

Final Report Submission:

April 2027

Rusfertide (PTG-300)
To further characterize the long-term safety profile of rusfertide, the following enhanced
pharmacovigilance activities will be requested:
We request that for Mimrylo you submit all serious domestic and foreign cases of the
following events as though they were 15-day Alert reports (which are described under
21 CFR [Code of Federal Regulations] 314.80(c)(1)) through the fifth year following
initial U.S. approval:
•

New onset or worsening thrombocytosis

•

Hematologic transformation to myelofibrosis or acute myeloid leukemia

•

Arterial or venous thromboembolic events

•

Janus kinase 2 allele burden increase

We request that you investigate all such reports of serious events as though they were
subject to 21 CFR 314.80(c)(1)(ii). When you receive new information for a previously
reported case, submit a follow-up ICSR using the same unique case identification number
(as described in 21 CFR 314.80(f)(5)(viii)).
Include a narrative summary including analyses of the above events reported with
Mimrylo as part of your required periodic safety reports (e.g., periodic adverse drug
experience report (PADER) required under 21 CFR 314.80(c)(2)), quarterly during the
first 3 years postapproval and annually thereafter, through the fifth year following initial
U.S. approval date.
Your analyses should include interval and cumulative data from the date of approval of
Mimrylo including cases from your global safety, and clinical trials databases. Your
analyses should provide an assessment of causality for each case, and document the
following, where available:
•

Indication for use (including all labeled and off-label use)

•

Temporal association

•

Dose and duration of therapy

•

Associated signs and symptoms

•

Confounders and underlying risk factors

•

Concomitant medications, including cytoreductive therapy use and dose

•

Treatment given for the event

•

Outcome

•

Dechallenge/rechallenge information

Rusfertide (PTG-300)

Erythrocytosis in Polycythemia Vera

Reference ID: 5867638

NDA 220605

Nonmalignant Hematology
Marieli Gonzalez-Cotto, PhD
Pedro DelValle, PhD, Fellow ATS/ Bo Lee, PhD
Office of Cardiology, Hematology,
Endocrinology and Nephrology (OCHEN)
C. Lee Elmore, PhD
Carleveva Thompson, MS

endogenous ferroportin in 0.5% human serum albumin provided evidence of
concentration-dependent ferroportin internalization and degradation with an IC50 of 38.1
nM.
•

Rusfertide also exhibited dose-dependent inhibition of iron homeostasis with consistent
effects on serum iron and transferrin saturation in mice, rats and monkeys ranging from
25.6% to 84% reduction in iron levels and up to 77% reduction in transferrin saturation.
Plasma iron lowering effects were sustained across species, persisting for up to 30
hours in mice, 32 hours in rats and 72 hours in cynomolgus monkeys after single SC
administration.

•

In a hepcidin-deficient mouse model of hereditary hemochromatosis (HFE2-/- mice),
rusfertide at SC doses 1 to 7.5 mg/kg Q2D for 2 to 4 weeks prevented iron accumulation
in the liver, produced sustained lowering of serum iron (from ~60-66 µM to ~8-17 µM)
and transferrin saturation (from ~93-95% to 7-19%), and was associated with dosedependent decreases in non-heme iron concentrations in liver, kidney, and pancreas
with concomitant increases in splenic iron concentrations, suggestive of iron routing for
sequestration.

•

The major human metabolites M4 and M9 representing 15% and 31% of total exposure,
respectively, are pharmacologically active. M4, a truncated proteolytic product of
rusfertide through sequential trypsin-like protease activity, exhibits similar potency to
rusfertide (IC50 = 58.3 nM vs. 38.1 nM for rusfertide). M9, a structurally intact oxidative
metabolite bearing a 14-hydroxy modification on the palmitoyl side chain, exhibits
approximately ~7.2-fold lower potency (IC50 = 276 nM) and likely limited contribution to
the overall drug activity despite its substantial exposure.

Overall, the above findings suggest that rusfertide is a potent hepcidin mimetic with the potential
to reduce systemic iron availability and decrease RBC production, thus supporting the
therapeutic rationale for its development as a possible treatment for PV.

3

Rusfertide and hepcidin each induced internalization of ferroportin at EC50

Rusfertide and its metabolites M1, M4 and M9 induced ferroportin
internalization in T47D human epithelial tumor cells that endogenously
express ferroportin when assayed in human serum albumin. The results
establish the internalization-inducing capacity of rusfertide and metabolites
M1, M4, and M9 at differentiated potencies. Compared to rusfertide, M1 is
most potent (2.8×) and M4 and M9 are 35% and 83% less potent.

PTG-C-031/
Serum Iron Levels in
C57BL/6 Mice
Following Single
Subcutaneous

Subcutaneous dosing of rusfertide and hepcidin or hepcidin analog at
equimolar concentrations of 1 μmol/kg showed that rusfertide significantly
outperformed native hepcidin and the hepcidin analog across all primary
pharmacological metrics, exhibiting a nearly two-fold increase in serum iron
reduction (35-93% versus 26-50%), a 25% extension in effect duration (up to
30 hours versus 24 hours), and superior potency at equimolar dosing.

Injection of Rusfertide

Serum Iron vs. Time after single SC dosing in Mice

PTG-C-022/
Evaluation of Serum
Iron as a Biomarker
for the Effect of Single
Rusfertide Dosing to
Wild-Type Mice

Rusfertide produced a dose-dependent serum iron reduction ranging from
25.6% at the lowest single dose of 0.024 mg/kg to 80.3% at the highest single
dose of 0.976 mg/kg at 4 hours post-dose. Rusfertide doses correspond to
serum concentrations of 56.8 to 5210 ng/mL, respectively.

PTG-C-012/
Pharmacokinetics and
Pharmacodynamics of
Rusfertide Following
Single Subcutaneous
Dosing to C57BL/6
Mice

Rusfertide SC doses of 0.488 mg/kg (0.2 μmol/kg) or 0.976 mg/kg (0.4
μmol/kg) in female wild-type mice suppressed serum iron, with measurable
decreases observed as early as 2 hours post-dose and sustained through 10or 24-hours post-dose, respectively. Serum iron nadir was achieved at 7
hours post-dose, with maximum reductions of 72.8% and 83.1% relative to
vehicle control at the 0.2 and 0.4 μmol/kg dose levels, respectively. Critically,
rusfertide plasma concentrations at 24 hours post-dose exceeded the
established EC50 of 11.5 ng/mL (4.71 nM) (as determined in the HEK–FPN

Serum Iron Concentration vs. Rusfertide Dose in WT mice

5

Reference ID: 5867638

cell-based assay) at both dose levels, confirming sustained pharmacologically
active exposure throughout the dosing interval.
Rusfertide PD/PK after Single Dosing in WT mice

PTG-C-017/
Oral Iron Challenge in
Healthy Wild-Type
C57BL/6 Mice

Single SC administration of rusfertide to female wild-type mice at 0.732 mg/kg
(0.3 μmol/kg), followed by oral gavage challenge with ferrous sulfate (2
mg/kg) at 3 hours post-dose, produced statistically significant reductions in
both serum iron concentrations and transferrin saturation (TSAT) relative to
vehicle-treated controls at 4 hours post-SC dosing (1-hour after iron
challenge). These suppressive effects were sustained through 10 hours postdose providing evidence that SC dosing of rusfertide can counteract elevated
iron and TSAT levels in mice with increased iron exposure.
TSAT levels after Rusfertide in Iron-Challenged Mice

PTG-C-076/
Minimum Efficacious
Subcutaneous
Rusfertide Dose in a
Hemochromatosis

Rusfertide administered SC at doses of 1 to 7.5 mg/kg every other day for 24
days in HFE2-/- mice, a hepcidin-deficient model of hereditary
hemochromatosis, demonstrated efficacy through dose-dependent
redistribution of non-heme iron from liver, kidney, and pancreas to the spleen.

6

Reference ID: 5867638

Mouse Model After 5
Weeks of Iron Loading

Rusfertide significantly decreases in serum iron at 5 and 7.5 mg/kg,
transferrin saturation (TSAT) at all doses, serum ferritin at all doses, and total
iron-binding capacity (TIBC) at 7.5 mg/kg only. These changes occurred
concomitantly with decreases in tissue iron concentrations in liver, kidney,
and pancreas and dose-dependent increases in splenic iron.
Following repeat dosing (12 doses Q2D), rusfertide exposure increased
greater than dose-proportionally. At 48 hours post-final dose, TSAT
decreased by 12.9% at 5 mg/kg and 79.1% at 7.5 mg/kg. This steep-dose
response demonstrates that the 7.5 mg/kg dose substantially exceeded the
target 30-45% reduction threshold while the 5 mg/kg dose fell below it.
These findings underscore the importance of dose selection to achieve target
pharmacodynamics, demonstrating that rusfertide prevents parenchymal iron
accumulation and redistributes iron in a model of primary iron overload.
PD effects after Repeat Dosing of Rusfertide

7

Reference ID: 5867638

PTG-C-028/
Pharmacodynamic
and Pharmacokinetic
Study of a Hepcidin
Mimetic (Rusfertide) in
Cynomolgus Monkeys
Following
Administration of a
Single Subcutaneous
Dose

Single SC doses of rusfertide to cynomolgus monkeys at 0.24, 0.61, or 0.97
mg/kg (equivalent to 0.1, 0.25, and 0.4 μmol/kg, respectively) produced dosedependent decreases in serum iron (67-80%) and TSAT (75-77%) with
maximal effects observed at 24 hours which returned to basal after 48 hours
post-dose.
The minimum effective dose was 0.61 mg/kg, which produced similar serum
iron (80%) and TSAT (77%) reductions compared to the higher dose of 0.97
mg/kg. Rusfertide systemic exposure (Cmax, AUC0-t) increased doseproportionally from 0.24 to 0.61 mg/kg, but less than dose-proportionally from
0.61 to 0.97 mg/kg with an apparent elimination half- life (t1/2) of 10.4 or 9.64
hours, respectively.
PD effects after Single SC dosing in Monkeys

PTG-C-030/
Pharmacodynamic
Study of a Hepcidin
Mimetic (Rusfertide) in
the Cynomolgus
Monkey Following
Administration of a
Single Subcutaneous
Dose

Single SC administration of rusfertide to healthy male cynomolgus monkeys
at 0.97 mg/kg (0.4 μmol/kg), followed by oral challenge with ferrous sulfate at
2 mg/kg 12 hours post-dose, produced statistically significant reductions in
both serum iron concentrations and TSAT relative to vehicle-treated controls
at 13 hours post-SC dosing (1-hour after iron challenge). Both serum iron and
TSAT levels were decreased with or without the iron challenge, and the
effects continued through the latest time point assessed, 24 hours post-SC
dose, providing evidence that SC dosing of rusfertide can counteract elevated
iron and TSAT levels in cynomolgus monkeys with increased iron exposure.

8

Reference ID: 5867638

PD effects after Single SC dosing followed by Oral Iron Challenge in Monkeys

Secondary Pharmacology
Rusfertide was evaluated for off-target activity in a panel of 38 human receptors, enzymes, and
transporters at concentrations up to 10 µM (Study 100026794). At 10 µM, rusfertide decreased
binding of control ligands by >50% for seven targets. IC50 values were determined for two
receptors: the CCK1 receptor (IC50=0.28 µM) and the δ2 opioid receptor (IC50=2.4 µM),
representing exposure multiples of 0.52-fold and 4.5-fold, respectively, over the extrapolated
human Cmax of 534 nM at the maximum recommended dose (MRHD) of 120 mg (PTG-30016). No CNS or opioid-related adverse events consistent with δ2 opioid receptor engagement
were identified. The remaining targets showed IC50 values >1 µM or were not calculable,
providing exposure multiples ≥118-fold over the therapeutic Cmax.
The δ2 opioid receptor represents the more notable finding given the relatively narrow 4.5-fold
margin. However, repeat-dose studies in rats at doses up to 300 mg/kg showed no behavioral,
neurological, or autonomic changes consistent with opioid receptor engagement. CCK1
modulation is associated with gastrointestinal effects and although a minimally higher incidence
of cecal mucosal mononuclear cell infiltrate at ≥3 mg/kg was observed in the 13-week rat study
(PTG-C-036) this finding was of minimal severity and not reproduced in longer-duration studies.

Safety Pharmacology
Rusfertide was evaluated in a comprehensive safety pharmacology program including in vitro
hERG assessment and in vivo cardiovascular, respiratory, and central nervous system studies.
In vitro hERG assessment showed IC50 >30 µM (>56-fold margin), and in vivo studies in
conscious telemetered monkeys showed no effects on ECG parameters or blood pressure at
doses up to 10 mg/kg (8.4-fold exposure margin), confirmed in chronic toxicity studies up to 39
weeks. In rats, rusfertide produced no effects on respiratory function or neurobehavioral
parameters at doses up to 25 mg/kg (20-fold exposure margins, each).

Pharmacokinetics/ ADME/Toxicokinetics
Absorption
Rusfertide is a synthetic cyclic peptide hepcidin mimetic containing natural amino acids, a single
disulfide bond, and a glutamic acid linker to a palmitic acid moiety, isolated as an acetate salt.
The physicochemical properties of rusfertide include high plasma protein binding (>99% across
rat, dog, monkey and human) and pH-dependent solubility (≥100 mg/mL in saline solution at pH
5.4).
The intended clinical route of SC injection administration for rusfertide was used in nonclinical
studies. Multiple formulations of rusfertide were evaluated throughout the nonclinical
development program. The majority of early toxicology studies utilized 0.9% sodium chloride as
(b) (4)
the vehicle and early bioavailability studies utilized
as the vehicle. These studies found rusfertide to have moderate to high
bioavailability that was formulation-dependent, with a relatively slow rate of absorption and
variable half-life across species and formulations. The half-life of rusfertide in single-dose
studies ranged from 2.7 to 12 hours in rats and monkeys, respectively.

Metabolism
The biotransformation of rusfertide is highly conserved across all evaluated species, proceeding
through two primary, CYP-independent metabolic pathways: proteolytic cleavage mediated by
trypsin-like peptidases, and hydroxylation of the palmitoyl moiety at the C14 position by an
unidentified enzyme. Across in vitro and in vivo studies spanning hepatocytes, tissue S9
homogenates, serum, and plasma, no unique human metabolites were identified, confirming
that the metabolic profile in cynomolgus monkeys closely recapitulates human metabolic
disposition.
In serum-based stability models, parent rusfertide exhibits high stability across all nonclinical
species with a half-life (t1/2) exceeding 24 hours. Conversely, the proteolytic metabolites M1 and
M4 demonstrate rapid clearance (t 1/2 <4 hours), supporting a rapid, sequential degradation
cascade progressing from M1→M4→M6. In contrast to its high stability in serum, rusfertide
undergoes rapid metabolic clearance in human liver and kidney S9 tissue homogenates. This
tissue-specific metabolism, alongside minor systemic clearance, generates low levels of
metabolites, including M11 (a rusfertide dimer formed via disulfide oxidation-reduction reactions)
as well as the minor proteolytic fragments M12, M13, and M14.
Liquid chromatography-high resolution mass spectrometry (LC-HRMS) profiling under clinical
and nonclinical protocols (Studies PTG-C-081 and PTG-C-098) identified the monohydroxylated
species M9 (14-hydroxypalmitoyl rusfertide) as a major circulating metabolite. M9 accounted for
20.75% to 22.91% of total chromatographic peak height in both monkey serum and human
plasma. Together, these data demonstrate that rusfertide metabolism is characterized by high
parent stability in circulation, localized tissue-specific proteolysis, and conserved lipid-chain
hydroxylation, establishing the cynomolgus monkey as a highly representative model for human
pharmacokinetic translation.

Excretion
The predominant route of 14C radioactivity associated with rusfertide elimination is CO2 via
expired air (21.3%) consistent with oxidative metabolism of the ¹⁴C-labeled palmitoyl fatty acid
chain, followed by feces (15%). Urinary excretion was minimal (4.5%). The largest proportion of
administered radioactivity (~43%) was retained in the carcass at 192 hours post-dose, with a
total recovery of 84.1% of the administered dose. This is consistent with the peptide nature of
rusfertide, extensive plasma binding, slow elimination from tissues and susceptibility to
proteolytic degradation.
Toxicokinetics
Following weekly subcutaneous administration, rusfertide exhibited dose-proportional
pharmacokinetics with no systemic accumulation across all evaluated species and study
durations. Male and female animals showed comparable pharmacokinetic profiles, with
exposure differences staying well under 2-fold in both species.
The 26-week repeat-dose toxicity study in rats (Study PTG-C-039) established a NOAEL of 10
mg/kg/week, yielding exposure margins of 4.8-fold to 11.1-fold over the MRHD exposure limits
(Cmax of 1,302 ng/mL and AUC of 100,516 h·ng/mL). In the 104-week carcinogenicity study
(Study PTG-C-105), the non-carcinogenic NOAEL of 3 mg/kg/week provided margins of
approximately 2.5-fold (Cmax) and 1.0-fold (AUC) relative to the MRHD.
In cynomolgus monkeys, the 39-week repeat-dose study (Study PTG-C-040) established a
NOAEL of 2 mg/kg/week. This dose yielded minimal exposure margins of 0.77-fold to 0.96-fold
over the MRHD, with the upper dose limits constrained by severe, pharmacologically mediated
anemia (on-target dose-limiting toxicity).
Because exposures to the major human metabolites M4 and M9 at the rat carcinogenicity
NOAEL (3 mg/kg/week) fell below the threshold for automatic qualification (6.4% and 2.7% of
MRHD exposures, respectively), dedicated qualification programs were executed:
M4 was qualified in a dedicated 13-week toxicity study in rats (Study PTG-C-131), establishing
a 3.8-fold margin of safety (MOS). M9 was qualified in the 13- and 39-week repeat-dose

14

Reference ID: 5867638

cynomolgus monkey studies, where nonclinical exposures represented 90.1% and 83.5% of the
MRHD M9 exposure, respectively.

General Toxicology
Rats:
•

Rusfertide induced dose-dependent anemia in repeat-dose toxicology studies of 4 to 26
weeks duration. Anemia was characterized by decreased red blood cell mass (red blood
cells (RBC), hemoglobin, hematocrit) with compensatory increases in reticulocytes,
platelets, and red cell distribution width. All hematological changes resolved following a 5week recovery period. These findings are fully consistent with the anticipated
pharmacodynamic activity of rusfertide as a hepcidin mimetic.

•

In 26-week repeat-dose toxicology study, white blood cells (WBC) parameters
demonstrated no dose-dependent or statistically significant changes except for decreased
neutrophils, large unstained cell (LUC), basophils percentage, and increased monocytes
in males at 10 mg/kg. The restriction of WBC changes to males despite higher rusfertide
exposure in females suggest these changes are driven by the degree of anemia rather
than direct drug effects and is consistent with a bone marrow shift toward erythroid
hyperplasia and reactive monocytosis secondary to pharmacology-mediated anemia.

•

NOAEL was defined at 10 mg/kg/week across all repeat-dose toxicology studies yielding
safety margins of 3.7x to 6.7x based on AUC comparisons. Dose-limiting toxicities observed
at higher doses included severe anemia, decreased body weight gain, splenomegaly, and
extramedullary hematopoiesis in the bone marrow, spleen, and liver.

•

Injection site reactions comprising fibrosis, inflammation, edema/hemorrhage occurred at all
dose levels, including controls, and increased in severity with escalating dose and prolonged
duration. The progressive but incomplete reversal of these findings suggests that the effects
are at least partially rusfertide-related.

•

Unanticipated findings in males specifically prostate/seminal vesicle atrophy, mammary
gland atrophy, and cecum inflammation were identified in the 4-week and 13-week studies
but were not sustained in the 26-week study. These findings did not influence the NOAEL
determination, did not adversely affect male fertility, and are not considered clinically
relevant.

•

The 26-week study (NOAEL = 10 mg/kg/week; safety margin 3.73x) served as the basis for
dose selection in the 2-year carcinogenicity study. A maximum tolerated dose of 3 mg/kg
was selected as doses exceeding this level were anticipated to surpass tolerability threshold
caused by progressive, rusfertide-related anemia.

Monkeys:
•

Rusfertide induced anemia at all doses in repeat-dose toxicology studies of 4 to 39 weeks
duration, as evidenced by decreased RBC with compensatory increases in platelets and
reticulocytes. These findings were mostly or fully reversible following 5-week recovery
periods. Anemic effect and associated findings were generally more severe in monkeys than
in rats.

16

Reference ID: 5867638

•

NOAEL was defined at 3 mg/kg/week in the 4-week study (1.7x the human exposure), 6
mg/kg/week in the 13-week study (1.1x), and 2 mg/kg/week in the 39-week study (0.9x); the
lower NOAEL in the 39-week study was attributable to a single death occurring at 6 mg/kg
on Day 257.

•

Mortality was observed in one monkey at 6 mg/kg in the 39-week study and was attributed
to myocardial degeneration and vacuolation secondary to severe anemia (hematocrit
declined from 37% to 8%), consistent with rusfertide’s exaggerated pharmacological activity
rather than direct cardiotoxicity, as supported by the absence of cardiovascular findings in
safety pharmacology studies and unaffected ECG parameters.

•

Injection site reactions (fibrosis, lymphocyte perivascular infiltrate, edema/hemorrhage,
inflammation) were observed at doses ≥0.6 mg/kg, showed dose-duration-dependent
increases in severity and progressive reversibility. The presence of fibrosis across all
groups, including controls, indicates a partial procedure-related etiology.

•

NOAEL of 2 mg/kg/week established in the 39-week study yields a safety margin of 0.9x.
While the margin is narrow, itis acceptable because mortality observed at supratherapeutic
dose was secondary to pharmacology-mediated anemia rather than direct organ toxicity.
Notably, the anemia was dose-dependent, reversible, and can be clinically monitorable in
the clinical setting.

sexes at 25 mg/kg which correlated with
lymphoid hypocellularity.
• Hepatocellular necrosis at 25 mg/kg (6/10 M,
2/10 F) with ↑ALT (+15%), ↑AST (+54-57%);
resolved during recovery
• ↓ body weight gain at 10 mg/kg (M: -17%, F:
-8%); ↓ food consumption (M: -10%)
• Extramedullary hematopoiesis in bone
marrow, spleen, liver at all doses
• Hemosiderin pigment in spleen and liver
• Off-target findings (males): prostate acinar
atrophy (≥10 mg/kg: 4/10), seminal vesicle
atrophy (≥10 mg/kg: 3/10), mammary
alveolar atrophy (≥3 mg/kg: 5/15 at 10
mg/kg), cecum inflammation (25 mg/kg: 5/10)
• Injection site fibrosis, inflammation,
edema/hemorrhage
• ↓ APTT at 25 mg/kg (-19%)
• All findings were reversible or trended lower
after recovery.
(b) (4)
Study Notes: formulation: 0.9% saline; conducted at
; includes CNS safety
pharmacology endpoints; NOAEL based on severity of anemia and hepatocellular effects at 25
mg/kg; off-target findings not observed in longer-duration studies; complete reversibility
demonstrated.

Table 11. Observations and Results on 26-Week Toxicity Study in Rats

Body Weights

Major findings
No rusfertide-related deaths
No rusfertide-related changes
Dose-dependent reductions in mean body weight gain in both male and female rats
over the 26-week treatment period. Statistically significant differences from controls
were observed only in the high-dose group for both sexes during the treatment period.
These changes corresponded with significant lower mean body weight and slight
decreases in food consumption (-12%) in males at the high dose. Body weight gain
was comparable to controls after the recovery period.

White blood cell (WBC) parameter changes were generally not dose-dependent or
statistically significant, with the exception of males in the 10 mg/kg group, which
exhibited statistically significant, moderate-to-severe alterations including decreased
neutrophils (−32.6%), large unstained cells (LUC; −50%), and percent basophils
(−28.6%), alongside increased monocytes (+85.7%). WBC changes were more
pronounced in males, consistent with the more severe anemia observed in this sex,
despite females demonstrating higher rusfertide systemic exposure. This sex-based
difference between exposure and hematological effects suggests that WBC changes
are driven by the degree of rusfertide-induced anemia rather than by direct effects.
Collectively, the observed WBC changes are consistent with a bone marrow shift
toward erythroid hyperplasia and reactive monocytosis secondary to hemolytic
anemia.

Histopathology
Adequate battery: Yes
Rusfertide-related microscopic findings include:
• Dosing site: subcutaneous fibrosis (minimal to moderate) occurred at all doses including control,
edema/hemorrhage (minimal to mild), myofiber degeneration/regeneration (minimal to mild), and
subcutaneous inflammation (minimal) occurred, all generally dose-proportional
• Bone marrow (femur and sternum): minimal hematopoietic hypercellularity at ≥1 mg/kg/dose
• Spleen: minimal to moderate extramedullary hematopoiesis and intracellular brown/green pigment
(hemosiderin) at ≥1 mg/kg/dose
• Liver: minimal extramedullary hematopoiesis and intracellular brown/green pigment at ≥1
mg/kg/dose.
• Following the 35-day recovery period, bone marrow findings and most hematologic changes
resolved completely; dosing site, spleen, and liver findings showed incomplete but progressive
ongoing reversal.

Parameters assessed: mortality, clinical observations, body weights, food
consumption, ophthalmoscopy, blood pressure, ECG, toxicokinetics,
clinical pathology (hematology, coagulation, clinical chemistry, urinalysis),
anti-drug antibody assessment, gross pathology, organ weights, and
histopathology.
Study Report Amendment #1 incorporated the Analysis of Anti-Drug
Antibody Report, updated abbreviations of urinary and hematological
parameters to comply with SEND data and added and discussed clinical
signs data for animal 4503C int the pathology report to match the SEND
data submitted. Except for the ADA report, these changes did not affect
data interpretation or conclusions.

Dose and frequency of
dosing:

0, 0.6, 2, and 6 mg/kg/dose; administered once weekly for 39 weeks (39
doses total, Days 1–267)

Route of administration:

Subcutaneous injection into the interscapular region

Formulation/Vehicle:

PTG-300 (rusfertide) dissolved in 0.9% Sodium Chloride for Injection,
USP

Species/Strain:

Cynomolgus monkey (Macaca fascicularis)

Number/Sex/Group:

4 males and females/group (Main); 2 males and females/group
(Recovery)

Age:

Approximately 2–3 years old at initiation of treatment

Satellite groups/ unique
design:

Recovery groups (2M/2F per group) observed for 35 days post last dose
and necropsied on Day 302; Main animals necropsied on Day 274 (7
days post last dose); toxicokinetic blood sampling on Days 1, 85, 92, 162,
and 267; dosing site rotated among 4 interscapular regions beginning
Day 92; anti-drug antibody samples collected pre-treatment and at
Weeks 4, 12, 26, 39, and 43 (recovery)

Deviation from study protocol affecting
interpretation of results: None

Table 13. Observations and Results on 39-Week Toxicity Study in Monkeys
Parameters

Major findings

Mortality

One high-dose (6 mg/kg/dose) male (Animal No. 4001A) was found dead on
Day 257; death attributed to secondary effects of pharmacologically driven
profound anemia (myocardial degeneration and vacuolation secondary to
anoxia/hypoxia)

Clinical Signs

No rusfertide-related changes

Body Weights

No rusfertide-related changes; the found-dead high-dose male (4001A) showed
a 20% decrease in body weight gain compared to control males prior to death

Ophthalmoscopy

No rusfertide-related ocular changes

ECG

No rusfertide-related effects on P-QRS-T morphology or measured ECG
parameters (heart rate, PR, QT, QTc, QRS duration) at any dose level

Hematology

Rusfertide induced dose-dependent decreases in red blood cell parameters
(RBC count, hemoglobin, hematocrit, MCH, MCHC) at ≥0.6 mg/kg/dose,
consistent with pharmacology-mediated anemia and increases in platelets,
reticulocyte counts, and RDW at ≥2 mg/kg/dose, consistent with a regenerative

27

Reference ID: 5867638

response.
No statistically significant rusfertide-related changes were observed in any WBC
parameters
Severe anemia in the high-dose male found dead; morphological changes
(anisocytosis, microcytosis, hypochromia, high concentration variance) at 6
mg/kg/dose. Most parameters resolved or trended towards resolution except
persistent severe platelet elevation, RDW and reticulocytes counts, which
trended slightly lower, at 6 mg/kg/dose after the recovery period.

Clinical Chemistry

No rusfertide-related changes

No rusfertide-related changes

Gross Pathology

Dark areas at dosing sites of 2/7 high-dose males (correlating with microscopic

No rusfertide-related changes

Histopathology
Adequate battery: Yes
Rusfertide produced non-adverse microscopic findings:
• Dosing sites: fibrosis, edema/hemorrhage, perivascular lymphocyte infiltrate, and/or chronic active
inflammation at ≥0.6 mg/kg/dose (dose-proportional; incomplete but progressive reversal at
recovery)
• Bone marrow: increased hematopoietic cellularity (femur, sternum) at ≥2 mg/kg/dose (complete
reversal at recovery)
• Spleen and liver: extramedullary hematopoiesis at ≥2 mg/kg/dose (complete reversal at recovery);
intracellular brown/green pigment (hemosiderin) at ≥2 mg/kg/dose (complete reversal at 2
mg/kg/dose; incomplete reversal at 6 mg/kg/dose at recovery)
In the found-dead high-dose male:
• Mild myocardial degeneration and minimal vacuolation (secondary to anemia)
• Minimal bone marrow hypocellularity with increased immature hematopoietic cells.

Anti-Drug Antibodies
(ADA)

Rusferide induced a dose-dependent ADA response, with 0, 2, 3, and 6
animals testing positive in control, 0.6, 2, and 6 mg/kg groups, respectively.
Titers were generally low at 0.6 and 2 mg/kg (95–3249 and 150–170,
respectively) and higher at 6 mg/kg (150–2301), with the earliest positive
results appearing at Day 28. Two animals in the 6 mg/kg group remained ADApositive at Day 301 during recovery. No sex differences in titer magnitude were
observed.

Genetic Toxicology
Rusfertide demonstrated no genotoxic potential across a comprehensive battery of genetic
toxicology studies, including bacterial reverse mutation assays, in vitro chromosomal aberration
assays in mammalian cells, and an in vivo chromosomal aberration assay in rats. The major
human metabolites M1, M4, and M9 were assessed in silico via QSAR modeling and predicted
to be non-mutagenic and non-carcinogenic, qualifying them as Class 5 impurities per ICH
M7(R2) guidelines.

Studies
PTG-C-085, PTG-C-096,
PTG-C-111 /

Rusfertide was evaluated in human peripheral blood lymphocytes at
concentrations ranging from 1 to 500 µg/mL with treatment durations of
4 hours in the presence and absence of metabolic activation (S9), and
21 hours without S9. Although cloudiness was noted in the culture
media at 500 µg/mL at the end of treatment periods, no cytotoxicity was
observed at any concentration tested.
Rusfertide did not cause chromosomal aberrations in human peripheral
lymphocytes under the conditions tested.
Rusfertide was SC administered at doses of 500, 1000, or 2000 mg/kg
to male Sprague Dawley rats (Hsd:SD strain) on Days 1, 3, and 5 (three
doses separated by 48-hour intervals). Dose formulation analysis
indicated the actual highest dose administered was 1424 mg/kg (71.2%
of target). A satellite group of 3 males received 2000 mg/kg with blood
samples collected at 8- and 24-hours post-dose to confirm systemic
exposure.
Rusfertide was not overly toxic at the doses evaluated, and systemic
exposure was verified through toxicokinetic sampling. Rusfertide did not
cause significant or dose-dependent increases in structural or numerical
chromosomal aberrations (polyploid or endoreduplicated cells) at the
conditions tested.
The mutagenic and carcinogenic potential of rusfertide metabolites M1,
M4, and M9 were evaluated using in silico quantitative structure-activity
relationship (QSAR) analysis with Derek Nexus and Leadscope Model
Applier systems. No alerts for mutagenicity or carcinogenicity were
identified for any of the three metabolites in Derek Nexus, and
Leadscope Model Applier predicted all metabolites to be non-mutagenic
in bacterial mutagenicity models for Escherichia coli and Salmonella
typhimurium. Structural analogues identified from Leadscope analyses
included polypeptide compounds with (b) (4) % structural similarity that
were uniformly negative in Ames assays, and the large molecular
weights of these metabolites preclude cellular entry and DNA
interaction. Based on ICH M7(R2) guidelines, metabolites M1, M4, and
M9 are considered Class 5 impurities and may be treated as nonmutagenic.

Carcinogenicity
The carcinogenicity potential of rusfertide was evaluated in a 26-week rasH2 transgenic mouse
study and a 104-week rat carcinogenicity study. The rat study included systemic exposure
measurements of the parent compound and metabolites M4 and M9, which showed comparable
parent:metabolite ratios to pivotal toxicology studies. Both studies showed no evidence of drugrelated neoplasms.

Reproductive Toxicology
Rusfertide was evaluated in fertility and early embryonic development (FEED) studies in male
and female rats and embryo-fetal development (EFD) studies in rats and rabbits. FEED studies
showed no effects on mating performance, sperm parameters, estrous cyclicity, or early
embryonic development at doses up to 10 mg/kg in males and 3 mg/kg in females, despite nonadverse hematology changes consistent with pharmacology. In EFD studies, rusfertide was well
tolerated in pregnant animals at maternal NOAELs of 3 mg/kg in rats and 1 mg/kg in rabbits but
caused malformations in rats at ≥1 mg/kg (nasopharyngeal and CNS defects, eye
malformations, fused ribs) and embryo lethality in rabbits at 1 mg/kg. The fetal NOAELs of 0.3
mg/kg in both species represent only 0.05x (rat) and 0.2x (rabbit) the human exposure at 120
mg, which are 20-fold and 5-fold lower than the maternal NOAELs, indicating rusfertide may
cause embryo-fetal harm at therapeutic doses without maternal toxicity. A pre- and post-natal
development study was not conducted and is not required for NDA submission.
Study Number/ Title: PTG-C-064 / A Subcutaneous Injection Fertility and Early Embryonic
Development Study in Male Rats
GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Study Findings:
•

Rusfertide produced no adverse effects on male fertility at doses up to 10 mg/kg. Mating
indices, fertility indices, and conception rates remained within historical control ranges at
all doses. Sperm concentration, motility, and morphology remained within normal
biological variation. Reproductive organ weights and testicular histopathology showed no

32

Reference ID: 5867638

rusfertide treatment-related changes.
•

Rusfertide produced minimal systemic toxicity. At 10 mg/kg only, body weight gain
decreased 11% (premating) and 39% (post-mating), resulting in 5.9% lower terminal
body weights, considered non-adverse given minimal magnitude and no impact on
reproduction. Hematological changes at ≥1 mg/kg reflected expected pharmacology,
with neutropenia, decreased red cell mass, increased reticulocytes (at 10 mg/kg), and
altered red cell morphology indicating iron-restricted erythropoiesis.

•

The study established a paternal NOAEL and male fertility/early embryonic development
NOEL of 10 mg/kg. Exposure at 10 mg/kg (AUClast: 261,865 ng·h/mL from 6-month rat
study) provided a 2.6X-fold margin over the MRHD.

•

Rusfertide produced no maternal toxicity, adverse effects on female fertility or early
embryonic development at doses up to 3 mg/kg. Fertility indices, conception rates, and
days to mating remained within historical control ranges at all doses.

•

The study established a maternal NOAEL and female fertility/early embryonic
development NOEL of 3 mg/kg (AUClast: 101,000 ng·h/mL), a 1-fold margin over the

•

Rusfertide produced dose-dependent teratogenic effects, in the absence of maternal

•

At the high dose, rusfertide caused craniofacial and CNS malformations in 18 fetuses
across 13 liters (65% litter incidence), with all major malformations exceeding historical
control data.

•

The study established a maternal NOAEL of 3 mg/kg (Cmax of 4,180 ng/mL and AUC of
101,000 ng/mL*h on GD Day 15) and an embryo-fetal development NOAEL of 0.3 mg/kg
(Cmax of 381 ng/mL and AUC of 5,170 ng/mL*h on GD 15 ), which provided a minimal
safety margin (1-fold) for maternal toxicity and an inadequate safety margin for embryofetal toxicity (based on AUC) over the MRHD

Study Number/ Title: PTG-C-047 / An Embryo-Fetal Development Study of Rusfertide by
Subcutaneous Injection in Rabbits
GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Study Findings:
•

Rusfertide did not produce teratogenic effects when administered subcutaneously to
pregnant rabbits at doses up to 1 mg/kg/dose. Malformations observed across all groups
showed no dose-response relationship and occurred at incidences comparable to
historical control data.

•

Embryolethality was observed at 1 mg/kg/dose, with one dam experiencing total
resorption resulting in post-implantation loss (9.86%) exceeding the historical control
range (9.1%). Embryolethality was also observed at ≥0.3 mg/kg/dose in a previous dose
range-finding study.

•

The study established a maternal NOAEL of 1 mg/kg/dose (Cmax of 3,640 ng/mL and
AUC(0-t) of 79,500 ng/mL*hr on GD 16) and an embryo-fetal development NOAEL of 0.3
mg/kg/dose (maternal Cmax of 1,140 ng/mL and AUC(0-t) of 18,800 ng/mL*hr on Day
16 pc) which provides a minimal safety margin (0.79X) for maternal toxicity and embryofetal development (0.19X).

Toxicological Qualification of Rusfertide Metabolites
All four rusfertide metabolites (M1, M4, M6, and M9) were fully qualified toxicologically. M1 and
M6, both minor metabolites, were adequately covered through parent compound testing and
QSAR evaluation. M9, the most abundant metabolite, was qualified through exposure
comparisons confirming >50% coverage in completed chronic toxicology studies in monkeys.
M4 required a dedicated 13-week toxicology study in rats, which established a (b) (4) fold margin
of safety relative to human exposure. QSAR analysis predicted all four metabolites to be nonmutagenic and non-carcinogenic. Findings from the M4 qualification study were consistent with
the pharmacological activity of rusfertide as a hepcidin mimetic; all observed effects
demonstrating resolution or a trend towards resolution and no adverse findings identified at the
(b)
NOAEL of (4) mg/kg/dose.
