Reference ID: 5860339

1 Background
The Applicant, Takeda Pharmaceutical Company Ltd., submitted NDA 220605 for rusfertide
(proprietary name MIMRYLO) for the treatment of adult patients with polycythemia vera (PV).
To support the safety and effectiveness of rusfertide, the Applicant primarily relied on data from
the Phase 3 VERIFY study (PTG-300-11) and the Phase 2 REVIVE study (PTG-300-04). In the
VERIFY study, the Patient-Reported Outcomes Measurement Information System (PROMIS)
Fatigue Short Form (SF) 8a and the Myelofibrosis Symptom Assessment Form (MFSAF)
version 4.0 Total Symptom Score-7 items (TSS7) were included as key secondary endpoints to
assess fatigue and PV-related symptom burden, respectively. Specifically, the mean change from
baseline to the end of Part 1a (Week 32) in the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score and the
MFSAF version 4.0 TSS7 were evaluated.
This statistical review was conducted in response to a Patient-Focused Statistical Science (PFSS)
consult from the Clinical Team, requesting an assessment of: (1) the clinical meaningfulness of
the fatigue- and PV symptom-related key secondary endpoints in the VERIFY study; and (2)
whether the available quantitative evidence supports the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a as fit-forpurpose in the proposed context of use. This review focuses on the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total
T-score, a clinical outcome assessment (COA)-based endpoint that measures fatigue severity and
its impact on patients. The MFSAF version 4.0 TSS7 is not evaluated in this review because
concerns regarding its content validity preclude a determination that it is fit-for-purpose for the
current context of use. A detailed discussion of those concerns is provided in the Division of
Clinical Outcome Assessment (DCOA) review. Notwithstanding the content validity concerns,
the clinical meaningfulness of the MFSAF version 4.0 TSS7 was assessed separately for
reference purposes and the results were shared with the Clinical Team.

2 The Fitness-for-Purpose of the Patient-Reported Outcomes
Measurement Information System (PROMIS) Fatigue Short
Form (SF) 8a: Quantitative Evidence Assessment
The Applicant submitted quantitative evidence to support the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a as a fit-forpurpose patient-reported outcome (PRO) measure for assessing fatigue in adults with PV. The
evidence was generated primarily from the Phase 3 VERIFY study (PTG-300-11) and included
evaluations of the proposed scoring method, score distributions, construct validity, reliability,
responsiveness, and other psychometric properties.
This review evaluates whether the available quantitative evidence supports interpreting the
PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score as a valid and reliable measure of fatigue in the proposed
context of use. Consistent with FDA's Patient-Focused Drug Development Guidance 31, this
review is organized according to the evidence-based rationale for determining whether a COA is
fit-for-purpose. Specifically, this review evaluates the quantitative evidence relevant to
Components B, D, E, F, G, and H of that rationale, including quantitative evidence that
1

https://www.fda.gov/regulatory-information/search-fda-guidance-documents/patient-focused-drug-developmentselecting-developing-or-modifying-fit-purpose-clinical-outcome

5

Reference ID: 5860339

complements qualitative findings (e.g., item-level response distributions), the appropriateness of
the scoring method and psychometric model, the influence of extraneous factors and
measurement error on scores, and the extent to which the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a scores
correspond to the meaningful aspect of health related to fatigue. Components and
subcomponents that rely primarily on qualitative evidence (e.g., concept elicitation, cognitive
interview, and content validity) are discussed only insofar as quantitative evidence is available
and are otherwise addressed in the DCOA review.

2.1 Component B. The PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a Includes All
Important Parts of Fatigue
The Applicant primarily provided qualitative evidence to demonstrate that the PROMIS Fatigue
SF 8a captures the important aspects of fatigue relevant to patients with PV. Although not
presented for this purpose by the Applicant, the descriptive evaluations of item response
distributions and item-level floor and ceiling effects can also be considered quantitative evidence
that complements the qualitative findings supporting this component.
Floor effects, defined as more than 20% of patients selecting the least severe response option,
were observed for all items except Item 1 (“During the past 7 days I feel fatigued”) at Baseline.2.
However, importantly, DCOA review noted that the cognitive debriefing (CD) results confirm
that the items were relevant and important to patients with PV.

2.2 Component D. Respondents Understand the Instructions and
Items as Intended by the Measure Developer
D.1. Respondents are able, without difficulty, to select response options that match their
experiences.
The Applicant primarily provided qualitative CD interviews to demonstrate that respondents
understood the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a instructions, items, and response options. The review
team complements these qualitative findings with descriptive evaluations of item response
distributions and item-level floor effects. Responses were observed across all response categories
for each PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a item, indicating that respondents were able to use the available
response options.
As noted under Component B, however, floor effects were observed for seven of the eight items
at Baseline. Although these findings indicate that many patients reported minimal fatigue on
several individual items at Baseline, they do not, by themselves, suggest that respondents
misunderstood the response options or were unable to select responses that reflected their
experiences. Rather, the findings suggest that several items may have been poorly targeted to the
2

The Applicant described this observation as a “ceiling effect” in the COA Evidence Dossier; however, we consider
this characterization to be more accurately described as a floor effect, as the observed clustering of scores at the least
severe end of the scale indicates that patients had limited room to demonstrate improvement.

6

Reference ID: 5860339

fatigue severity distribution of the study population, resulting in limited opportunity to detect
improvement on those items. DCOA review noted that CD results confirm that the items were
clearly understood by patients with PV.

2.3 Component E. The Method of Scoring Responses to the
PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a is Appropriate for Assessing Fatigue
E.1. The method of combining item responses (i.e., scoring) is appropriate to assess fatigue.
The Applicant proposed a reflective indicator measurement model to support a single PROMIS
Fatigue SF 8a total T-score. To evaluate the proposed scoring approach, the Applicant assessed
the dimensionality of the measure using confirmatory factor analysis (CFA).
The CFA generally supported the proposed unidimensional structure. All eight items
demonstrated strong factor loadings at Baseline (range: 0.902–0.969), supporting the
interpretation that the items measure a common underlying fatigue construct. In addition, the
Comparative Fit Index (CFI=0.997) and Standardized Root Mean Square Residual
(SRMR=0.017) met the Applicant's prespecified criteria for acceptable model fit (CFI≥0.95;
SRMR≤0.08).
However, the Root Mean Square Error of Approximation (RMSEA=0.139; 95% CI: 0.117–
0.162) exceeded the conventional criterion of ≤0.06 (Hu and Bentler, 1999). In response to an
FDA Information Request (IR) dated April 7, 2026, the Applicant noted that RMSEA may
overestimate poor model fit in relatively simple models with a small number of degrees of
freedom. We consider this explanation reasonable. Furthermore, evaluation of residual
correlations did not identify evidence of local dependence (all less than 0.20) or other patterns of
residual misfit that would suggest meaningful model misspecification. Taken together, the strong
factor loadings, acceptable CFI and SRMR, and the absence of substantial residual dependence
support the proposed one-factor measurement model.
E.2. The assumptions of the psychometric model used to generate COA scores are sufficiently
met.
The PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score is generated using the PROMIS Item Response Theory
(IRT)-based scoring algorithm. Appropriate use of this scoring approach requires that the
underlying IRT model assumptions, including unidimensionality and local independence, are
reasonably satisfied. The Applicant evaluated unidimensionality using CFA, which, as discussed
above in Component E.1, generally supported a single underlying fatigue construct.
The Applicant did not directly evaluate the IRT assumption of local independence using IRTbased diagnostics (e.g., residual correlations based on the fitted IRT model or Chen & Thissen
(1999)’s LD [local dependence] χ² statistics). In response to an FDA IR dated April 7, 2026, the
Applicant provided residual correlation analyses to further investigate the elevated RMSEA. As
described above in Component E.1, we did not identify evidence of substantial residual
dependence among PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a items that would suggest meaningful violations of
local independence. Although these analyses were not formal IRT local dependence diagnostics,
7

Reference ID: 5860339

the available evidence does not indicate that local dependence is likely to meaningfully affect the
proposed scoring approach.
The Applicant also presented item characteristic curves, item information functions, and test
information functions based on a partial credit model (PCM). Because these analyses were not
conducted under the psychometric model used to generate the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total Tscores (i.e., graded response model which, unlike the PCM), allows item discrimination
parameters to vary across items), they provide limited evidence regarding the assumptions
underlying the proposed scoring approach and are not considered further in this assessment.
E.3. Empirical assessment of the missing-data rule shows the method for handling missing
item responses is appropriate for assessing fatigue.
The PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a uses a response pattern scoring approach, whereby an individual's
fatigue level is estimated based on the observed pattern of completed item responses. Although
response pattern scoring can technically generate a score when fewer than all items are
completed, it remains important to prespecify the minimum number of completed items required
to ensure adequate score reliability and interpretability within the proposed context of use.
The Applicant did not explicitly specify the minimum number of completed items required to
generate a valid PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score. Accordingly, FDA requested clarification
of the missing-data rule and a summary of item-level completion in an IR dated April 7, 2026. In
response, the Applicant stated that the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a was administered electronically
(b) (4) application, which required participants to complete items sequentially and
using the
did not permit item skipping. Consequently, all participants who completed the PROMIS Fatigue
SF 8a at Baseline, Week 20, and Week 32 completed all eight items. Therefore, partial item
completion did not occur in the VERIFY study, and the response pattern scoring algorithm was
effectively applied only to complete assessments.
E.4. Scores from the COA are sufficiently sensitive to reflect changes in fatigue within the
context of use.
As noted above, floor effects were observed for seven of the eight PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a items
at Baseline, indicating that many patients entered the study with relatively low fatigue levels on
several individual items and thereby limiting the opportunity to detect further improvement on
those items following treatment. Because the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score is derived
from responses across all eight items using an IRT-based scoring algorithm, the extent to which
these item-level floor effects influence the responsiveness of the total score depends on the
contribution of each item within the measurement model. Despite these item-level floor effects,
the total T-score demonstrated adequate variability across the observed score range and showed
sensitivity to change, as evidenced by larger negative changes in PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total Tscore among participants who reported improvement on the Patient Global Impression of
Severity (PGI-S) and Patient Global Impression of Change (PGI-C) anchors relative to those
who remained stable or worsened. These findings indicate that the total T-score retained
sensitivity to fatigue-related changes within the study population, notwithstanding the item-level
floor effects observed at Baseline.
8

Reference ID: 5860339

2.4 Component F. Scores from the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a Are Not
Overly Influenced by Processes/Concepts That Are Not Part of
Fatigue
F.4. Implementation and/or study design decisions do not overly influence assessment of
fatigue.
(b) (4) application in
The PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a was administered electronically using the
a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial. The electronic data capture system required
sequential completion of all PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a items and did not permit item skipping.
Consequently, completed assessments contained no missing item responses. No quantitative
analyses were submitted evaluating the potential influence of implementation decision on
PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a scores beyond the observed completion rates. However, given the
brevity of the instrument (eight items) and the sequential administration format, it is unlikely that
the requirement for sequential completion overly influenced patients’ fatigue assessments.

2.5 Component G. Scores from the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a Are Not
Overly Influenced by Measurement Error
G.1. Test-retest reliability coefficient indicates scores are not overly influenced by variation
over time within clinically stable patients.
The Applicant evaluated test-retest reliability among participants classified as clinically stable
based on the PGI-S, using assessments obtained between Screening and Baseline. Test-retest
reliability was estimated using a two-way mixed-effects model with absolute agreement. The
estimated intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC) was 0.89 (95% CI: 0.851, 0.919), exceeding the
Applicant's prespecified criterion for acceptable reliability (ICC ≥0.75) and indicating good testretest reliability.
G.2. Internal consistency reliability.
The Applicant evaluated internal consistency using both Cronbach’s alpha and McDonald’s
omega. At Baseline, Cronbach's alpha was 0.97 and McDonald’s omega was 0.99. We note that
internal consistency estimates should be interpreted in conjunction with the proposed
measurement model because both Cronbach’s alpha and McDonald’s omega assume that the
proposed score appropriately reflects a common underlying construct. As discussed in
Component E, the available evidence generally supports the proposed unidimensional structure
of the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a.

9

Reference ID: 5860339

2.6 Component H. Scores from the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a
Correspond to the Meaningful Aspect of Health Related to
Fatigue
H.1. Correlation coefficients for the relationship between PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a scores and
other clinical outcome assessments are as hypothesized a priori.
The Applicant evaluated convergent evidence by examining the relationship between the
PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score and other fatigue-related patient-reported outcome
measures. At Baseline, the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score demonstrated strong
correlations with the European Organization for Research and Treatment of Cancer Quality of
Life Questionnaire Core 30 (EORTC QLQ-C30) Fatigue scale (r = 0.723), the
Myeloproliferative Neoplasm Symptom Assessment Form (MPN-SAF) Fatigue item (r = 0.851),
and the MFSAF Weekly Fatigue item (r = 0.817). These findings are consistent with the
Applicant's a priori hypotheses and support that the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a measures the
intended fatigue construct.
Although correlations with some reference measures (e.g., EORTC QLQ-C30 Physical
Functioning, Emotional Functioning, and Role Functioning, and the MPN-SAF TSS10) differed
from the a priori hypotheses, these deviations were modest and/or clinically explainable.
Overall, the observed pattern of correlations was clinically plausible and provides convergent
evidence of the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a.
H.2. Empirical comparisons of PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a scores for patient groups known to
differ with respect to fatigue show relationships hypothesized a priori.
The Applicant evaluated known-groups evidence by comparing PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total Tscores across patient groups defined by the PGI-S and the MFSAF Weekly Fatigue item. At
Baseline, PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-scores increased monotonically with increasing fatigue
severity for both anchors, indicating that the instrument distinguished among patients with
differing levels of fatigue severity.

2.7 Conclusion
Overall, the available quantitative evidence supports the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score as
a fit-for-purpose measure of fatigue in adults with PV for the proposed context of use. This
conclusion should be considered together with qualitative evidence, which is reviewed separately
by DCOA.

10

Reference ID: 5860339

3 Evaluation of Meaningfulness of Treatment Effect on the
PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a Endpoint
3.1 Background: Applicant’s Assessment and Conclusion
To aid in the interpretation of the key secondary endpoint evaluating the mean change from
baseline to the end of Part 1a (Week 32) in the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score, the
Applicant conducted anchor-based analyses supplemented with empirical cumulative distribution
function (eCDF) curves and probability density function (PDF) plots, as well as distributionbased analyses, using data from the Phase 3 VERIFY study (PTG-300-11). The analyses were
conducted at Week 20 and Week 32.
For the anchor-based analyses, the Applicant proposed three anchor measures: the Patient Global
Impression of Severity (PGI-S), the Patient Global Impression of Change (PGI-C), and EORTC
(b) (4) The
QLQ-C30 Item

as the target anchor change category. Based on these analyses, the Applicant derived a meaningful
score difference (MSD) range of approximately -6.70 to -6.60 points for the PROMIS Fatigue SF
8a total T-score.

However, the Applicant’s analyses had the following important limitations:
•

The Applicant's proposed anchor scale was not considered appropriate to support
the anchor-based analyses. The PGI-S assesses overall PV symptom severity over the
past 24 hours, which is misaligned with both the concept of interest (i.e., fatigue severity
and impact) and the recall period (i.e., past 7 days) of the target measure, the PROMIS
Fatigue SF 8a. This conceptual and recall period misalignment reduces the utility of the
PGI-S as an anchor for interpreting changes in the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score,
as patients may be thinking about different symptoms and a different time window when
11

Reference ID: 5860339

they respond to the anchor and when they respond to the COA. Refer to the DCOA review
for further discussion of the anchor scales.
•

The Applicant did not pre-specify a target anchor change category informed by
patient input. The target anchor change category used in the Applicant's original analyses
(i.e., 1-category improvement on PGI-S) was not pre-specified during the IND phase and
was not informed by qualitative evidence (e.g., cognitive or exit interviews) demonstrating
that patients consider this level of change to represent a meaningful improvement. Rather,
this category was determined post hoc based on the observed data. The absence of such
evidence reduces the interpretability of the derived MSD values from the patient
perspective.

•

The Applicant did not account for subjects’ baseline symptom severity or potential
misclassification rates when deriving MSD values. The amount of change that patients
consider meaningful may depend on the severity of their symptoms at baseline. Failure to
account for baseline symptom severity may result in MSD estimates that are not
representative of the full range of patients in the target population. Additionally, the
Applicant did not assess the misclassification rate associated with the derived MSDs, that
is, the proportion of subjects who would be incorrectly classified as having experienced a
meaningful improvement based on the values but who did not achieve meaningful
improvement based on the anchor scales.

Given these concerns, and in particular the primary concern regarding the conceptual
misalignment of the PGI-S with the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a, FDA issued an IR on March 26,
2026, asking the Applicant to conduct additional anchor-based meaningful change analyses using
three items from the EORTC QLQ-C30 that are more closely aligned with the concept of fatigue:
Item 10 (“Did you need to rest?”), Item 12 (“Have you felt weak?”), and Item 18 (“Were you
tired?”).
In response to the IR, the Applicant submitted updated analyses incorporating these three
EORTC QLQ-C30 items as additional anchor measures. Based on correlations between the
available anchor items and the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score, the Applicant ultimately
used EORTC QLQ-C30 Item 18 and PGI-S as the anchors for the updated analyses, using a 1category improvement on each as the target anchor change category. The Applicant did not
incorporate EORTC QLQ-C30 Items 10 and 12 into the updated analyses, as these items
demonstrated low correlations with the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score. Based on the
updated analyses, the Applicant derived a revised MSD range of approximately -7.45 to -6.60
points and concluded that an improvement of approximately 6.60 to 7.45 points in PROMIS
Fatigue SF 8a total T-score from Baseline to Week 32 represents a meaningful improvement in
patient-reported fatigue.

12

Reference ID: 5860339

3.2 FDA’s Assessment
Given the limitations with the Applicant's analyses described in Section 3.1, FDA conducted
anchor-based analyses supplemented with eCDF curves using the three items from the EORTC
QLQ-C30 as anchor measures: Item 10 (“Did you need to rest?”), Item 12 (“Have you felt
weak?”), and Item 18 (“Were you tired?”) as well as the PGI-S and PGI-C. These analyses
additionally incorporated considerations for subjects’ baseline fatigue severity and
misclassification rates, which were not addressed in the Applicant's analyses, to provide a
comprehensive evaluation of the clinical meaningfulness of the observed treatment effect on the
PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score endpoint. The following sections document FDA’s
assessment of the clinically meaningful within-subject change in PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total Tscore in the VERIFY study.

3.2.1 Anchor Assessment
As noted above, FDA identified a conceptual misalignment between the PGI-S, PGI-C, and
(b)
EORTC QLQ-C30 Item (4) anchor measures and the concept of fatigue severity and impact
measured by the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a. Based on internal discussion with DCOA, FDA
requested that the Applicant conduct additional anchor-based analyses using three EORTC QLQC30 items that assess fatigue-related concepts more closely aligned with the concept of interest:
o EORTC QLQ-C30 Item 10: “Did you need to rest?”
o EORTC QLQ-C30 Item 12: “Have you felt weak?”
o EORTC QLQ-C30 Item 18: “Were you tired?”
All three items use a 4-category response scale ranging from “Not at all” (1) to “Very much” (4),
with a recall period of the past week. It is important to note a key limitation of these anchor
items: the EORTC QLQ-C30 was not administered at Week 0 (Baseline) in the VERIFY study
but was administered at Screening and Week 32. As a result, the anchor analysis period
(Screening to Week 32) does not align exactly with the endpoint analysis period (Baseline to
Week 32). The Screening assessments were collected between 1 and 42 days prior to Baseline,
and although both timepoints occurred prior to first dose, the variability in timing may introduce
additional uncertainty in the interpretation of change. That said, because both assessments were
obtained before treatment initiation, this limitation is considered less impactful than the
conceptual misalignment between the PGI-S and PGI-C anchors and the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a.
The PGI-S and PGI-C were retained as supplementary anchors in FDA’s assessment, despite
their conceptual limitations, to provide additional context for the interpretation of the PROMIS
Fatigue SF 8a endpoint. The PGI-S assesses overall PV symptom severity using a 4-category
response scale (“None,” “Mild,” “Moderate,” and “Severe”), and the PGI-C assesses overall PV
symptom change using a 5-category response scale (“Much better,” “A little better,” “No
change,” “A little worse,” and “Much worse”). Table 2 summarizes the anchor scales considered
for the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score endpoint interpretation. Refer to the DCOA review
for additional details on the adequacy of the anchor scales.

3.2.2 Anchor-Based Analyses
3.2.2.1
Distribution of Baseline PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a Total TScore
To understand the magnitude of change in the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score considered
meaningful to subjects, we examined the distribution of baseline PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total Tscore in PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score by treatment arm (see Table 4). The PROMIS
Fatigue SF 8a total T-score is calculated using response pattern scoring, with higher T-scores
indicating greater fatigue severity.
Within the Intention-to-Treat (ITT) population, the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score at
baseline ranged from 33.10 to 77.70 for both the rusfertide and placebo arms. The mean and
median PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-scores at baseline were 52.50 and 52.50 for the rusfertide
arm and 51.18 and 51.60 for the placebo arm, respectively, indicating similar baseline fatigue
severity across treatment arms.

3.2.2.2

Target Anchor Change Category

Ideally, target anchor change categories should be pre-specified by the Applicant, agreed upon
by FDA during the IND phase, and be supported by qualitative evidence demonstrating that
patients consider the specified level of change to represent a meaningful improvement. However,
as noted in Section 3.1, the target anchor change categories used in the Applicant's analyses were
not pre-specified and were not informed by patient input.
In the absence of pre-specified, patient-informed target anchor change categories, FDA used a 1category improvement on the EORTC QLQ-C30 Item 18 and PGI-S as the target anchor change
categories for the anchor-based analyses, based on the observed distribution of change in these
anchor measures and the eCDF curves (see detailed discussions below in this section and
Sections 3.2.2.4 and 3.2.2.5). For the PGI-C, FDA used “much better” as the target anchor
change category based on the eCDF curve (see detailed discussion in Section 3.2.2.6). These
decisions were based on internal discussions with DCOA. It is important to note that the use of
post hoc target anchor change categories that are not informed by patient input is a limitation of
the anchor-based analyses conducted for this application.
To examine the distribution of change in fatigue severity between Screening and Week 32, a
cross-tabulation of EORTC QLQ-C30 Item 18 at Screening and at Week 32 was performed
(Table 5). Among subjects who reported “a little” tiredness at Screening, the most common
change was “no change” (N = 67, 22.9%), followed by a 1-category improvement (N = 18,
6.1%). Among subjects who reported “quite a bit” of tiredness at Screening, the most common
change was “no change” (N = 20, 6.8%), followed by a 1-category improvement (N = 16, 5.5%).
Among subjects who reported “very much” tiredness at Screening, the most common change was
a 1-category improvement (N = 8, 2.7%), followed by “no change” (N = 5, 1.7%).

3.2.2.3

Results

3.2.2.4

EORTC QLQ-C30 Item 18 as the Anchor

The eCDF plot of the change from Baseline to Week 32 in the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total Tscore by category of change in EORTC QLQ-C30 Item 18 from Screening to Week 32
(uncollapsed categories) shows a clear and consistent separation between the “improved 1
category” and “no change/worsening” curves, supporting the use of a 1-category improvement
on EORTC QLQ-C30 Item 18 as the target anchor change category (see ). At a value of -8 points
fewer than 20% of participants who reported no change or worsening on the anchor would be
misclassified as having meaningfully improved.

What subjects consider to be clinically meaningful improvement may be impacted by their
baseline fatigue severity. Therefore, subjects’ baseline fatigue severity should be considered
when determining a range of clinically meaningful improvement values. Table 7 presents the
change from Baseline to Week 32 in the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score for subjects with a
1-category improvement in EORTC QLQ-C30 Item 18, stratified by Screening EORTC QLQC30 Item 18 score. When baseline fatigue severity is taken into account, the distribution of
change from Baseline to Week 32 in PROMIS Fatigue Sf 8a total T-score varies across baseline
severity levels. Subjects who reported “quite a bit” of tiredness at Screening had a median
change of -12.18 points, while subjects who reported “a little” tiredness at Screening had a
median change of -5.33 points, and subjects who reported “very much” tiredness at Screening
had a median change of -5.49 points.

Based on the results of the anchor-based analyses using EORTC QLQ-C30 Item 18 as the
anchor, FDA determined that a plausible range of MSD values falls between a reduction of 4.5 to
12.7 points from Baseline in the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score, where:
•

-12.7 points represents the upper end of the MSD range, based on the 50th percentile of
change in subjects with a 1-category improvement who reported “quite a bit” of tiredness
at Screening.

•

-8.0 points represents the value that maintains the misclassification rate at or below 20%.

•

-4.5 points represents the lower end of the MSD range, based on the 50th percentile of
change in subjects with a 1-category improvement who reported “a little” or “very much”
tiredness at Screening.

Final MSD Range (EORTC QLQ-C30 Item 18): -12.7 to -4.5

3.2.2.6

PGI-C as the Anchor

The eCDF plot of the change from Baseline to Week 32 in the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total Tscore by PGI-C category at Week 32 (uncollapsed categories) shows separation between the “much
better” curve and the “no change/worsening” curves; however, the “a little better” curve overlaps
substantially with the “no change” curve (see Figure 3). Based on these results, FDA used “much
better” on the PGI-C as the target anchor change category. The eCDF plot shows that the median
change in PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score for subjects who reported “much better” on the
PGI-C was -4.60 points (Q1 = -12.70, Q3 = 0.00). At a value of -11 points fewer than 20% of
patients would be misclassified as having meaningfully improved.

21

Reference ID: 5860339

Figure 3. eCDF, Change From Baseline to Week 32 in PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a Total T-Score by
PGI-C Category at Week 32 (Uncollapsed Categories)

Source: Applicant’s Figure 6.b from the Applicant’s response to IR dated March 26, 2026.

Based on the results of the anchor-based analyses using PGI-C as the anchor, FDA determined
that a plausible range of MSD values falls between a reduction of 4.60 to 11.0 points from
Baseline in the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score, where:
•

-11.0 points represents the upper end of the MSD range, based on the value that
maintains the misclassification rate at or below 20%.

•

-4.60 points represents the lower end of the MSD range, based on the 50th percentile of
change in subjects who reported “much better” on the PGI-C.

Final MSD Range (PGI-C): -11.0 to -4.60 points.
It is noted that while the PGI-C assesses overall PV symptom change rather than fatigue
specifically, the eCDF curves for PGI-C showed sufficient separation to support its use as a
supplementary anchor in the triangulation of results. The conceptual misalignment between the
PGI-C and the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a is acknowledged as a limitation of this analysis.

3.2.2.7

Triangulation of Results

The MSD ranges derived using all evaluated anchor measures are summarized in Table 9 below.

Abbreviations: EORTC QLQ-C30 = European Organization for Research and Treatment of Cancer Quality of Life Questionnaire Core 30; MSD
= meaningful score difference; N/A = not applicable; PGI-C = Patient Global Impression of Change; PGI-S = Patient Global Impression of
Severity.

Based on the triangulation of results across all interpretable anchor measures (EORTC QLQ-C30
Item 18, PGI-S, and PGI-C), FDA determined that a plausible final range of MSD values falls
between a reduction of 4.5 to 12.7 points from Baseline in the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total Tscore. This final MSD range was derived primarily from the EORTC QLQ-C30 Item 18 anchor,
which is most closely aligned with the concept of fatigue severity and impact measured by the
PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a and demonstrated the highest correlation with the PROMIS Fatigue SF
8a total T-score among all available anchors.
The MSD ranges derived using the PGI-S (-8.0 to -7.25) and PGI-C (-11.0 to -4.60) both fall within
or overlap substantially with the range derived using EORTC QLQ-C30 Item 18 (-12.7 to -4.5),
providing additional support for the final MSD range. Notably, the lower bounds of the MSD
ranges derived from all three interpretable anchors converge around -4.5 to -4.6 points, further
supporting -4.5 points as the lower bound of the final MSD range.

3.2.2.8

Score Interpretation

To assess how much more likely an average patient receiving rusfertide versus placebo would
experience a meaningful improvement in fatigue, we examined the eCDF plot of within-subject
changes in the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score from Baseline to Week 32 by treatment arm.
The eCDF plot using observed data from the VERIFY study is shown in Figure 4.
As shown in Figure 4, the between-group response rate difference across the lower and upper ends
of the final MSD range (-12.7 to -4.5) is approximately 8% to 14.63%. That is, patients are between
8% and 14.63% more likely to experience a meaningful improvement in fatigue if they receive
rusfertide versus placebo. This difference provides may suggest that the observed treatment effect
reflects a clinically meaningful improvement in fatigue from the patient perspective.

23

Reference ID: 5860339

Figure 4. eCDF, Change From Baseline to Week 32 in PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a Total T-score
(ITT Set)

Source: Applicant’s Figure 9.a from the Applicant’s response to IR dated March 26, 2026.
Abbreviations: eCDF = empirical cumulative distribution function; ITT = intent to treat; PROMIS = Patient-Reported Outcomes Measurement
Information System; SF = Short Form.

As a supplementary analysis, the least squares (LS) mean changes from Baseline to Week 32 in
PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score for each treatment arm were compared to the final MSD
range. The LS mean change was approximately -1.79 points (95% CI: -2.83, -0.75) for the
rusfertide arm and 0.19 points (95% CI: -0.86, 1.24) for the placebo arm (see Figure 5). Both
estimates fall well below the lower bound of the final MSD range (-4.5 points), indicating that, on
average, patients in neither treatment arm are expected to have experienced a clinically meaningful
improvement in fatigue by Week 32. While there is a statistically significant difference between
the treatment and placebo groups, the anchor-based analyses suggests that this difference may not
translate into a clinically meaningful benefit in fatigue from the patient perspective.

24

Reference ID: 5860339

Figure 5. Clinical meaningfulness of the observed effect on PROMIS Fatigue Short Form 8a
Total T-score Score from Baseline to Week 32 (ITT Set)

Source: Reviewer’s figure based on the review team’s MSD range of −21.7 to −4.5 and the LS mean estimates and corresponding
95% confidence intervals for the placebo and rusfertide arms from FDA Analysis (Dataset: ADQSPM).
Abbreviations: ITT = intent to treat; LS = least squares; MSD = meaningful score difference; PROMIS = Patient-Reported Outcomes
Measurement Information System; SF = Short Form.

3.3 Conclusion
The anchor-based analyses, supplemented by eCDF curves suggest patients are between 8% and
14.63% more likely to experience a meaningful improvement in fatigue if they receive the
treatment versus placebo. However, LS mean comparisons suggest on average neither patients
that received treatment nor patients that received placebo were likely to experience a meaningful
improvement in fatigue. Taken together, these results suggest there is limited evidence to support
that the observed treatment effect on the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score reached the level
of improvement that patients would consider clinically meaningful. Notably, the Clinical team
determined results of the PROMIS Fatigue SF 8a total T-score endpoint would be informative to
prescribers and patients and therefore should be included in the label.

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)

14. Clinical Pharmacology
14.1. In Vitro Studies
14.1.1. Plasma Protein Binding
The plasma protein binding of rusfertide in human plasma was evaluated in Study MC16M0032. Rusfertide was highly bound in human plasma at concentrations of 4000, 12000, and
36000 ng/mL, with binding reported as >99% across all concentrations tested.
The plasma protein binding of metabolites M4 and M9 in human plasma was assessed in Studies
PTG-C-151 and PTG-C-106, respectively. Both metabolites were highly bound in human
plasma. M4 showed mean bound fractions of 98.8% and 99.8% at 0.5 and 5 μM, respectively,
and M9 showed bound fractions of >94.3% and >99.5% at 0.5 and 5 μM, respectively.

14.1.2. Red blood cell-to-Plasma Partition Ratio
The red blood cell partitioning of rusfertide was assessed in Study 100032285. The mean red
blood cell to plasma partitioning ratio of rusfertide in human blood was 2.7.

14.1.3. Metabolism studies
14.1.3.1. In Vitro Stability of Rusfertide in Human Biological
Matrices
Study PTG-C-056 demonstrated a rusfertide half-life exceeding 24 hours in human serum, with
76.8% of the compound remaining at the 24-hour time point. Study 100032285 found rusfertide
was relatively stable in whole blood, with approximately 72% remaining at 120 minutes and a
half-life exceeding 120 minutes. Collectively, these data indicate that rusfertide is stable in
human serum, plasma, and whole blood.
In Study PTG-C-057, rusfertide was incubated at 20 μM in human kidney and liver S9 fractions
(without NADPH) at 37°C. In human kidney S9, rusfertide was unstable, with a half-life of only
0.56 hours, and in human liver S9, the half-life was 1.6 hours.
14.1.3.2. Metabolite identification
Studies in human plasma, serum, and whole blood indicate that rusfertide undergoes 2
independent metabolic pathways; one involving proteolysis by unspecified trypsin-like proteases
to form metabolites M1 and M4, and 1 involving hydroxylation to form metabolite M9 (Studies
PTG-300-C-056, PTG-C-057, PTG-C-087, and 100032285). Metabolite identification and
structural confirmation have identified M1 as the initial proteolytic product, which is then further
proteolyzed to form M4. The proposed structure of M9 is consistent with a single hydroxylation
of the palmitoyl moiety. Minor metabolites (for example, M6) from proteolysis at different
locations were also detected in vitro.
2

Reference ID: 5858373

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
In vitro studies (Studies PTG-C-089 and PTG-C-101) indicate that metabolites M1, M4, and M9
have in vitro activity in the T47D ferroportin internalization assay, with the rank order of
potency of M1 > rusfertide > M4 >> M9 (Table 1).

14.1.3.3. Identification of Enzymes Responsible for the
Metabolism of Rusfertide
In Study PTG-C-078, the CYP enzyme involvement in rusfertide metabolism was assessed
using two complementary approaches: (1) incubation with heterologously expressed human
recombinant CYP isoforms (rCYP1A2, rCYP2A6, rCYP2B6, rCYP2C8, rCYP2C9, rCYP2C19,
rCYP2D6, rCYP2E1, rCYP3A4, and rCYP3A5) at 1 μM rusfertide and 50 pmol/mL CYP
protein, and (2) incubation in HLM (0.5 mg/mL protein) with and without specific chemical
inhibitors for each CYP isoform. The results showed that the CYP enzymes evaluated (CYP1A2,
CYP2A6, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP2E1, CYP3A4, and
CYP3A5) do not play a significant role in the metabolism of rusfertide.
14.1.3.1. Identification of Enzymes Responsible for the
Metabolism of M4 and M9
Study PTG-C-156 evaluated whether the rusfertide metabolites M4 and M9 are substrates of
human CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP3A4, and CYP3A5,
using a substrate depletion approach in rCYP enzymes (1 μM test compound, 30 nM rCYP, 1
mM NADPH, 37°C, up to 60 minutes).
After a 60-minute incubation, the percentages remaining of M4 were 2.1%, 1.1%, 3.4%, 11.3%,
1.7%, 1.4%, 1.0%, and 1.9% in the presence of rCYP1A2, rCYP2B6, rCYP2C8, rCYP2C9,
rCYP2C19, rCYP2D6, rCYP3A4, and rCYP3A5, respectively, while the percentages remaining
of M9 were 16.7%, 40.7%, 63.7%, 82.3%, 13.5%, 37.5%, 50.9%, and 60.9% in the same
isoforms. However, comparable depletion was also observed in the negative vector control for
both M4 (1.3% remaining) and M9 (48.8% remaining), and the reaction phenotyping results in
rCYPs were deemed inconclusive.
As a follow-up, stability of M4 and M9 was confirmed in Kpi buffer alone (90.5% and 106.6%
remaining, respectively), but instability was observed in both rCYP vector and HLM in the
presence and absence of NADPH, therefore reaction phenotyping assessment would not be
conclusive.

3

Reference ID: 5858373

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)

14.1.4. Identification of Transporters Involved in
Rusfertide Disposition
The substrate potential of rusfertide for eleven human drug transporters (OAT1, OAT3, OCT1,
OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, BCRP, P-gp, and BSEP) was evaluated in
Study PTG-C-070. Rusfertide was tested at 2, 20, and 200 μM, as well as at 20 μM in the
presence of the respective reference inhibitor for each transporter.
Across all eight SLC transporters and all concentrations tested, less than 2-fold difference in
uptake was observed between transporter-expressing cells and control cells. These results
indicate that PTG-300 is not a substrate of OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3,
MATE1, or MATE2-K under the study conditions.
In addition, no 2-fold or higher uptake was seen in vesicles supplied with adenosine triphosphate
(ATP) compared to vesicles supplied with adenosine monophosphate (AMP) at any
concentration of rusfertide tested for BCRP, P-gp, or BSEP, indicating that rusfertide is not a
substrate of these transporters under assay conditions.

14.1.5. Identification of Transporters Involved in M4
and M9 Disposition
The substrate potential of the M4 and M9 metabolites for eleven human drug transporters
(OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, BCRP, P-gp, and
BSEP) was evaluated in Study PTG-C-152.
Neither M4 nor M9 were found to be substrates of P-gp, BCRP, BSEP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3,
OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, MATE1, or MATE2-K at ≤30 μM.

14.1.6. In Vitro Evaluation of the Inhibition Potential
of Rusfertide against Metabolizing Enzymes and
Transporters
14.1.6.1. Metabolizing enzymes
The inhibition potential of rusfertide against human CYP450 enzymes was initially evaluated in
two Studies 100033020 and 100033650_US034-0000154. Rusfertide at 10 µM caused <50%
inhibition of CYP1A2, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A, while exhibiting 50.1% and
63.9% inhibition of CYP2B6 and CYP2C8, respectively. At lower concentrations (1 and 3 µM),
rusfertide demonstrated <30% inhibition of CYP2B6 and CYP2C8.
The direct and time-dependent inhibition (TDI) potential of rusfertide was further evaluated in
the Study PTG-C-072, across a concentration range of 0.05 to 50 µM in pooled human liver
microsomes. Rusfertide (up to 50 μM) did not directly inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2C8, CYP2C9,
CYP2C19, and CYP2D6. Rusfertide inhibited CYP2B6, CYP3A (midazolam), and CYP3A4
(testosterone) with IC50 values of 14.2, 36.3, and 48.9 μM, respectively.
No TDI potential was observed for CYP1A2, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, or CYP3A
(testosterone as the substrate). For CYP2B6, CYP2C8, and CYP3A (midazolam as the substrate),
4

Reference ID: 5858373

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
time-dependent IC50 shifts were observed following preincubation in the absence of NADPH;
however, no difference in inhibition was observed between preincubation with and without
NADPH, indicating that the observed shifts are non-NADPH-dependent and not consistent with
classic mechanism-based TDI. For an assessment of the clinical drug interaction potential based
on these findings, refer to Section 14.1.6.3.
14.1.6.2. Transporters
The inhibition potential of rusfertide against human drug transporters was evaluated in Study
PTG-C-071, which comprised two sub-studies: an initial screening study (OPT-2020-034) and a
follow-up IC50 determination study (OPT-2020-143). Rusfertide was assessed as a potential
inhibitor of OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K, BCRP,
P-gp, and BSEP.
In the initial screening study (OPT-2020-034), rusfertide was tested at 0.2 μM for renal
transporters OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, MATE1, and MATE2-K, at 1 μM for hepatic transporters
OCT1, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, and BSEP, and at 200 μM for efflux transporters BCRP and P-gp.
No inhibition was observed for OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, MATE1 or MATE2-K at 0.2 μM, and no
inhibition was seen for OCT1 or BSEP at 1 μM. Inhibition was observed for OATP1B1 (41.5%)
and OATP1B3 (27.1%) at 1 μM, and for BCRP (94.0%) and P-gp (93.0%) at 200 μM, prompting
IC50 determination for these three transporters.
In the follow-up study (OPT-2020-143), IC50 values were determined to be 5.09 μM for
OATP1B1, 8.18 μM for BCRP, and 5.94 μM for P-gp, respectively. For an assessment of the
clinical drug interaction potential based on these findings, refer to Section 14.1.6.3.
14.1.6.3. Evaluation of Clinical DDI Risk for Rusfertide
The potential for clinical drug-drug interactions due to CYP and transporter inhibition was
assessed based on ICH M12 Drug Interaction Studies guidance as presented in Table 2. At
clinically relevant concentrations (Cmax,u=1.17 ng/mL (0.480 nM) or Cmax,u=13.02 ng/mL
(5.34 nM) based on the mean steady-state Cmax value following SC administration of a 108 mg
once-a-week dose, derived from clinical trial PTG-300-16 assuming dose proportionality from
54 to 108 mg, and the unbound plasma fraction from in vitro studies (fu,p = 0.0009, or
alternatively assuming fu,p = 0.01), rusfertide is not expected to cause clinically significant
inhibition of all the listed enzymes and transporters.

Rusfertide (PTG-300)

14.1.7. In Vitro Evaluation of the Induction Potential
of Rusfertide against Metabolizing Enzymes
The induction potential of rusfertide against human CYP450 enzymes was evaluated in Study
PTG-C-073. The results showed that rusfertide did not induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4
enzyme activity over the tested concentration range (0.01 to 1 µM). The maximum fold
induction in enzyme activity was ≤1.12-fold of vehicle control, and the percentage of positive
control response was ≤20%. Similarly, rusfertide did not induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4
mRNA expression, with a maximum fold induction of ≤1.16-fold of vehicle control and a
percentage of positive control response of ≤20%. These results did not meet the study's
predefined induction threshold of ≥2-fold increase and ≥20% of the positive control response.

14.1.8. In Vitro Evaluation of the Inhibition Potential
of M4 and M9 against Metabolizing Enzymes and
Transporters
14.1.8.1. Metabolizing enzymes
The inhibition potential of M4 and M9 against major human CYP450 enzymes was initially
evaluated in Study PTG-C-153. M4 and M9 were incubated across a concentration range of
0.03–30 µM with probe substrates for CYP1A2 (phenacetin), CYP2B6 (bupropion), CYP2C8
(paclitaxel), CYP2C9 (diclofenac), CYP2C19 (S-mephenytoin), CYP2D6 (bufuralol), and
CYP3A4 (midazolam and testosterone) in pooled human liver microsomes. M4 exhibited low
inhibition (IC50 >30 µM) toward CYP1A2, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4 (testosterone), while IC50
values for CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP3A4 (midazolam) were 7.60, 9.31,
12.6, 28.7, and 18.9 µM, respectively. M9 demonstrated low inhibition (IC50 >30 µM) against
all CYP isoforms tested.
The time-dependent inhibition (TDI) potential of M4 and M9 was further evaluated in Study
PTG-C-154, across a concentration range of 0.03–30 µM in pooled human liver microsomes,
using pre-incubation in the absence and presence of NADPH for 30 minutes at 37°C. The IC50
ratios (−NADPH/+NADPH) for M4 were greater than 1.5 for CYP3A4 with midazolam (IC50:
5.10 µM without NADPH, 2.03 µM with NADPH; ratio 2.5) and CYP3A4 with testosterone
(IC50: 11.5 µM without NADPH, 4.64 µM with NADPH; ratio 2.5), indicating a TDI signal for
CYP3A4. IC50 ratios for CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, and CYP2C19 were less than 1.5,
suggesting no meaningful TDI for these isoforms. IC50 ratios for CYP2C9 and CYP2D6 could
not be determined due to insufficient inhibition. M9 showed IC50 values greater than 30 µM for
all CYPs tested in both the absence and presence of NADPH, and IC50 ratios were therefore not
determined.
Given the TDI signal observed for CYP3A4, the kinetic inactivation constants KI and kinact for
M4 were further investigated in Study PTG-C-160. M4 (0.04 to 30 µM) or reference inhibitor
verapamil (0.07 to 50 μM ) was pre-incubated with pooled human liver microsomes and NADPH
for 0.5, 5, 10, 20, 30, and 60 minutes at 37°C, using midazolam and testosterone as CYP3A
probe substrates. Ki and Kinact values could not accurately be determined due to the lack of
inhibition of CYP3A observed under the experimental conditions. However, inactivation was
7

Reference ID: 5858373

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
observed between 0.5 and 30 minutes at 30 μM, the highest concentration tested, indicating a
potential time dependency.
14.1.8.2. Transporters
The inhibition potential of M4 and M9 was evaluated against eleven transporters in Study PTGC-152. M4 was identified as an inhibitor of OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, and BSEP
with IC₅₀ values of 0.193, 0.391, 2.13, 28.3, and 8.44 μM, respectively, while showing no
meaningful inhibition of P-gp, BCRP, OCT1, OCT2, MATE1, or MATE2-K (IC₅₀ > 30 μM). M9
was identified as an inhibitor of OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 with IC₅₀ values of 2.50 and 3.86 μM,
respectively, and did not inhibit any of the remaining transporters evaluated (IC₅₀ > 30 μM).
14.1.8.1. Evaluation of Clinical DDI Risk for M4 and M9
The potential for clinical drug-drug interactions due to CYP and transporter inhibition was
assessed based on ICH M12 Drug Interaction Studies guidance as presented in Table 3. At
clinically relevant concentrations (M4 Cmax,u=1.56 nM and M9 Cmax,u=12.2 nM based on the
mean steady-state Cmax value following SC administration of a 108 mg once-a-week dose,
derived from clinical trial PTG-300-16 assuming dose proportionality from 54 to 108 mg, and
the unbound plasma fraction from in vitro studies), M4 and M9 are not expected to cause
clinically significant inhibition of all the listed enzymes and transporters.

14.1.9. In Vitro Evaluation of the Induction Potential
of M4 and M9 against Metabolizing Enzymes
The induction potential of M4 and M9 against human CYP450 enzymes was evaluated in Study
PTG-C-155. M4 and M9 (at concentrations ranging from 0.01 to 1 µM) did not induce
CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4 mRNA expression.

14.1.10. Summary of Clinical Drug-Drug Interaction
Potential for Rusfertide and its M4 and M9
Metabolites
•
•

Rusfertide is not a substrate for the major CYP isoforms. M4 and M9 CYP phenotyping
could not be assessed due to their instability in the incubation media.
Rusfertide, M4, and M9 do not inhibit the major CYP isoforms either directly or via a
time-dependent mechanism at clinically relevant concentrations, with the exception of
9

Reference ID: 5858373

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)

•
•

M4, which showed potential time-dependent inhibition of CYP3A4. However, the Kinact
and Ki values for M4 could not be accurately determined due to lack of inhibition of
CYP3A in human liver microsomes. The IC50 value for M4 following preincubation in
the presence of NADPH was high (≥2.03 µM) and considering the low unbound Cmax of
M4 (approximately 0.00156 µM at the maximum rusfertide dose of 108 mg), the
potential for a clinically relevant DDI is considered low.
Rusfertide, M4 and M9 do not induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4.
Rusfertide, M4 and M9 are neither a substrate nor an inhibitor of the human drug
transporters OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, MATE1, MATE2-K,
BCRP, P-gp, and BSEP at therapeutic concentrations.

14.2.2. Adjusted Dose Strengths for Rusfertide
Lyophilized Powder Formulation
The intended commercial lyophilized powder formulation was utilized in clinical trials
12

Reference ID: 5858373

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
PTG-300-11 and PTG-300-21, and the clinical pharmacology studies PTG-300-15, PTG-300-16,

Upon review of the rusfertide manufacturing process in accordance with US FDA Manual of
Policies and Procedures 5019.1 Rev. 1, it was identified that expansion volume was not
accounted for during development of the LPF, resulting in dose strengths that deviated from the
originally planned values. Following alignment with the US FDA at the Type B pre-NDA CMC
meeting on 24 November 2025, dose strengths for labeling were adjusted to reflect the targeted
strengths used in the clinical batches as shown in Table 6:

14.2.3. Trial PTG-300-01
The results of this Phase 1, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, single-ascending-dose
trial in 56 healthy male participants (Cohorts 1–6) and 6 participants in a repeat-dose cohort
(Cohort 7) demonstrated:
PK: Following single SC administration of rusfertide (saline formulation) over a dose range of 1
to 80 mg, rusfertide concentrations were detected within 1 hour, with plasma concentrations
increasing and then remaining sustained for 24 to 48 hours before declining. As shown in Table
7, the median Tmax was 24 hours for doses of 3 to 20 mg and decreased to 5 hours and 2 hours
for the 40 mg and 80 mg doses, respectively. Mean t½ and CL/F generally increased with dose.

PK: Pharmacokinetic parameters for the subjects who completed all four treatments are
summarized in Table 10. The absolute bioavailability of rusfertide was 30.7% and 28.2%
following SC administration at 40 mg/mL and 200 mg/mL, respectively, and 56.6% following
IM administration.

14.2.5. Trial PTG-300-15
The results of this Phase 1, open-label, randomized, crossover trial in healthy participants
evaluating the PK, PD, and safety of rusfertide lyophilized powder formulation (LPF) across a
dose range of 10 to 60 mg and the prefilled syringe formulation (PFS; liquid formulation in
prefilled syringe) at 20 mg following SC administration demonstrated:
PK: Following subcutaneous administration, rusfertide concentrations were detected within 1
hour of dose administration with plasma concentrations increasing and sustained with median
time to peak concentration (Tmax) of 24 hours for doses up to 30 mg, and 2- to 4 hours for the
45 and 60 mg doses. There was dose-related increase in Cmax and AUC. Apparent plasma
clearance of rusfertide for the lyophilized product ranged from approximately 0.63 L/h to 1.5 L/h
with clearance increasing with rusfertide dose (Table 11). The lyophilized formulation of 20 mg
rusfertide had approximately 1.6- fold higher exposure compared to the 20 mg pre-filled syringe
(PFS) formulation based on the Cmax and AUC.

14.2.6. Trial PTG-300-16
The results of this Phase 1, single-center, open-label, randomized trial in healthy participants
evaluating the multiple-dose PK of rusfertide 60 mg LPF administered SC once weekly (QW) for
5 weeks across three injection sites (abdomen, upper thigh, upper outer arm) demonstrated:
PK: The median rusfertide plasma concentration-time profiles are presented by injection site in
Figure 3. The profiles were generally similar following injections in the abdomen and upper arm
for both Dose 1 and Dose 5. Following Dose 1, a modestly lower (approximately 30%)
maximum rusfertide concentration and a generally lower concentration-time profile were noted
for injection in the upper thigh compared with the other injection sites. However, this difference
was attenuated by Dose 5 (Table 12). Although single-dose exposure differences across injection
sites were noted, rusfertide exposures are subject to variability from multiple sources. In the
context of individualized dose titration to maintain HCT <45%, these differences are not
considered clinically meaningful.

As shown in Table 13, the overall mean accumulation ratios (Dose 5/Dose 1) were 1.62 for

achieved by the third dose based on trough concentration data.

20

Reference ID: 5858373

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
respectively. At steady state (Dose 5), mean metabolite AUC168-to-total AUC168 ratios for M4
and M9 were 15% and 31%, respectively, confirming both as major metabolites. Taking relative
potency (M4 is considered to have 65% of the potency of rusfertide, and M9 is considered to
have 14% of the potency of rusfertide) and molecular weight into account, rusfertide is the major
contributor to pharmacological activity (~78%), with minor contributions from M4 (~16%) and
M9 (~6%).
PD: Acute decreases in serum iron and TSAT were observed following each rusfertide dose,
with subsequent recovery prior to the next dose. Decreases in hematocrit (HCT) were noted
beginning at Day 8 (168 hours after a single dose) and continued with repeated dosing. PD
effects were generally similar across the three injection sites .

14.2.7. Trial PTG-300-17
The results of this Phase 1, randomized, double-blind, placebo- and positive-controlled, 6sequence, 3-period crossover trial in healthy adult participants evaluating the effect of rusfertide
on the QTc interval demonstrated:
Following single SC administration of rusfertide 90 mg, mean plasma concentrations reached a
mean Cmax of 1160 ng/mL at a median Tmax of 4 hours. The mean Cmax of M4 was 341
ng/mL (median Tmax 48 hours) and the mean Cmax of M9 was 372 ng/mL (median Tmax 48
hours).
Per cardiac safety IRT review (DARRTS April 09, 2026, Reference ID: 5777795), at doses up to
90 mg, rusfertide did not prolong the QTc interval to any clinically relevant extent. The upper
bound of the two-sided 90% confidence interval for the placebo-corrected, baseline adjusted
ΔΔQTcF was below 10 msec at all timepoints for both dose levels in the primary by-time
analysis. Rusfertide did not have clinically significant effects on other ECG parameters (heart
rate, PR interval, or QRS duration).

14.2.8. Trial PTG-300-18
The results of this Phase 1, multicenter, open-label, single-dose, parallel-group trial evaluating
the PK, PD, and safety of a single SC dose of rusfertide 20 mg LPF in 8 healthy participants, 8
participants with moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B) and 8 participants with severe
renal impairment (eGFR <30 mL/min/1.73 m²) are summarized below. Notably the study used
BSA-normalized eGFR (mL/min/1.73 m²) for renal impairment categorization rather than
absolute eGFR (mL/min) as recommended by FDA guidance; however, conversion to absolute
eGFR confirmed that all subjects remained within their designated renal impairment categories,
with severe renal impairment subjects having absolute eGFR values of 8.45–29.77 mL/min and
healthy subjects having absolute eGFR values of 105.54–127.26 mL/min.
PK: Rusfertide PK parameters are summarized by trial group in Table 14, and geometric least
squares mean (GLSM) ratios of Cmax and AUCinf are presented in Table 15. Mean rusfertide
AUCinf was 12% higher and Cmax was 33% higher in subjects with severe renal impairment
compared with healthy subjects. In the subjects with moderate hepatic impairment, AUCinf was
32% lower and Cmax was 22% lower compared with healthy subjects.

Metabolites PK: Both AUCinf and Cmax for metabolite M4 were lower in the impaired organ
function groups than in the healthy group. In the severe renal impairment group, AUCinf was
11% lower and Cmax was 24% lower, and in the moderate hepatic impairment group, AUCinf
was 13% lower and Cmax was 44% lower, than in the healthy group. No significant differences
were seen in exposure across the 3 trial groups. The mean ratio of M4 AUCinf to total AUCinf
(calculated as AUCinf values for rusfertide + M4 + M9) was similar in the 3 groups: 15.1% for
healthy participants, 11.1% for severe renal impairment participants, and 14.2% for moderate
hepatic impairment participants.
Metabolite M9 exposures were higher in participants with severe renal impairment than in
healthy participants (AUCinf 93% higher, Cmax 81% higher). M9 exposure values were
modestly higher (<30%) in participants with moderate hepatic impairment than in healthy
participants (AUCinf 11% higher, Cmax 24% higher). The mean ratio of M9 AUCinf to total
AUCinf (calculated as AUCinf values for rusfertide + M4 + M9) was 29.9% for healthy
participants, 42.3% for severe renal impairment participants, and 43.4% for moderate hepatic
impairment participants.
These findings are not thought to be clinically meaningful because of the lower potency of M9
relative to rusfertide and the individualized dose titration of rusfertide to maintain HCT <45%.
Considering these relative potencies, the overall exposure to pharmacologically relevant

Rusfertide (PTG-300)

14.3. Bioanalytical Method Validation and
Performance
The Applicant submitted 5 method validation reports and 14 bioanalytical performance reports
which support all PK data of rusfertide and its metabolites M4 and M9 (Table 16). Overall, assay
method validations are appropriate for quantifying rusfertide in human plasma and the
bioanalytical reports are acceptable to support the conclusions drawn from corresponding clinical
studies. The review team deems that the PK data are reliable.
The incurred sample reanalysis (ISR) for metabolite M4 did not meet the pre-specified
acceptance criterion in Trial PTG-300-15 (41.7%) and Trial PTG-300-22 (66.5%). However, the
clinical significance of these ISR failures is considered minimal. Rusfertide is a synthetic cyclic
peptide, and its metabolite M4 is a proteolytic degradation fragment that is expected to be further
catabolized into amino acids through normal physiological pathways. As such, metabolite data
are not required to support labeling decisions. The M4 and M9 data collected across the
development program were generated as scientific due diligence to characterize the overall PK
profile of rusfertide. Therefore, the ISR failures observed for M4 are not considered to impact
the reliability of the overall bioanalytical dataset.
The PD effects of rusfertide were assessed using standard clinical laboratory blood tests (for
example, measurements of serum iron and TSAT).

14.4.2. Immunogenicity Assays
The immunogenicity assays submitted by the applicant have been assessed by OPQAIII. Refer to
the OPQR short report in DARRTS dated August 12, 2026 (Reference ID: 5851454) for a
detailed assessment of the assays. A brief summary is provided below.
The applicant developed a multi-tiered strategy for ADA detection and characterization. The
Applicant’s initial assay does not have adequate sensitivity, with a reported sensitivity of 353.63
ng/mL and poor drug tolerance, which may be insufficient to detect potentially clinically
meaningful ADA response in the studies PTG-300-01, PTG-300-04, PTG-300-07, PTG-300-08,
PTG-300-10, PTG-300-14, PTG-300-15, PTG-300-16, PTG-300-20, and PTG-300-22. There is
concern that the incidence of ADA positive sample reporting were potentially underreported
when using this assay.
The Applicant improved the sensitivity of the ADA assay in the pivotal phase 3 study PTG-30011 by decreasing the minimum required dilution (MRD) which lowered the method’s limit of
quantification, possibly by eliminating background noise. Consequently, the optimized method
enabled more accurate detection of lower ADA levels in the samples. Even with improved
sensitivity the Applicant’s assay still did not reach the recommended sensitivity of 100 ng/mL.
Even though the assay does not meet the recommended sensitivity, there was no evidence for an
impact on PK or PD and the ADA titers detected were generally low <300. Based on the changes
in the method the assay is fit for purpose.
The assay to detect anti-hepicidin antibodies was determined to have adequate sensitivity and
reproducibility.
The NAb assay is representative of the proposed mechanism of action and uses an appropriate
(b) (4)
cell line. The assay has a sensitivity of
ug/mL of NAb which is not sensitive enough to
detect potentially clinically meaningful levels of NAb. Due to the lack of a clinical impact of
ADA observed to date, the need to develop a more sensitivity NAb assay is not warranted as it is
unlikely to provide any additional clinically meaningful information. However, the incidence of
NAb may be underestimated.

14.4.3. Effect of Immunogenicity on PK of Rusfertide
The potential impact of ADA status on rusfertide PK was assessed through population PK
analysis. Covariate analysis incorporating ADA status as a time-varying covariate on clearance
(CL) did not reveal a clear correlation between ADA status and rusfertide PK. Refer to Section
14.5.2.

Rusfertide (PTG-300)

14.5. Pharmacometrics Assessment
14.5.1. Review Summary
The proposed dosing regimen is consistent with the dosing studied in the pivotal Phase 3 study
(PTG-300-11). In Study PTG-300-11, the starting dose was 20 mg rusfertide or placebo
administered SC once a week for all subjects. The dose was adjusted to maintain HCT <45%.
For subjects with CTCAE Grade 2 and higher anemia, the dose may have been decreased. For
subjects with study drug-related Grade 3 or higher toxicity the dose was required to be decreased
as follows:
•
•
•

If a subject was receiving 10 mg, the subject was to be discontinued
If a subject was receiving 20 mg or 30 mg, the dose level was to be decreased by 10 mg.
If a subject was receiving doses of 45 mg or higher, the dose level was to be decreased by
15 mg.

The proposed dosing regimen and schedules are primarily supported by the safety and efficacy
finding from Study PTG-300-11, with population PK (PopPK) analysis and exposure-response
analysis serving as supportive evidence. The PopPK analysis reasonably describes the observed
PK data for rusfertide LPF in PV patients from Study PTG-300-11 and is acceptable for deriving
PK exposure parameters to support the labeling language. E-R analyses for efficacy and safety
generally support dose adjustment strategies. However, because doses were guided by individual
response, the current E-R analyses are likely confounded by dose adjustment and may not fully
capture the underlying E-R relationships. Longitudinal PK-PD analyses for efficacy variables
such as hematocrit and safety variables such as platelets and leukocytes would provide a more
informative characterization of the E-R relationships for these parameters.
The PopPK and E-R analyses were conducted using the originally planned dose strengths for
LPF. For dosage strengths discrepancy between the proposed and the studied dose, refer to
Section 14.2.2.

14.5.2. Population PK Analysis
The Applicant's population PK (PopPK) model adequately describes the observed PK data across
the analysis population, including patients with polycythemia vera (PV). Model parameters were
estimated with acceptable precision. Goodness-of-fit (GOF) plots stratified by study and dose
level did not reveal obvious model misspecification, and visual predictive check (VPC) plots
demonstrate that the model appropriately captures both the central tendency and variability of the
observed data. Therefore, the PopPK model is acceptable to derive PK exposures to support
labeling and exposure-response (E-R) analyses.
Summary of the Applicant’s population PK analysis
The studies contributing to the PopPK analysis includes Phase 1 studies (PTG-300-01, PTG-30007, PTG-300-15, PTG-300-16, PTG-300-18, PTG-300-22), Phase 2 studies (PTG-300-04, PTG300-08), and Phase 3 studies (PTG-300-11, PTG-300-21). There were 627 samples (7.39%) posttreatment below the lower assay limit of quantification (BLQ), which were excluded from the
analysis. The final PopPK analysis dataset included 7778 PK samples from 456 subjects.
The analysis population consisted of 73.5% White, 8.1% Black, and 12.1% Asian, with a
predominance of males (70.6%) and PV patients (60.1%). Median (range) values were 81.4
(44.5, 151) kg for baseline body weight, 52 (20, 86) years for age, 44 (33, 56) g/L for baseline
albumin, 20 (7, 95) U/L for baseline alanine aminotransferase, 21 (8, 95) U/L for baseline
aspartate aminotransferase, 75.0 (31.0, 205) U/L for baseline alkaline phosphatase, 0.57 (0.15,
3.2) mg/dL for baseline bilirubin, 109 (6.42 167) mL/min for baseline body surface areacorrected eGFR. A total of 45 (9.9%) subjects had mild HI and 7 (1.5%) subjects had moderate
HI based on NCI-ODWG. A total of 64 (14%) subjects had mild RI and 10 (2.2%) subjects had
moderate RI based on CKD-EPI.
The final analysis datasets include PK data for PFS formulation (46.6% of PopPK analysis
population) and for lyophilized products (53.4% of PopPK analysis population) and the PK data
from IV route of administration (2.5%), SC (95.4%), and IM (2.1%). Note some subjects in
crossover trials received different formulations, and via different routes of administration.
The base structural model for rusfertide was a 2-compartment model with first-order absorption
and a combination of linear and nonlinear (Michaelis-Menten) CL by fitting log-transformed
rusfertide concentrations. The total CL (CLtotal) is the sum of the linear and nonlinear
components: CL (total) = CL + Vmax* 𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶 /(Km + 𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶), where 𝐶𝐶𝐶𝐶 is the linear component

All continuous covariates were incorporated using a scaled structure based on the median value
of the covariate in the population. All categorical covariates were incorporated using a
proportional structure with the most common level of the covariate being the reference. After
stepwise covariate modeling, the final model included disease status on CL and formulation
effect on F1 for SC route of administration.

The Applicant evaluated impact of ADA status on the PK of rusfertide using the final PopPK
model after including ADA data from Study PTG-300-11. Covariate analysis was conducted by
evaluating the effect of ADA status at the record level (i.e., as a time-varying covariate) on
clearance (CL) in addition to the previously identified covariates in the base model. The last
observation carried forward method was used to populate the ADA status between assessments.
ADA status was examined with three categories: negative, positive, and missing. For the subject
level ADA comparisons, a subject was defined as ADA positive if they had at least 1 ADA
33

Reference ID: 5858373

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
positive sample and defined as ADA negative if all ADA samples were negative. A subject was
defined as missing if all ADA samples were missing.
Summary of subject level of ADA status by study is presented in the Table 21 below. The
association between ADA status and disease status is likely related to the single-dose designs of
the healthy/non-PV subject studies. Among the 139 PV patients in Study PTG-300-11, a total of
39.6% were ADA-positive and 60.4% ADA-negative. In the record levels of the analysis dataset,
4.3% and 87.8% of the records were ADA-positive and ADA-negative. Among the 1358 records
from Study 300-11, 21.1% records were positive ADA and 78.9% records were negative ADA.
Table 21. Summary of ADA statistics at Subject Level in PopPK Dataset

Source: Applicant’s addendum to PopPK and E-R Report, Table 2.

Model parameter estimates remained generally consistent between the final PopPK model
without ADA as a covariate and the analysis with ADA as a covariate. Incorporating ADA as a
covariate resulted in a decrease in rusfertide CL (-11.6%) during ADA-positive periods
compared to ADA-negative periods, while periods with missing ADA status showed a +4.16%
increase in CL. When the post-hoc PK parameters and individual predicted rusfertide exposures
were estimated based on the final PopPK model, the mean and median rusfertide CL and half-life
values were similar across the 3 ADA categories (positive, negative and missing). When
individual predicted AUCss were estimated for PV patients in Study 300-11 receiving LPF
rusfertide 20 mg QW regimen, the predicted AUC were comparable between PV patients with
positive ADA status and those with negative ADA status. PopPK analysis evaluating anti-drug
antibody (ADA) status as a covariate on rusfertide CL did not reveal a clear correlation between
ADA status and rusfertide PK.

14.5.3. Exposure-Response
Exposure-efficacy analyses
Exposure-efficacy relationship was evaluated using data (N=139) from Part 1A of the Phase 3
Study PTG-300-11.
For E-R analysis evaluating clinical response endpoint defined as the absence of phlebotomy
eligibility starting at Week 20 through Week 32 of Part 1A of Study PTG-300-11, the PK
exposures (AUC, Cmax, Cavg) between dosing interval Week 31 and Week 32 were used; if
subjects discontinued before Week 32, the exposures during the last dosing interval were used.
The cumulative Cavg was calculated from the start of rusfertide treatment (time 0) to Week 32
for subjects reaching Week 32 of the study, or until the subject’s last dose for subjects not
reaching Week 32. No apparent E-R relationships were identified (Figure 7).
For E-R analysis evaluating phlebotomy endpoint, the PK exposures (AUC, Cmax, Cavg) from
the last dosing interval prior to the event were simulated for the dosing interval analyses; the
exposures during the last dosing interval were simulated for subjects without an event. The
cumulative Cavg was calculated from the start of rusfertide treatment (time 0) to the time of the
first phlebotomy for subjects experiencing an event, or until the last dose for subjects without an
35

Reference ID: 5858373

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
event. Generally, a positive association between increasing PK exposures and the decreasing
probability of phlebotomy was observed (Figure 8). With Cavg, the estimated odds ratio of
0.99132 (95% CI: 0.98501, 0.99683), which indicate that an increase of 10 mg (from 20 mg to
30 mg) and 15 mg (from 30 mg to 45 mg) in the QW rusfertide LPF dose administered SC are
expected to decrease the probability of phlebotomy by 34.8% and 44.7%, respectively.
Because doses were adjusted based on an individual patient’s response to maintain hematocrit
(HCT) below 45%, the presented E-R relationships are likely confounded by dose adjustment.
While the dose adjustment pattern observed in Study PTG-300-11 and the E-R analyses for
phlebotomy suggest a positive exposure-response relationship, a more robust approach would
involve characterizing time-course of PK-PD relationships (i.e., HCT change), which can
appropriately account for temporal changes in PK and the effects of dose adjustments.

Figure 7 E-R for Probability of Clinical Response

Source: Adapted from Applicant’s PopPK and E-R Report, Figure 63. Note: The graphical E-R relationship was similar with
other exposure metrics (i.e., AUC, Cmax during dosing interval).

Reference ID: 5858373

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)

Figure 8 E-R for Probability of Phlebotomy

Source: Adapted from Applicant’s PopPK and E-R Report, Figure 64. Note: The graphical E-R relationship was similar with
other exposure metrics (i.e., AUC, Cmax during dosing interval) but with p>0.05.

Exposure-safety analyses
Exposure-safety relationship was evaluated using data (N=139) from Part 1A of the Phase 3
Study PTG-300-11. The selected safety endpoints evaluated included injection site reactions
(ISR), anemia, and any Grade ≥3 treatment-emergent AEs. Logistic regression analyses for the
binary safety endpoints used simulated exposures from the last dosing interval before the adverse
event. For subjects without an event, exposures from the last dosing interval before end of
treatment during Part 1A were used. Cumulative Cavg was derived from time 0 to the last dose
before the adverse event for subjects with an event. If a subject did not have an event, their
cumulative Cavg was calculated from time 0 to their last dose during Part 1A. For the ISR safety
endpoint, some subjects experienced an event on the first day of dosing. For these subjects, the
AUC during a dosing interval metric was calculated only over Day 1 (i.e., up to 24-hours postdose) rather than over the full weekly dosing interval (i.e., up to 168 hours post-dose).
No clear exposure-associated trends were observed for infusion site reactions (ISR), anemia, or
any Grade ≥3 treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs). However, because dose adjustments
were guided by not only HCT level but also the occurrence of CTCAE Grade ≥2 anemia and
study drug-related Grade ≥3 toxicities, these analyses are unlikely to fully capture the underlying
exposure-response relationships. For anemia in particular, which may be exacerbated as a drug
effect, characterization of the PK-PD time course would be informative for understanding the
nature and magnitude of the relationship between the rusfertide PK exposure and anemia.
In clinical evaluation (Refer to Integrated Assessment Section 7 Safety), changes in platelet and
leukocyte counts were observed during rusfertide treatment in PV patients (Study PTG-300-11).
The Applicant’s analyses did not include E-R analysis for these parameters. The
pharmacometrics reviewer examined snapshot E-R relationships between the change from
baseline in platelet and leukocyte counts and the observed rusfertide trough concentrations at
corresponding visits. While the scatter plots suggested that increase in platelet counts was more
pronounced in rusfertide treatment group compared to placebo, no clear concentration-response
trend was discernible across the exposure range observed under the trial dosing regimens. Given
the exploratory and descriptive nature of these analyses, definitive conclusions should not be
drawn. A more rigorous time-course PK/PD analysis of platelet and leukocyte counts would be
37

Reference ID: 5858373

NDA 220605
Rusfertide (PTG-300)
needed to better characterize the temporal and underlying, E-R relationships for these
parameters.
